Law of the iterated logarithm for k / 2 k/2 –permanental processes and the local times of related Markov processes
Michael B. Marcus Jay Rosen

Abstract
Let Y Y be a symmetric Borel right process with locally compact state space T ⊆ R 1 T\subseteq R^{1} and potential densities u ⁡ ( x , y ) u(x,y) with respect to some σ \sigma -finite measure on T T . Let g g and f f be finite excessive functions for Y Y . Set u g , f ​ ( x , y ) = u ⁡ ( x , y ) + g ⁡ ( x ) ​ f ​ ( y ) , x , y ∈ T . u_{g,f}(x,y)=u(x,y)+g(x)f(y),\qquad x,y\in T. In this paper we take Y Y to be a symmetric Lévy process, or a diffusion, that is killed at the end of an independent exponential time or the first time it hits 0. Under general smoothness conditions on g g , f f , u u and points d ∈ T d\in T , laws of the iterated logarithm are found for X k / 2 = { X k / 2 ​ ( t ) , t ∈ T } X_{k/2}=\{X_{k/2}(t),t\in T\} , a k / 2 − k/2- permanental process with kernel { u g , f ​ ( x , y ) , x , y ∈ T } \{u_{g,f}(x,y),x,y\in T\} , of the following form: For all integers k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , lim sup x → 0 | X k / 2 ​ ( d + x ) − X k / 2 ​ ( d ) | ( 2 ​ σ 2 ​ ( x ) ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ 1 / x ) 1 / 2 = ( 2 ​ X k / 2 ​ ( d ) ) 1 / 2 , a . s . , \limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|X_{k/2}(d+x)-X_{k/2}(d)|}{\left(2\sigma^{2}\left(x\right)\log\log 1/x\right)^{1/2}}=\left(2X_{k/2}(d)\right)^{1/2},\qquad a.s., where, σ 2 ​ ( x ) = u ⁡ ( d + x , d + x ) + u ⁡ ( x , x ) − 2 ​ u ​ ( d + x , x ) . \sigma^{2}(x)=u(d+x,d+x)+u(x,x)-2u(d+x,x). Using these limit theorems and the Eisenbaum Kaspi Isomorphism Theorem, laws of the iterated logarithm are found for the local times of certain Markov processes with potential densities that have the form of { u g , f ​ ( x , y ) , x , y ∈ T } \{u_{g,f}(x,y),x,y\in T\} or are slight modifications of it.

1
Introduction

Let
\{K(s,t),s,t\in T\}
be a kernel with the property that for all
{\mathbf{t}_{n}}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
in
T^{n}
, the matrix
\mathbf{{\cal K}(t_{n})}=\{K(t_{i},t_{j}),i,j\in[1,n]\}
determines an
n-
dimensional random variable random variable
(X_{\alpha}(t_{1}),\ldots,X_{\alpha}(t_{n}))
with Laplace transform,

E\left(e^{-\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}X_{\alpha}(t_{i})}\right)=\frac{1}{|I+\mathbf{{\cal K}(t_{n})}S(\mathbf{s_{n}})|^{\alpha}},

(1.1)

where
S(\mathbf{s_{n}})
is a diagonal matrix with positive entries
\mathbf{s_{n}}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
, and
\alpha>0
. We refer to
\mathbf{{\cal K}(t_{n})}
as the kernel of
(X_{\alpha}(t_{1}),\ldots,X_{\alpha}(t_{n}))
.
It follows from the Kolmogorov Extension Theorem that
\{K(s,t),s,t\in T\}
determines a stochastic process which we denote by
X_{\alpha}=\{X_{\alpha}(t),t\in T\}
and refer to as an
\alpha-
permanental process.

There are well known examples of
\alpha-
permanental processes when
\alpha=k/2
.
Let
\eta=\{\eta(t);t\in R^{1}\}
be a mean zero Gaussian process with covariance
U=\{U(s,t),s,t\in R^{1}\}.
Let
\{\eta_{i};i=1,\ldots,k\}
be independent copies of
\eta
. Define,

Y_{k/2}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\eta^{2}_{i}(t)}{2},\qquad t\in{\cal T}.

(1.2)

The positive stochastic process
\{Y_{k/2}(t),t\in R^{1}\}
is referred to as a chi–square process of order
k
with covariance
U
.
It is well known that for
\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\}\subset{\cal T}

E\left(e^{-\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}Y_{k/2}(t_{j})}\right)=\frac{1}{|I+\overline{U}(\mathbf{t_{n}})S(\mathbf{s_{n}})|^{k/2}},

(1.3)

where
\overline{U}(\mathbf{t_{n}})
is the
n\times n
matrix,
\{U(t_{j},t_{k});1\leq j,k\leq n\}
.
Therefore
Y_{k/2}=\{Y_{k/2}(t);t\in{\cal T}\}
is a
k/2
–permanental process with kernel
\overline{U}(n)
.

It is important to note that whereas (
1.3
) holds for all integers
k
whenever
U
is the covariance of a Gaussian process, it may or may not hold when
k/2

is replaced by some
\alpha>0
that is not a multiple of 1/2. (The question of when
(
1.3
) holds for all
\alpha>0
was raised by Paul Lévy when he asked when does a Gaussian process have infinitely divisible squares. This was answered by
Griffiths and Bapat. An extensive treatment of these results is given in
[
9
, Chapter 13]
.) Nevertheless,
it follows from Eisenbaum and Kaspi,
[
5
, Theorem 3.1]
that if
\widetilde{U}=\{\widetilde{U}(s,t),s,t\in T\}
is
a potential density of a transient Markov process on
T
which is finite for all
s,t\in T
, then there exist
\alpha-
permanental processes
\{Y_{\alpha,t},t\in T\}
with kernel
\widetilde{U}
for all
\alpha>0
.

It is at this point that this really becomes interesting because in the Eisenbaum Kaspi theorem the kernel
\widetilde{U}
is not necessarily symmetric, or equivalent to a symmetric matrix; (see
[
15
]
). When it is not, the corresponding permanental processes are not squares of a Gaussian process. They are a new kind of positive stochastic process defined by kernels that need not be symmetric. It is intriguing to study sample path properties of these processes. They can not be analyzed using standard Gaussian process techniques.

In this paper we obtain LILs for the local behavior of a large class of
k/2-
permanental processes.
We can get an idea of what they should look like by considering what they are for
\{\eta^{2}(t)/2,t\in R^{1}\}
, where
\eta=\{\eta(t);t\in R^{1}\}
is a mean zero Gaussian process with covariance
\{u(x,y),x,y\in R^{1}\}
. (As we point out above,
\{\eta^{2}(t)/2,t\in R^{1}\}
, is a
1/2-
permanental process with kernel
u
.)

Let
\phi(t)
be a positive function on
[0,\epsilon]
for some
\epsilon>0
.
Suppose that for
d\in R^{1}
, where
u(d,d)=E(\eta^{2}(d))>0
,

\limsup_{t\to 0}\frac{|\eta(d+t)-\eta(d)|}{\left(2\phi(t)\log\log 1/t\right)^{1/2}}=1,\qquad a.s.,

(1.4)

or, equivalently,

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\sup_{|t|\leq\delta}\frac{|\eta(d+t)-\eta(d)|}{\left(2\phi(t)\log\log 1/t\right)^{1/2}}=1,\qquad a.s.

(1.5)

(These local moduli of continuity hold for a very large class of Gaussian processes. See e.g.,
[
9
, Theorem 7.2.15]
.) Then since,

\frac{\eta^{2}(d+t)}{2}-\frac{\eta^{2}(d)}{2}=\frac{1}{2}(\eta(d+t)-\eta(d))(\eta(d+t)+\eta(d)),

(1.6)

we see that,

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to 0}\frac{|\eta^{2}(d+t)/2-\eta^{2}(d)/2|}{\left(2\phi(t)\log\log 1/t\right)^{1/2}}

(1.7)

\displaystyle\quad=\limsup_{t\to 0}\frac{|\eta(d+t)-\eta(d)|}{\left(2\phi(t)\log\log 1/t\right)^{1/2}}|\eta(d)|=\sqrt{2}\left(\frac{\eta^{2}(d)}{2}\right)^{1/2},\qquad a.s.

In
[
16
]
we generalize this to Chi-square processes,
X_{k/2}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\eta^{2}_{i}(t)/2
,
k\geq 1
, where
\{\eta_{i},i=1,\ldots,k\}
are independent copies of
\eta
. We find that
for all
k\geq 1
,

\limsup_{t\to d}\frac{|X_{k/2}(t)-X_{k/2}(d)|}{(2\phi(t)\log\log 1/t)^{1/2}}=\sqrt{2}X^{1/2}_{k/2}(d)\qquad a.s.

(1.8)

In this paper we extend this LIL to a wide class of
k/2
permanental processes with kernels that are not symmetric and consequently are not Chi–square processes.

Let
T
denote a locally compact space with a countable base and let
Y
be a symmetric transient Borel right process with state space
T
, and potential densities
u(x,y)
with respect to some reference measure
m
.
If there exist functions
u^{\beta}=\{u^{\beta}(x,y),x,y\in T\}
for which,

E^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\beta t}h\left(Y_{t}\right)\,dt\right)=\int_{T}u^{\beta}(x,y)h(y)\,dm(y),

(1.9)

for all
x\in T
and all positive measurable functions
h
on
T
, we refer to
u^{\beta}

as the
\beta-
potential densities of
Y
with respect to the reference measure
m
. (In this case we say that
Y
is strongly symmetric,
[
9
, p. 75]
.) When (
1.9
) holds for
\beta=0
, we say that
0-
potential densities exist and refer to them simply as the potential densities of
Y
. In addition we use the convention that the domain of
h
is extended to include
\Delta
, the cemetery state for
Y
, and
h(\Delta)=0
. (This is necessary for (
1.9
) to make sense.)

Let
\{f(x)
,
x\in T\}
be an excessive function for
Y
. (A function
f
is excessive for
Y
if
E^{x}\left(f(Y_{t})\right)\uparrow f(x)
as
t\to 0
for all
x\in T
.)
It is easy to check that for any positive measurable function
h
,

f(x)=\int_{T}u(x,y)h(y)\,dm(y)=E^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}h\left(Y_{t}\right)\,dt\right),

(1.10)

is excessive for
Y
.
Excessive functions that can be expressed in this way are called potential functions for
Y
.

Let
\{f(x)
,
x\in T\}
and
\{g(x)
,
x\in T\}
be excessive functions for
Y
. Set

u_{g,f}(x,y)=u(x,y)+g(x)f(y),\qquad x,y\in T.

(1.11)

The fact that
u_{g,f}:=\{u_{g,f}(x,y),x,y\in T\}
is the kernel of
\alpha-
permanental processes is given by the next theorem. When
g\equiv 1
this result is in
[
13
, Theorem 1.11]
.

Theorem 1.1

Let
{Y}\!=\!(\Omega,{\cal F}_{t},Y_{t},\theta_{t},P^{x})
be a symmetric transient Borel right process with state space
T
, and continuous potential densities
u(x,y)
with respect to some locally bounded reference measure
m
. Then for any finite excessive functions
g
and
f
for
Y
and
\alpha>0
,
u_{g,f}

is the kernel of an
\alpha
–permanental process.

In
[
13
, Theorem 1.11]
we require that
u(x,y)>0
for all
x,y\in T
. This is used, along with
[
6
, (2.5)]
, to show that the reference measure
m
is locally bounded. In the statement of Theorem
1.1
we simply include the statement that the reference measure
m
is locally bounded in the hypothesis.

When
Y
in Theorem
1.1
has continuous potential densities
u=\{u(x,y),\newline
x,y\in T\}
with respect to some reference measure on
T
,
it follows from
[
9
, Lemma 3.3.3]
that
\{u(x,y);x,y\in T\}
is positive definite and therefore is the covariance of a Gaussian process. We refer to Gaussian processes with covariances that are the potential densities of transient Markov processes as associated Gaussian processes. By
[
9
, Lemma 3.4.3]
these covariances have the property that,

u(x,y)\leq u(x,x)\wedge u(y,y),\hskip 14.45377pt\forall x,y\in T.

(1.12)

(This is a significant condition since, in general, covariances of Gaussian processes only satisfy
u(x,y)\leq(u(x,x)u(y,y))^{1/2}
.)
It follows from
[
9
, Lemma 3.4.6]
that (
1.12
) implies that,

u(x,x)>0,\hskip 14.45377pt\forall x\in T.

(1.13)

To obtain LILs for permanental processes with kernels of the form of (
1.11
) we must identify the symmetric potential densities
u(x,y)
. In Theorems
1.2
we take them to be the potential densities of Lévy processes killed at a time that is an independent exponential random variable.

Let
Z=\{Z_{t};t\in R^{1}\}
be a real valued symmetric
Lévy process with characteristic exponent
\psi
, i.e.,

E\left(e^{i\lambda Z_{t}}\right)=e^{-t\psi(\lambda)},\qquad\forall\,t\geq 0,

(1.14)

where,

\frac{1}{1+\psi(\lambda)}\in L^{1}(R^{1}).

(1.15)

For
\beta>0
, the
\beta-
potential density of
Z
with respect to Lebesgue measure is,

u^{\beta}:=u^{\beta}(x,y)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\cos\lambda(x-y)}{\beta+\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda,\qquad x,y\in R^{1};

(1.16)

see e.g.,
[
9
, (4.84)]
. Note that the potential density
u^{\beta}
is the
0-
potential density of the process
Y=\{Y_{t},t\in R^{1}\}
that is obtained by killing
Z
at the end of an independent exponential time with mean
1/\beta
. These are the processes that we consider in Theorem
1.2
.

Note that the function
\{u^{\beta}(x,y),x,y\in R^{1}\}

is translation invariant and consequently it is the covariance of a stationary Gaussian process. The proof of Theorem
1.2
uses sample path properties of these Gaussian processes.
When we consider translation invariant potential densities like
u^{\beta}(x,y)
we alternately write them as,

u^{\beta}(x,y)\qquad\text{and}\qquad u^{\beta}(x-y).

(1.17)

Throughout this paper we use the notation,

\Delta_{d}(\delta)=[d-\delta,d+\delta].

(1.18)

Theorem 1.2

I
.
Let
Y
be a symmetric Lévy process in
R^{1}
killed at an exponentially distributed time with mean
1/\beta
, with continuous potential densities
u^{\beta}=\{u^{\beta}(x-y),x,y\in R^{1}\}
with respect to Lebesgue measure.
For any integer
k\geq 1
, let
X_{k/2}=\{X_{k/2}(t),t\in R^{1}\}
be a
k/2-
permanental process with kernel

u^{\beta}_{g,f}(x,y)=u^{\beta}(x-y)+g(x)f(y),\qquad x,y\in R^{1}.

(1.19)

where
g,f
are excessive functions for
Y
.

Set

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}\left(x\right)=2\left(u^{\beta}(0)-u^{\beta}(x)\right).

(1.20)

Assume that,

(i)

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}\left(x\right)
is regularly varying at
0
with index
0<r\leq 1
;

(ii)

for some
p>1
and
\delta>0
,

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}\left(x\right)\geq x\left(\log 1/x\right)^{p},\qquad x\in[0,\delta].

(1.21)

Then
for any
d\in R^{1}
and
g,f\in C^{1}(\Delta_{d}(\delta))
for some
\delta>0
,

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|X_{k/2}(d+x)-X_{k/2}(d)|}{\left(2(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}\left(x\right)\log\log 1/|x|\right)^{1/2}}=\left(2X_{k/2}(d)\right)^{1/2},\qquad a.s.

(1.22)

II.
This LIL continues to hold without requiring condition
(ii)
when
g,f\in C^{2}(\Delta_{d}(\delta))
, for some
\delta>0
, and
g^{\prime}(d)=f^{\prime}(d)=0
.

The random variable
X_{k/2}(d)
is a chi–square random variable of order
k
which is the sum of
k
independent squares of a normal random variable with mean zero and variance
u^{\beta}(0)+g(d)f(d)
.

We now explain the significance of Theorem
1.2
, II. To begin we note that
\psi(\lambda)
, the characteristic exponent of a Lévy process can be written as,

\psi(\lambda)=C\lambda^{2}+\psi_{1}(\lambda),\qquad\text{where $\psi_{1}(\lambda)=o(\lambda^{2})$ as $\lambda\to\infty$}.

(1.23)

When
C>0
the Lévy process has a Gaussian component and,

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\sim\frac{|x|}{C},\qquad\text{as $x\to 0$.}

(1.24)

This follows from (
1.29
) below because
C_{2}=1
; (see
[
7
, 3.741.3]
).
Consequently, the condition in (
1.21
) implies that the Lévy processes
Y
do not have a Gaussian component.

We also want to consider Lévy processes with a Gaussian component.
Taking the Fourier transform we see that,

{e^{-a|y-x|}\over a}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\cos\lambda(x-y)}{a^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\,d\lambda.

(1.25)

Therefore, for
\beta>0
,

u^{\beta}_{C}(x-y):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\cos\lambda(x-y)}{\beta+C\lambda^{2}}\,d\lambda=\frac{1}{2}{e^{-({\beta/C})^{1/2}\,|x-y|}\over({\beta C})^{1/2}},\qquad x,y\in R^{1}.

(1.26)

When
C=1/2
this is the potential density of Brownian motion killed after an independent exponential time with mean
1/\beta
.
In these cases the potential densities
\{u^{\beta}(x,y);x,y\in R^{1}\}
are also the covariances of Ornstein–Uhlenbeck processes on the positive half line, which are stationary Gaussian process on
R^{1}
.

Note that for all
\beta>0
,

(\sigma_{C}^{\beta})^{2}\left(x\right)=2(u_{C}^{\beta}(x,x)-u_{C}^{\beta}(x,0))\sim\frac{|x|}{C},\qquad\text{as $x\to 0$},

(1.27)

does not depend on
\beta
.
Theorem
1.2
, II enables us to consider
Lévy processes
Y
with a Gaussian component.
When
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}\left(x\right)
is regularly varying at
0
with index
0<r<1
, (
1.21
) is satisfied and we don’t need the restrictive condition that
f^{\prime}(d)=g^{\prime}(d)=0
.

For all
\beta\geq 0
set,

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}\left(x\right)=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1-\cos(\lambda x)}{\beta+\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda.

(1.28)

(This is (
1.20
) when
\beta>0
.)
In Section
8
we show that there exist characteristic exponents
\psi
that are asymptotic to every regularly varying function at infinity with index
1<r<2
, and to a large class of regularly varying functions at infinity with index 2 subject to the condition that
\psi(\lambda)=o(\lambda^{2})
.
When
\psi
is asymptotic to a regularly varying function at infinity with index
1<r\leq 2
,

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\sim C_{r}\frac{1}{|x|\psi(1/x)},\qquad\text{as $x\to 0$},\,\forall\,\beta\geq 0,

(1.29)

where,

C_{r}=\frac{4}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}s/2}{s^{r}}\,ds.

(1.30)

Consequently, the denominator in (
1.22
) does not depend on
\beta
, although it does depend on
r
.

We now consider a class of symmetric potential densities that are not translation invariant. As in
Theorem
1.2
let
Y
be a symmetric Lévy process in
R^{1}
that is killed at the end of an exponential time with mean
1/\beta
, with continuous translation invariant potential densities
u^{\beta}=\{u^{\beta}(x,y),x,y\in R^{1}\}
with respect to Lebesgue measure.
Let
Y^{\prime}
be the symmetric Borel right process with state space
T=R^{1}-\{0\}
obtained by killing
Y
the first time it hits
0
. The process
Y^{\prime}
has continuous potential densities with respect to Lebesgue measure restricted to
T
, which we denote by
v^{\beta}=\{v^{\beta}(x,y),x,y\in T\}
, where

\displaystyle v^{\beta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{\beta}(x,y)-\frac{u^{\beta}(x,0)u^{\beta}(0,y)}{u^{\beta}(0,0)}

(1.31)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{\beta}(x-y)-\frac{u^{\beta}(x)u^{\beta}(y)}{u^{\beta}(0)};

see
[
9
, (4.165)]
.

Theorem 1.3

I.
Let
Y^{\prime}
be a symmetric Borel right process with state space
T=R^{1}-\{0\}
as described above with continuous potential densities
v^{\beta}=\{v^{\beta}(x,y),x,y\in T\}
with respect to Lebesgue measure restricted to
T
.
Let
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)
be as in (
1.20
)
and assume that;

(i)

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)
is regularly varying at
0
with index
0<r\leq 1
;

(ii)

for some
p>1
, and
\delta>0,

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\geq x\left(\log 1/x\right)^{p},\qquad x\in[0,\delta];

(1.32)

(iii)

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\in C^{1}\left(T\right).

For integers
k\geq 1
, let
X_{k/2}=\{X_{k/2}(t),t\in T\}
be a
k/2
-permanental process with kernel

v^{\beta}_{g,f}(x,y)=v^{\beta}(x,y)+g(x)f(y),\qquad x,y\in T,

(1.33)

where
g
and
f
are excessive functions for
Y^{\prime}
. Then
for any
d\neq 0
, when
g,f\in C^{1}(\Delta_{d}(\delta))
for some
\delta>0
,

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|X_{k/2}(d+x)-X_{k/2}(d)|}{\left(2(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\log\log 1/|x|\right)^{1/2}}=\left(2X_{k/2}(d)\right)^{1/2},\qquad a.s.

(1.34)

II.
This LIL continues to hold without requiring condition
(ii)
when
g,f\in C^{2}(\Delta_{d}(\delta))
, for some
\delta>0
,
g^{\prime}(d)=f^{\prime}(d)=0
and
\psi(\lambda)
is a stable mixture, (defined immediately below).

In Section
8
we show that condition
(iii)
of Theorem
1.3
, that
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\in C^{1}\left(T\right)
, is satisfied when
\psi(\lambda)
is the characteristic exponent of Lévy processes that are stable mixtures. That is when,

\psi\left(\lambda\right)=\int_{1}^{2}\lambda^{s}\,d\mu(s),

(1.35)

where
\mu
is a finite positive measure on
(1,2]
. See Remark
8.2
.

Remark 1.1

There is a further restriction that has to be added to Theorem
1.3
, II. When
Y
is exponentially killed Brownian motion starting at some point
x>0
, then because its paths are continuous, the process
Y^{\prime}
obtained by killing
Y
the first time it hits
0
has state space
(0,\infty)
not
R^{1}-\{0\}
. Similarly, if it starts at
x<0
.

We now consider (
1.16
) with
\beta=0
, in which case,

u^{0}(0,0)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda.

(1.36)

Note that when

\frac{\psi(\lambda)}{\lambda}=O(1),\qquad\text{as $\lambda\to 0$},

(1.37)

u^{0}(0,0)=\infty.
Nevertheless, by modifying
u^{0}(x,y)
we can obtain the potential densities of Markov processes.

Let

\phi(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1-\cos\lambda x}{\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda,\qquad x\in R^{1}.

(1.38)

(The integral near
\lambda=0
is finite and continuous in
x
because
\psi
is the characteristic exponent of a Lévy process. See
[
9
, (4.77)]
. It is finite and continuous in
x
on the rest of its domain by (
1.15
).)
Set,

u^{(0)}=u^{(0)}(x,y)=\phi(x)+\phi(y)-\phi(x-y),\qquad x,y\in R^{1}-\{0\}.

(1.39)

It follows from
[
9
, Theorem 4.2.4]
that
u^{(0)}
is the potential density with respect to Lebesgue measure of the Lévy process with characteristic exponent
\psi(\lambda)
, say
\overline{Z}
, killed the first time it hits 0. Denote this process by
\overline{Y}
.
For example if
\overline{Z}
is a symmetric stable process
u^{(0)}
is the covariance of fractional Brownian motion.

Since
\{u^{(0)}(x,y)
,
x,y\in R^{1}-\{0\}\}
is the potential density of a symmetric Borel right process it is positive definite and therefore it is the covariance of a Gaussian process. Furthermore, since
\{u^{(0)}(x,y)
,
x,y\in R^{1}\}
is continuous and
u^{(0)}(0,0)=0
, it is
also the covariance of a Gaussian process, say
\eta=\{\eta(x),x\in R^{1}\}
, with
\eta(0)=0
. We define,

\displaystyle(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\left(\eta(x)-\eta(y)\right)^{2}

(1.40)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{(0)}(x,x)+u^{(0)}(y,y)-2u^{(0)}(x,y)=2\phi(x-y).

Note that
\eta
has stationary increments;
see Remark
4.2
. Therefore we write,

(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x,y)=(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x-y).

(1.41)

We have,

(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x)=2\phi(x)=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1-\cos\lambda x}{\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda=(\sigma^{0})^{2}(x),

(1.42)

where
(\sigma^{0})^{2}(x)
is given in (
1.28
).

Theorem 1.4

I.
Let
\overline{Y}
be a symmetric Borel right process with state space
T=R^{1}-\{0\}
and continuous potential densities
u^{(0)}
, (defined in (
1.39
)).
Let
(\sigma^{0})^{2}(x)
be as in (
1.42
)
and assume that;

(i)

(\sigma^{0})^{2}(x)
is regularly varying at
0
with index
0<r\leq 1
;

(ii)

for some
p>1
, and
\delta>0,

(\sigma^{0})^{2}(x)\geq x\left(\log 1/x\right)^{p},\qquad x\in[0,\delta];

(1.43)

(iii)

(\sigma^{0})^{2}(x)\in C^{1}(T)
.

For integers
k\geq 1
, let
X_{k/2}=\{X_{k/2}(t),t\in R^{1}-\{0\}\}
be a
k/2
-permanental process with kernel

u_{g,f}(x,y)=u^{(0)}(x,y)+g(x)f(y),\qquad x,y\in R^{1}-\{0\},

(1.44)

where
g,f
are excessive functions for
\overline{Y}
.
Then, for any
d\neq 0
, when
g,f\in C^{1}(\Delta_{d}(\delta))
for some
\delta>0
,

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|X_{k/2}(d+x)-X_{k/2}(d)|}{\left(2(\sigma^{0})^{2}(x)\log\log 1/|x|\right)^{1/2}}=\left(2X_{k/2}(d)\right)^{1/2},\qquad a.s.

(1.45)

II.

This LIL continues to hold without requiring condition
(ii)
when
g,f\in C^{2}(\Delta_{d}(\delta))
, for some
\delta>0
,
g^{\prime}(d)=f^{\prime}(d)=0
and
(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x)
is increasing in some neighborhood of
d
.

It follows from
[
9
, Lemma 9.6.3]
that
(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x)
is increasing when
\psi(\lambda)
is a stable mixture.

We now consider permanental processes for which the symmetric part of the kernel is the potential density of a diffusion.
Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a diffusion in
R^{1}
that is regular and without traps and is symmetric with respect to some
\sigma
–finite measure
m
.
(A diffusion is regular and without traps when

P^{x}\left(T_{y}<\infty\right)>0,\forall x,y\in R^{1}
.) We assume that the second order generator for
\mathcal{Z}
has continuous coefficients.

Analogous to the study of the Lévy process
Z

on page
1.14
we consider three transient symmetric diffusions determined by
\cal Z
:

a.

Z
is
\cal Z
killed at the end of an independent exponential time with mean
1/\beta
. The potential of
Z
is

\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}p(x)q(y),&\quad x\leq y\\
q(x)p(y),&\quad y\leq x\end{array}\right.,

(1.46)

where
p,q\in C^{2}(R^{1})
are positive and strictly convex and
p
is increasing and

q
is decreasing. (For the dependence of
p
and
q
on
\beta
see
[
9
, (4.14)]
.)

b.

Z^{\prime}
is the process with state space
T=(0,\infty)
that is obtained by starting
Z
in
T
and then killing it the first time it hits 0. The potential of
Z^{\prime}
is

\widetilde{v}^{\beta}(x,y)=\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)-\frac{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,0)\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,y)}{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,0)},\qquad x,y>0,

(1.47)

so that

\widetilde{v}^{\beta}(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}p(x)q(y)-\displaystyle\frac{p(0)}{q(0)}q(x)q(y),&\quad 0<x\leq y\\
\\
q(x)p(y)-\displaystyle\frac{p(0)}{q(0)}q(x)q(y),&\quad 0<y\leq x.\end{array}\right.

(1.48)

c.

\overline{Z}
is the process with state space
T=(0,\infty)
that is obtained by starting
\cal Z
in
T
and then killing it the first time it hits 0.

\overline{Z}
has potential densities

\widetilde{u}_{T_{0}}(x,y)=s(x)\wedge s(y),\qquad x,y>0,

(1.49)

where
s\in C^{2}(0,\infty)
is strictly positive and strictly increasing.

Theorem 1.5

a
.
For any integer
k\geq 1
, let
X_{k/2}=\{X_{k/2}(t),t\in R^{1}\}
be a
k/2-
permanental process with kernel

\widetilde{u}^{\beta}_{g,f}(x,y)=\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)+g(x)f(y),\qquad x,y\in R^{1}.

(1.50)

where
f,g
are excessive functions for
Z
.
Then for any
d\in R^{1}
for which
g,f\in C^{2}(\Delta_{d}(\delta))
for some
\delta>0
, and
g^{\prime}(d)=f^{\prime}(d)=0
,

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|X_{k/2}(d+x)-X_{k/2}(d)|}{\left(2\tau(d)|x|\log\log 1/|x|\right)^{1/2}}=\left(2X_{k/2}(d)\right)^{1/2},\qquad a.s.

(1.51)

where,

\tau(d)=\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(d,d)\frac{d}{dx}\left(\log\frac{p(x)}{q(x)}\right)\bigg|_{x=d}\,.

(1.52)

b
.
For any integer
k\geq 1
, let
X_{k/2}=\{X_{k/2}(t),t\in(0,\infty)\}
be a
k/2-
permanental process with kernel

\widetilde{v}^{\beta}_{g,f}(x,y)=\widetilde{v}^{\beta}(x,y)+g(x)f(y),\qquad x,y>0.

(1.53)

where
f,g
are excessive functions for
Z^{\prime}
.
Then for any
d>0
for which
g,f\in C^{2}(\Delta_{d}(\delta))
for some
\delta>0
, (
1.51
)
continues to hold for
x\to 0
.

c
.
For any integer
k\geq 1
, let
X_{k/2}=\{X_{k/2}(t),t\in(0,\infty)\}
be a
k/2-
permanental process with kernel

\widetilde{u}_{T_{0},g,f}(x,y)=\widetilde{u}_{T_{0}}(x,y)+g(x)f(y),\qquad x,y>0.

(1.54)

where
f,g
are excessive functions for
\overline{Z}
. Then for any
d>0
for which
g,f\in C^{2}(\Delta_{d}(\delta))
for some
\delta>0
, and
g^{\prime}(d)=f^{\prime}(d)=0
,

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|X_{k/2}(d+x)-X_{k/2}(d)|}{\left(2s^{\prime}(d)|x|\log\log 1/|x|\right)^{1/2}}=\left(2X_{k/2}(d)\right)^{1/2},\qquad a.s.

(1.55)

Remark 1.2

To reconcile the terms in the denominators of (
1.22
) and (
1.51
) we show in Lemma
4.9
that

(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\beta})(x-y)\sim\tau(x)|x-y|
as
|x-y|\to 0
.

Remark 1.3

Killing a diffusion on
R^{1}
the first time it hits
0
gives rise to three different processes: One on
(0,\infty)
, a second one on
(-\infty,0)
, and a third process on
R^{1}-\{0\}
. In the third case, if the process starts in
(0,\infty)
it remains in
(0,\infty)
throughout its lifetime, and similarly if it in starts in
(-\infty,0)
. In this paper we consider the process on
(0,\infty)
.

Remark 1.4

In all the LILs in Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
we take
\limsup_{x\to 0}
. The statements also hold for
\limsup_{x\uparrow 0}
and
\limsup_{x\downarrow 0}
. This observation is particularly relevant to the LIL in Theorem
1.5
,
c.
, since we show in Section
9.7.3
that when
g,f\in C^{2}(0,\infty)
are excessive functions for
\overline{Z}
and
g^{\prime}(d)=f^{\prime}(d)=0
for some
d>0
, then
g(x)
and
f(x)
are constant for

x\geq d
. Consequently
\widetilde{u}_{T_{0},g,f}(x,y)
in (
1.54
) is symmetric for
x,y\geq d
. Therefore we want to consider the analogue of (
1.55
) with
\limsup_{x\to 0}
replaced by
\limsup_{x\uparrow 0}
.

We give two specific examples of LILs for permanental processes. In the first we consider the situation in Theorem
1.4
when,

\phi(x)=\frac{4|x|^{\rho}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}s/2}{s^{\rho+1}}\,ds:=|x|^{\rho}C_{\rho+1},\qquad\rho\leq 1.

(1.56)

Here
\phi(x)+\phi(y)-\phi(x-y)
is the potential of a symmetric stable process with index
\rho+1
, when
\rho<1
, or a constant times Brownian motion when
\rho=1
, killed the first time it hits 0. Label this process
\widetilde{Y}
. The function
\phi(x)
is given by (
1.38
) with
\psi(\lambda)=|\lambda|^{\rho+1}
.

For integers
k\geq 1
, let
Z_{k/2}=\{Z_{k/2}(t),t\in R^{1}-\{0\}\}
be a
k/2
-permanental process with kernel

C_{\rho+1}\left(|x|^{\rho}+|y|^{\rho}-|x-y|^{\rho}\right)+g(x)f(y),\qquad x,y\in R^{1}-\{0\},

(1.57)

where
g,f
are excessive functions for
\widetilde{Y}
, with the property that
g,f\in C^{1}
on
[d,d+\delta]
,
0\notin[d,d+\delta]
.
Then when
\rho<1
,

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|Z_{k/2}(d+x)-Z_{k/2}(d)|}{\left(4C_{\rho+1}\,|x|^{\rho}\log\log 1/|x|\right)^{1/2}}=\left(2Z_{k/2}(d)\right)^{1/2},\qquad a.s.

(1.58)

If in addition
g,f\in C^{2}(T)
and
g^{\prime}(d)=f^{\prime}(d)=0
this LIL continues to hold when
\rho=1
. (In which case
|x|+|y|-|x-y|=|x|\wedge|y|
is the covariance of Brownian motion.)

Here
Z_{k/2}(d)
is a chi–square random variable of order
k
, as described in the paragraph following Theorem
1.2
, in which the mean zero normal random variable has variance
2C_{\rho+1}|d|^{\rho}+g(d)f(d)
.

We next consider the situation in Theorem
1.2
where
u^{\beta}(x,y)
is given in (
1.26
) with
C=1/2
. This is the potential density of Brownian motion, killed after an independent exponential time with mean
1/\beta
.
For any integer
k\geq 1
let
\widetilde{Z}_{k/2}=\{\widetilde{Z}_{k/2}(t),t\in R^{1}\}
be a
k/2
-permanental process with kernel
\{u^{\beta}(x,y)+g(x)f(y),x,y\in R^{1}\}
,
where
f,g
are
C^{2}(R^{1})
excessive function for
\overline{B}
.
Then for any
d\in R^{1}
with
f^{\prime}(d),g^{\prime}(d)=0
, for all integers
k\geq 1
,

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|\widetilde{Z}_{k/2}(d+x)-\widetilde{Z}_{k/2}(d)|}{\left(4|x|\log\log 1/|x|\right)^{1/2}}=\left(2\widetilde{Z}_{k/2}(d)\right)^{1/2},\qquad a.s.

(1.59)

Here
\widetilde{Z}_{k/2}(d)
is a chi–square random variable of order k, as described in the paragraph following Theorem
1.2
, in which the mean zero normal random variable has variance
(2\beta)^{-1/2}+g(d)f(d)
.

Our initial interest in permanental processes was that their sample path properties can be used to obtain sample path properties of local times of related Markov processes. (See
[
10
]
.) In Sections

5
–
7
we give LILs for the local times of Markov processes with potential densities that are the same as or closely related to kernels of the form of
u_{g,f}(x,y)
in (
1.11
), and consequently, in different examples, are equal to the kernels of the permanental processes in Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
.
These Markov processes are obtained by rebirthing a symmetric transient Borel right process, say
Y
with state space
T
, and potential densities
u(x,y)
with respect to some reference measure
m
, to obtain a new Markov process
\widetilde{Y}
. I.e.,
\widetilde{Y}
starts out as
Y
, but instead of going to a cemetery state at the end of its lifetime it is “reborn” with measure
\nu
,
|\nu|\leq 1
, and remains in the cemetery state with probability
1-|\nu|
. If the process is reborn it goes to the set
B\subset T
with measure
\nu(B)
, after which it continues to evolve repeatedly as we just described. When
|\nu|<1
we say the process is partially reborn. In this case the process eventually dies out. When
|\nu|=1

we say the process is fully reborn.
In Sections
6
and
7
we use Theorems
1.2
–
1.5

to obtain LILs for the local times of partial and fully rebirthed Markov processes.

The following LIL is an example of the results the are obtained. It is a special case of LILs in Example
7.1
:
Let
Y
be a symmetric Lévy process killed at the end of an exponential time with mean
1/\beta
, as in Theorem
1.2
and let
\widetilde{Y}
be a fully rebirthed Markov process determined by
Y
and the probability measure
\mu
. The process
\widetilde{Y}
has potential densities of the form

u^{\beta}_{C,f}(x,y)=u^{\beta}(x-y)+Cf(y),\qquad x,y\in R^{1},

(1.60)

for some known constant constant
C
, where

f(y)=\int_{R^{1}}u^{\beta}(y-z)\,d\mu(z).

(1.61)

(Such functions are excessive for
Y
.)

Let

L=\{L^{y}_{t}\,,\,(y,t)\in R^{1}\times R_{+}\}

denote the local times for

\widetilde{Y}
, normalized so that

E^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\beta t}d_{t}L^{y}_{t}\right)=u^{\beta}_{C,f}(x,y),\qquad x,y\in R^{1}.

(1.62)

When
f\in C^{1}(R^{1})
and
(i)
and
(ii)
in Theorem
1.2
hold,

L
is jointly continuous on
R^{1}\times R_{+}
, and for all
v\in R^{1}
,

\limsup_{u\to v}\frac{|L^{u}_{t}-L^{v}_{t}|}{\left(2(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}\left(u-v\right)\log\log 1/|u-v|\right)^{1/2}}=\left(L^{v}_{t}\right)^{1/2},\qquad\text{for a.e. $t\in R_{+}$},

(1.63)

P^{x}
almost surely, for all
x\in R^{1}
.

Here is a more explicit example of LILs for the local times of a partially rebirthed diffusion.
Let
{\cal X}=\{{\cal X}(t),{t\in R_{+}^{1}}\}
be a diffusion
with potential densities

\widetilde{u}_{T_{0}}(x,y)=s(x)\wedge s(y),\qquad x,y>0,

(1.64)

described in (
1.49
), starting in
(0,\infty)
and killed the first time it hits 0.
Let
{\widetilde{\cal X}}=\{{\widetilde{\cal X}(t)},t\in R_{+}^{1}\}
be a partial rebirthing of
{\cal X}
determined by a measure
\mu
as described in Remark
6.1
. We take
\mu=h(x)\,dx

where
h(x)
is positive and continuous on
[0,x_{0}]
with
h(x)=0
on
[x_{0},\infty)
. The partially rebirthed process
\widetilde{\cal X}
has state space
S=(0,\infty)\cup\{*\}
, where
*
is an isolated point that
\cal X
and subsequently
\widetilde{\cal X}
goes to when it dies and awaits a possible rebirth. Let
\{\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}_{T_{0}}(x,y),x,y\in S\}
denote the potential density of
\widetilde{\cal X}
. It is a function of
\widetilde{u}_{T_{0}}(x,y)

and
\mu
as described in Theorem
6.1
.

Let
\widetilde{L}=\{\widetilde{L}_{t}^{y},(y,t)\in S\times(0,\infty)\}
be the local times of
\widetilde{\cal X}
normalized so that

E^{x}\widetilde{L}_{\infty}^{y}=\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}_{T_{0}}(x,y).

(1.65)

Then for any
x_{0}\in(0,\infty)

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|\widetilde{L}_{t}^{x_{0}+x}-\widetilde{L}_{t}^{x_{0}}|}{(2s^{\prime}(x_{0})|x|\log\log 1/x)^{1/2}}=\left({2\widetilde{L}_{t}^{x_{0}}}\right)^{1/2}\qquad a.s.\mbox{ for a.e. }t<\zeta,

(1.66)

and

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|\widetilde{L}_{\infty}^{x_{0}+x}-\widetilde{L}_{\infty}^{x_{0}}|}{(2s^{\prime}(x_{0})|x|\log\log 1/x)^{1/2}}=\left({2\widetilde{L}_{\infty}^{x_{0}}}\right)^{1/2}\qquad a.s.,

(1.67)

P^{v}
almost surely, for all
v\in(0,\infty)
. For details see Remarks
6.3
and
9.1
. (This also holds for
\limsup_{x\uparrow 0}
).

To see the dependence of
\widetilde{L}_{\infty}^{x_{0}}
on
\mu
we note that,

E^{v}\widetilde{L}_{\infty}^{x_{0}}=s(v)\wedge s(x_{0})+\int_{0}^{x_{0}}s(y)h(y)\,dy.

(1.68)

This integral can be expressed in a more probabilistic fashion since it is equal to
\|h\|_{1}E(s(\theta))
where
\theta
is a random variable with probability density
h/\|h\|_{1}
.

Results on sample path properties of permanental processes are given in
[
10
,
11
,
13
]
. The upper bounds in Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
are given in
[
13
, Theorems 1.13]
, and in fact hold for all
\alpha>0
, not just
\alpha=k/2
for integers
k\geq 1
.
However, finding lower bounds for the local and uniform moduli of continuity of permanental processes seems to be more difficult.
In
[
13
, Section 7]
we use a Slepian type lemma to get lower bounds for certain sequences of permanental process like
\{X_{\alpha}(t_{j}),j\in\mathbb{N}\}
. However, we can not apply this lemma to increments
\{X_{\alpha}(t_{j})-X_{\alpha}(0),j\in\mathbb{N}\}
. (The classical Slepian’s lemma for Gaussian processes applies to increments because the increment of a Gaussian process is a Gaussian process. We can not apply the Slepian type lemma for permanental processes to increments of a permanental process because increments of a permanental process are generally not a permanental process. This is easy to see. Let
\{X(t),t\in T\}
be a permanental process. Let
t_{0}\in T
and assume that
X(t_{0})\neq 0
. Then
\{X(t)-X(t_{0}),t\in T\}
is not a permanental process because the terms of a permanental process are all positive.)

To prove Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
we use a comparison theorem between general permanental processes and permanental processes with symmetric kernels, Theorem
2.1
, and show in Lemma
2.3
how to use it to relate
\{X_{k/2}(t),t\in[0,1]\}
to chi-square processes so that we can use Gaussian techniques to study their sample path properties. In Section
3
we prove Theorem
3.1
which gives lower bounds for the LILs of a wide class of permanental processes with kernels that are not symmetric. Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
are proved in Section
4
.
In Section
8
and
9
we give examples of potentials and excessive functions that satisfy the hypotheses of Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
.

In Section
5
we consider Markov processes with potential densities that are the same as the kernels of 1/2–permanental processes. In Theorems
5.1
and
5.4
we show that the local and uniform moduli of continuity
of the local times of the Markov processes are the same as the local and uniform moduli of continuity
of the 1/2–permanental processes. These theorems are used in Section
6
to give LILs for the local times of partially rebirthed Markov processes with potential densities that are the same as the kernels of the permanental processes in Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
. These theorems are also used in Section
7
to give LILs for the local times of certain fully rebirthed Markov processes.

The hypotheses of Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
contain conditions on kernels of permanental processes and excessive functions of related Markov processes. In Section
8
we give many examples when the conditions on the kernels are satisfied. We do the same in Section
9
for the excessive functions.

We thank Pat Fitzsimmons for his help on Sections
5
and
7
.

2
Comparing general permanental sequences to symmetric permanental sequences

Finding lower bounds for stochastic processes is a difficult problem. The only effective method we know is to use Slepian’s Lemma, which applies only to Gaussian processes. Therefore we need a relationship between permanental processes that are not Gaussian processes and Gaussian processes. In this paper we accomplish this by using Theorem
2.1
below.

Let
C=\{c_{i,j}\}_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
be an

n\times n
matrix. We call
C
a positive matrix,
and write

C\geq 0
, if

c_{i,j}\geq 0
for all

i,j
.

A matrix

A
is said to be a nonsingular
M
-matrix if

(1)

a_{i,j}\leq 0
for all
i\neq j
.

(2)

A
is nonsingular and
A^{-1}\geq 0
.

It follows from
[
5
, Lemma 4.2]
that the right-hand side of (
1.1
) is a Laplace transform for all
\alpha>0
if

A=K^{-1}
exists and is a nonsingular
M
-matrix. We refer to
A
as the
M
-matrix corresponding to
\widetilde{X}
. We also use the terminology that
K
is an inverse
M
-matrix.

When
A
is an
M
-matrix, we define

A_{sym}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}A_{j,j}&j=1,\ldots,n\\
-(A_{i,j}A_{j,i})^{1/2}&i,j=1,\ldots,n,i\neq j\end{array}\right..

(2.1)

In addition, when
A=K^{-1}
we define

K_{isymi}=(A_{sym})^{-1}.

(2.2)

(The notation
isymi
stands for: take the inverse, symmetrize and take the inverse again.)

Lemma 2.1

If
K
is an inverse
M
-matrix, then so is
K_{isymi}
.

Proof
By hypothesis
A=K^{-1}
is a non-singular
M
-matrix. Therefore, by
[
12
, Lemma 3.3]
,
A_{sym}
is a non-singular
M
-matrix. We have denoted it’s inverse by
K_{isymi}
.

We now show how we can relate probabilistic properties of
\alpha
-permanental processes that have kernels that are not equivalent to a symmetric kernel to
\alpha
-permanental processes that have kernels that are symmetric, and therefore, when
\alpha=k/2
, to Chi–square processes of order
k
. The next lemma combines
[
12
, Corollaries 3.1 and 3.2]
.

Theorem 2.1

For any
\alpha>0
let
Z_{\alpha}(n)=(Z_{\alpha,1},\ldots,Z_{\alpha,n})
be the
\alpha
-perman-ental random variable determined by an
n\times n
kernel
K(n)
that is an inverse
M
-matrix and set
A(n)=K(n)^{-1}
. Let
\widetilde{Z}_{\alpha}(n)
be the
\alpha
-permanental random variable determined by
K(n)_{isymi}
. Then for all functions
g_{n}
of
Z_{\alpha}(n)
and
\widetilde{Z}_{\alpha}(n)
and sets
{\cal B}_{n}
in the range of
g_{n}

\displaystyle\frac{|A(n)|^{\alpha}}{|A(n)_{sym}|^{\alpha}}P\left(g_{n}(\widetilde{Z}_{\alpha}(n))\in{\cal B}_{n}\right)\leq P\left(g_{n}(Z_{\alpha}(n))\in{\cal B}_{n}\right)

(2.3)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\leq\left(1-\frac{|A(n)|^{\alpha}}{|A(n)_{sym}|^{\alpha}}\right)+\frac{|A(n)|^{\alpha}}{|A(n)_{sym}|^{\alpha}}P\left(g_{n}(\widetilde{Z}_{\alpha}(n))\in{\cal B}_{n}\right).

We use this theorem with
\alpha=1/2
, in which case
\widetilde{Z}_{\alpha}(n)
is a process of Gaussian squares.

We now develop the material need to apply Theorem
2.1
to the
\alpha
-permanental processes considered in Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
. We begin with the proof of
Theorem
1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1

By the Kolmogorov extension theorem it suffices to show that for any finite subset
T_{m}=\{t_{0},t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}\}
in
T
there exists an
\alpha
–permanental vector, say
X_{\alpha}
, with kernel

u_{g,f}(t_{i},t_{j})=u(t_{i},t_{j})+g(t_{i})f(t_{j}),\qquad t_{i},t_{j}\in T_{m}.

(2.4)

To do this we introduce another point
t_{-1}\notin T_{m}
and show that there exists an
\alpha
-permanental vector with kernel,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ lcll }u_{g,f}(t_{-1},t_{-1})&=&1,&\\
u_{g,f}(t_{i},t_{-1})&=&g(t_{i}),&i=0,\ldots,m,\\
u_{g,f}(t_{-1},t_{j})&=&f(t_{j}),&j=0,\ldots,m,\\
u_{g,f}(t_{i},t_{j})&=&u(t_{i},t_{j})+g(t_{i})f(t_{j}),&i,j=0,\ldots,m.\end{array}

It is clear from the definition of permanental vectors in (
1.1
) by its Laplace transform that if, say
\widetilde{Y}
, is a permanental vector on a set
S
then it is a permanental vector on subsets of
S
. Therefore,
X_{\alpha}
is a permanental vector.

Let
\{U_{i,j},i,j=0,1,\ldots,m\}
be an
m+1\times m+1
matrix with entries
U_{i,j}=u(t_{i},t_{j})
. It follows from
[
5
, Lemma 4.2]
that to show that (
2
) is the kernel of an an
\alpha
–permanental vector, it suffices to show that the following
(m+2)\times(m+2)

matrix is invertible and its inverse is an M–matrix:

\displaystyle K=\left(\begin{array}[]{ cccc }1&f(t_{0})&\ldots&f(t_{m})\\
g(t_{0})&U_{0,0}+g(t_{0})f(t_{0})&\ldots&U_{0,m}+g(t_{0})f(t_{m})\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
g(t_{m})&U_{m,0}+g(t_{m})f(t_{o})&\ldots&U_{m,m}+g(t_{m})f(t_{m})\end{array}\right)\!.

It is clear from (
2
), by subtracting
g(t_{j})
times the first row from the
j+1-
st row that,

|K|=|U|.

(2.15)

Since it follows from
[
11
, Lemma A.1]
that
U
is invertible,
|U|>0
.
Therefore
K
is invertible.

Let

A=K^{-1}.

(2.16)

By multiplying the following matrix on the right by
K
one can check that,

A=\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{ cccc }1+\rho&-v_{0}&\dots&-v_{m}\\
-r_{0}&U^{0,0}&\dots&U^{0,m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-r_{m}&U^{m,1}&\dots&U^{m,m}\end{array}\!\!\!\right),

(2.17)

where,

r_{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{m}U^{i,j}g(x_{j}),\quad v_{j}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}U^{i,j}f(x_{i}),\quad i,j=1,\ldots,m,

(2.18)

and

\rho=\sum_{i,j=0}^{m}U^{i,j}f(x_{i})g(x_{j}).

(2.19)

(We use the notation
U^{i,j}=(U^{-1})_{i,j}.
)

It follows from
[
11
, Lemma A.1]
that
U^{-1}
is an M–matrix, therefore to show that
A
is an M–matrix, it suffices to show that for all
j=0,\ldots,m
,
r_{j}\geq 0
and
v_{j}\geq 0
. Since
U
is symmetric to show this it suffices to show that for any finite excessive function
h

for
\overline{Y}

\sum_{i=0}^{m}U^{i,j}h(x_{i})\geq 0,\quad i,j=1,\ldots,m.

(2.20)

We obtain (
2.20
) by considering an earlier version of this theorem in which
g\equiv 1
and
f=h
where

h(y)=\int u(x,y)\,d\mu(x),

(2.21)

and
\mu
is a finite measure on T.
We show in
[
13
, Theorem A.1]
that (
2
), with
g\equiv 1
, is the kernel of a transient Borel right process. We show in
[
11
, Lemma 7.1]
this implies that
A
is an M–matrix. This implies (
2.20
) for functions
h
expressible as in (
2.21
).
Since, as explained in the proof of
[
13
, Theorem 1.11]
, all excessive functions are limits of such functions, it follows that (
2.20
) holds for all excessive functions
h
.

Let
{\cal Y}\!=\!(\Omega,{\cal F}_{t},{\cal Y}(t),\theta_{t},P^{x})
be a symmetric Borel right process with state space
T=R^{1}
or
R^{1}-\{0\}
and continuous potential densities
u=\{u(x,y),x,y\in T\}
with respect to some locally bounded reference measure
m
on
T
. In either case we assume that
u
has a continuous extension
u=\{u(x,y),x,y\in R^{1}\}
and take

\eta=\{\eta_{x},x\in R^{1}\}
to be a Gaussian process with covariance
\{u(x,y);x,y\in R^{1}\}
.
Set

\sigma^{2}(x,y):=E(\eta_{x}-\eta_{y})^{2}=u(x,x)+u(y,y)-2u(x,y).

(2.22)

By (
1.12
)

u(x,y)\leq u(x,x)\wedge u(y,y),\qquad\forall x,y\in R^{1}.

(2.23)

Then obviously,

0\leq u(x,x)-u(x,y)\leq\sigma^{2}(x,y),\qquad\forall x,y\in R^{1}.

(2.24)

Fix
d\in T
. We point out in (
1.13
) that
u(d,d)>0
. Therefore, it follows from the continuity of
u
that for some
\delta>0
and function
b
,

\min_{x,y\in\Delta_{d}(\delta)}u(x,y)=b(d,\delta)>0.

(2.25)

(See (
1.18
).)

We impose the following regularity conditions on the covariance
u
. We assume that for each
d\in T
there exists a continuous increasing function
\varphi_{d}(x)
,
x\geq 0
, such that
\varphi_{d}(0)=0
, and there exists a
\delta>0
, which can depend on
d
, such that,

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle\asymp

\displaystyle\varphi_{d}\left({|x-y|}\right),\qquad\forall x,y\in\Delta_{d}(\delta),\,\,\text{and},

(2.26)

\displaystyle u(x,x)-u(x,y)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle c_{1}\varphi_{d}\left({|x-y|}\right),\qquad\forall x,y\in\Delta_{d}(\delta),\,\,x\geq y,

for some constant
c_{1}>0
, and

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|x|}{\varphi_{d}(x)}=O(1).

(2.27)

(The notation in (
2.26
) indicates that there exist constants,
0<c_{2}\leq c_{3}<\infty
, such that,
c_{2}\varphi\left({|x-y|}\right)\leq\sigma^{2}(x,y)\leq c_{3}\varphi\left({|x-y|}\right)
.)

We generally simply write
\Delta_{d}
or simply
\Delta
instead of
\Delta_{d}(\delta)
and
\varphi
instead of
\varphi_{d}
.

Let
\{f(x),x\in T\}
and
\{g(x),x\in T\}
be finite excessive functions for
\cal Y
. We show in Theorem
1.1
that

u_{g,f}(x,y)=u(x,y)+g(x)f(y),\qquad x,y\in T,

(2.28)

is the kernel of an
\alpha-
permanental process. We now show how to relate path properties of this permenental process to path properties of a mean zero Gaussian process with a covariance that is a minor modification of
u(x,y)
.

For all
n
sufficiently let
\{t^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{m(n)}
be an increasing sequence with

t^{\prime}_{0}=d\geq 0,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{n\to\infty}t^{\prime}_{m(n)}=t^{\prime}_{0}.

(2.29)

Consider the Gaussian sequence

\eta(m(n),n)=\{\eta(t^{\prime}_{j});j=0,1,\ldots,m(n)\},

(2.30)

with covariance,

u(m(n),n):=\{u(x,y);x,y\in t^{\prime}_{0},t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{m(n)}\},

(2.31)

We set,

u(m(n),n)=G(m(n),n)=\{G(m(n),n)_{j,k};j,k=0,1,\ldots,m(n)\}.

(2.32)

and assume that

\sup_{n,m(n)}G(m(n),n)_{j,k}>0,\qquad\forall\,j,k=0,1,\ldots,m(n).

(2.33)

We show in
[
11
, Lemma A.1]
that
(G(m(n),n))^{-1}

exists and is an
M-
matrix. That is, it has positive diagonal entries and off diagonal entries that are less than or equal to 0.

To simplify the notation we write,

t_{j}^{\prime}=d+t_{j},\qquad j=0,1,\ldots,m(n),

(2.34)

and to indicate where we are going we point out that in what follows
we choose
m(n)
so that,

\lim_{n\to\infty}m\left(n\right)=\infty,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{n\to\infty}m\left(n\right)t_{m(n)}=0.

(2.35)

We also require that
f
and
g
have a continuous derivative on
[t^{\prime}_{0},t^{\prime}_{m(n)}]=[d,d+t_{m(n)}]
. If
l
is a function with this property then,

(l^{\prime})^{*}|t_{j}-t_{k}|\leq|l(t^{\prime}_{j})-l(t^{\prime}_{k})|\leq(l^{\prime})^{**}|t_{j}-t_{k}|,

(2.36)

where,

(l^{\prime})^{*}=\inf_{u\in[t^{\prime}_{0},t^{\prime}_{m(n)}]}l^{\prime}(u)\qquad\text{and}\qquad(l^{\prime})^{**}=\sup_{u\in[t^{\prime}_{0},t^{\prime}_{m(n)}]}l^{\prime}(u).

(2.37)

In particular,

l(t^{\prime}_{j})=l(d)+O(t_{m(n)}).

(2.38)

Therefore, both
f
and
g
satisfy (
2.38
).

Consider the
(m(n)+2)\times(m(n)+2)
matrix given by (
2
) with
t_{j}
replaced by
t_{j}^{\prime}
,
m
replaced by
m(n)
and
U_{j,k}
replaced by
G(m(n),n)_{j,k}
. and denote it by
K(m(n),n)
.
(To simplify the notation we often denote
m(n)
by
m
.)
Written out,

\displaystyle K(m,n)

(2.39)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{ cccc }1&f(t^{\prime}_{0})&\ldots&f(t^{\prime}_{m})\\
g(t^{\prime}_{0})&G(m,n)_{0,0}+g(t^{\prime}_{0})f(t^{\prime}_{0})&\ldots&G(m,n)_{0,m}+g(t^{\prime}_{0})f(t^{\prime}_{m})\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
g(t^{\prime}_{n})&G(m,n)_{m,0}+g(t^{\prime}_{n})f(t^{\prime}_{0})&\ldots&G(m,n)_{m,m}+g(t^{\prime}_{n})f(t^{\prime}_{m})\end{array}\right).

As we point out in (
2.15
),

|K(m,n)|=|G(m,n)|>0.

(2.45)

Therefore
K(m,n)
is invertible.
Define.

A(m,n)=K(m,n)^{-1},

(2.46)

and note that,

|A(m,n)|=|G(m,n)|^{-1}.

(2.47)

It follows from (
2.17
) that,

A(m,n)=\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{ cccc }1+\rho_{m,n}&-v_{0}&\dots&-v_{m}\\
-r_{0}&G(m,n)^{0,0}&\dots&G(m,n)^{0,m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-r_{m}&G(m,n)^{m,0}&\dots&G(m,n)^{m,m}\end{array}\!\!\!\right),

(2.48)

where,

r_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{m}G(m,n)^{j,k}g(t^{\prime}_{k}),\quad v_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{m}G(m,n)^{j,k}f(t^{\prime}_{k}),\quad j=0,\ldots,m,

(2.49)

and

\rho_{m,n}=\sum_{j,k=0}^{m}G(m,n)^{j,k}f(t^{\prime}_{j})g(t^{\prime}_{k}).

(2.50)

It is clear from (
2.47
) that
|A(m,n)|
does not depend on
\{f(t^{\prime}_{j}),g(t^{\prime}_{j})\}_{j=0}^{m}
.

By following the proof of Theorem
1.1
, beginning with the second paragraph, we see that

A(m,n)
is an
M
–matrix and
K(m,n)
is the kernel of a permanental process.

Set,

\begin{array}[]{ll }\mathbf{r}=(r_{0},\ldots,r_{m}),&\quad\mathbf{v}=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{m})\\
\mathbf{f}=(f(t^{\prime}_{0}),f(t^{\prime}_{1})\ldots,f(t^{\prime}_{m})),&\quad\mathbf{g}=(g(t^{\prime}_{0}),g(t^{\prime}_{1})\ldots,g(t^{\prime}_{m}))\end{array}

(2.51)

By definition
f(t^{\prime}_{0})=f(d)
and
g(t^{\prime}_{0})=g(d)
. Consequently,

\mathbf{f}=(f(d),f(t^{\prime}_{1})\ldots,f(t^{\prime}_{m})),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{g}=(g(d),g(t^{\prime}_{1})\ldots,g(t^{\prime}_{m})).

(2.52)

We assume that
g(d)
and
f(d)
are strictly greater than 0.

In this notation, using (
2.38
), we can write (
2.50
) as

\rho(m,n)=\sum_{k=0}^{m}v_{k}g(t^{\prime}_{k})=(\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{g})=(\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{1})\left(g(d)+O(t_{m(n)})\right).

(2.53)

where
\mathbf{1}
is a vector with all its components equal to 1.
Similarly,

\rho(m,n)=\sum_{j=0}^{m}r_{j}f(t^{\prime}_{j})=(\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{f})=(\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{1})\left(f(d)+O(t_{m(n)})\right).

(2.54)

Lemma 2.2

\sum_{j=0}^{m}r_{j}=\frac{g(d)}{G(m,n)_{0,0}}+O\left(\varphi(t_{m(n)})\right),

(2.55)

\sum_{j=0}^{m}v_{j}=\frac{f(d)}{G(m,n)_{0,0}}+O\left(\varphi(t_{m(n)})\right),

(2.56)

and

\rho(m,n)=\sum_{j=0}^{m}v_{j}g(t^{\prime}_{j})=\frac{g(d)f(d)}{G(m,n)_{0,0}}+O\left(\varphi(t_{m(n)})\right).

(2.57)

Proof
Consider (
2.48
) as a block matrix. We have,

|A(m,n)|=|G(m,n)|^{-1}\left(1+\rho_{m,n}-\mathbf{v}G(m,n)\mathbf{r}\right).

(2.58)

Therefore, by (
2.47
),

\rho_{m,n}=\mathbf{v}G(m,n)\mathbf{r},

(2.59)

or, equivalently, by (
2.53
)

(\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{g})=\sum_{j=0}^{m}g(t^{\prime}_{j})v_{j}=\mathbf{v}G(m,n)\mathbf{r}.

(2.60)

(This is also easy to verify directly because, since
G(m,n)
is symmetric,
\mathbf{v}G(m,n)\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{r}G(m,n)\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{r}G(m,n)=\mathbf{g}
.)

Let,

G(m,n)_{*}=\min_{j,k\in[0,m]}G(m,n)_{j,k}\text{\,\, and \,\,}G(m,n)_{**}=\max_{j,k\in[0,m]}G(m,n)_{j,k}.

(2.61)

It follows from (
2.60
) that

G(m,n)_{*}\sum_{j=0}^{m}v_{j}\sum_{j=0}^{m}r_{j}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{m}v_{j}g(t^{\prime}_{j})\leq G(m,n)_{**}\sum_{j=0}^{m}v_{j}\sum_{j=0}^{m}r_{j}.

(2.62)

Using (
2.38
) we see that,

\frac{\left(g(d)+O(t_{m(n)})\right)}{G(m,n)_{**}}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{m}r_{j}\leq\frac{\left(g(d)+O(t_{m(n)})\right)}{G(m,n)_{*}}.

(2.63)

The statement in (
2.55
) now follows from Lemma
2.5
and (
2.27
). The proof of the statement in (
2.56
) is similar. Using (
2.54
) and (
2.55
) we get (
2.57
).

Using the fact that
G(m,n)
is symmetric we see that,

\hskip-289.07999ptA(m,n)_{sym}

(2.64)

=\left(\begin{array}[]{ ccccc }1+\rho_{m,n}&-h_{0}&-h_{1}&\dots&-h_{m}\\
-h_{0}&G(m,n)^{0,0}&G(m,n)^{0,1}&\dots&G(m,n)^{0,m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-h_{m}&G(m,n)^{m,0}&G(m,n)^{m,1}&\dots&G(m,n)^{m,m}\end{array}\right),

where,

h_{j}=\left(v_{j}r_{j}\right)^{1/2},\qquad j=0,\ldots,m.

(2.65)

We write
A(m,n)_{sym}
in block form,

A(m,n)_{sym}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ ccccc }1+\rho_{m,n}&-{\bf h}\\
-{\bf h}^{T}&G(m,n)^{-1}\end{array}\right),

(2.66)

where
{\bf h}=(h_{0},h_{1},\ldots,h_{m})
. Then,

|A(m,n)_{sym}|=|G(m,n)^{-1}|\,\,\left(1+\rho_{m,n}-{\bf h}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T}\right).

(2.67)

Using this and (
2.47
) we see that,

\nu_{m,n}={|A(m,n)_{sym}|\over|A(m,n)|}=1+\rho_{m,n}-{\bf h}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T}.

(2.68)

To apply Theorem
2.1
we give conditions under which
\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{m,n}=1
.
We do this in the next two theorems.

Theorem 2.2

If
f,g\in C^{1}(\Delta_{t_{0}^{\prime}})
and,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{0\leq j\neq k\leq m(n)}m(n)|t_{k}-t_{j}|\left(\varphi\left(|t_{k}-t_{j}|\right)\right)^{-1}=0,

(2.69)

and,

t_{m(n)}=o\left(\frac{1}{m(n)}\right),\qquad\text{as $n\to\infty$,}

(2.70)

then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{m,n}=1.

(2.71)

Proof
To obtain (
2.71
), by (
2.68
), we must show that,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\rho_{m,n}-{\bf h}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T}\right)=0,

(2.72)

or equivalently, by (
2.53
), that,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{m}v_{j}g(t^{\prime}_{j})-{\bf h}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T}\right)=0.

(2.73)

(Recall that
m=m(n)
. Also, we note that it follows from
[
12
, Lemma 3.3]
that
\left(\sum_{j=0}^{m}v_{j}g(t^{\prime}_{j})-{\bf h}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T}\right)\geq 0
.

We now find a lower bound for
{\bf h}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T}.
Using the fact that
G(m,n)^{-1}
is an
M
matrix we see that,

\displaystyle g(t^{\prime}_{j})v_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(t^{\prime}_{j})\sum_{k=0}^{m}G(m,n)^{j,k}f(t^{\prime}_{k})

(2.74)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(t^{\prime}_{j})\sum_{k=0}^{m}G(m,n)^{j,k}f(t^{\prime}_{j})-g(t^{\prime}_{j})\sum_{k=0}^{m}|G(m,n)^{j,k}|(f(t^{\prime}_{k})-f(t^{\prime}_{j}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}G(m,n)^{j,k}g(t^{\prime}_{k})f(t^{\prime}_{j})-g(t^{\prime}_{j})\sum_{k=0}^{m}|G(m,n)^{j,k}|(f(t^{\prime}_{k})-f(t^{\prime}_{j}))

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle f(t^{\prime}_{j})\sum_{k=0}^{m}|G(m,n)^{j,k}|(g(t^{\prime}_{k})-g(t^{\prime}_{j}))

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle r_{j}f(t^{\prime}_{j})-\left(g^{**}(f^{\prime})^{**}+f^{**}(g^{\prime})^{**}\right)\sum_{k=0}^{m}|t_{k}-t_{j}||G(m,n)^{j,k}|,

where we use (
2.36
) for the last inequality and
f^{**}
and
g^{**}
are defined similarly to
(f^{\prime})^{**}
and
(g^{\prime})^{**}
in (
2.37
). Using (
2.63
), which, obviously, shows that the terms
r_{j}
are bounded, we have,

r_{j}f(t^{\prime}_{j})\geq r_{j}\left(f(d)-t_{j}(f^{\prime})^{*}\right)\geq r_{j}f(d)-Ct_{m(n)}(f^{\prime})^{*},

(2.75)

for some constant
C>0
.

It follows from (
2.26
) that

G(m(n),n)_{j,j}-G(m(n),n)_{j,k}\geq C\varphi\left(|t_{j}-t_{k}|\right),\qquad\forall j\geq k,\,

(2.76)

for some constant
C>0
. Therefore we can use
Lemma
2.7
below to see that,

\sum_{k=0}^{m}|t_{k}-t_{j}||G(m,n)^{j,k}|=o\left(\frac{1}{m(n)}\right).

(2.77)

Consequently,

g(t^{\prime}_{j})v_{j}\geq r_{j}f(d)+o\left(\frac{1}{m(n)}\right)-Ct_{m(n)}(f^{\prime})^{*}.

(2.78)

Using (
2.70
) we get,

g(t^{\prime}_{j})v_{j}\geq r_{j}f(d)+o\left(\frac{1}{m(n)}\right).

(2.79)

Finally, using (
2.38
) and the hypothesis that
g(d)>0
we have,

v_{j}\geq r_{j}\frac{f(d)}{g(d)}+o\left(\frac{1}{m(n)}\right).

(2.80)

We need a lower bound for
v_{j}^{1/2}
. This is a subtle point so it is useful to get a more precise description of the term
o\left({1}/{m(n)}\right)
in (
2.80
). We write (
2.78
) as,

v_{j}\geq r_{j}\frac{f(d)}{g(d)}+\frac{\epsilon(n,j)}{m(n)},

(2.81)

where
\lim_{n\to\infty}\epsilon(n,j)=0.
If
\epsilon(n,j)\geq 0
,
v_{j}^{1/2}\geq(r_{j}f(d)/g(d))^{1/2}
. This is the case when
r_{j}=0
because
v_{j}\geq 0.

If
\epsilon(n,j)<0
and
r_{j}>0
,

v_{j}^{1/2}\geq\left(r_{j}\frac{f(d)}{g(d)}-\frac{|\epsilon(n,j)|}{m(n)}\right)^{1/2}\geq\left(r_{j}\frac{f(d)}{g(d)}\right)^{1/2}-\left(\frac{|\epsilon(n,j)|}{m(n)}\right)^{1/2}.

(2.82)

where we use the fact that
v_{j}\geq 0
.
This implies that,

\sum_{j=0}^{m(n)}\left(r_{j}v_{j}\right)^{1/2}\geq\sum_{j=0}^{m(n)}r_{j}\,\left(\frac{f(d)}{g(d)}\right)^{1/2}-\left(\frac{|\epsilon(n,j)|}{m(n)}\right)^{1/2}\,\,\sum_{j=0}^{m(n)}r_{j}^{1/2}.

(2.83)

Using the inequality,

\sum_{j=0}^{m(n)}r_{j}^{1/2}\leq m^{1/2}(n)\left(\sum_{j=0}^{m(n)}r_{j}\right)^{1/2},

(2.84)

and Lemma
2.2
, which shows that
\sum_{j=0}^{m(n)}r_{j}<2g(d)/G(m,n)_{0,0}:=C
, for all
n
sufficiently large, we see that,

\sum_{j=0}^{m(n)}\left(r_{j}v_{j}\right)^{1/2}\geq\sum_{j=0}^{m(n)}r_{j}\,\left(\frac{f(d)}{g(d)}\right)^{1/2}-C|\epsilon^{*}(n)|^{1/2},

(2.85)

where
\epsilon^{*}(n)=\sup_{j}\epsilon(n,j)
. Note that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\epsilon^{*}(n)=0
.

We use (
2.85
) to find a lower bound for
{\bf h}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T}
in (
2.67
).
It follows from
Lemma
2.4
that,

G(m,n)_{j,k}=G(m,n)_{0,0}+O\left(\varphi\left(t_{m(n)}\right)\right).

(2.86)

Therefore, by Lemma
2.4

\displaystyle{\bf h}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k=0}^{m(n)}(r_{j}v_{j})^{1/2}G(m,n)_{j,k}(r_{k}v_{k})^{1/2}

(2.87)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{m(n)}(r_{j}v_{j})^{1/2}\right)^{2}\left(G(m,n)_{0,0}+O\left(\varphi\left(t_{m(n)}\right)\right)\right).

It follows from (
2.85
) that,

\left(\sum_{j=0}^{m(n)}(r_{j}v_{j})^{1/2}\right)^{2}\geq\left(\sum_{j=0}^{m(n)}r_{j}\right)^{2}\frac{f(d)}{g(d)}+o_{n}(1).

(2.88)

Consequently,

\displaystyle{\bf h}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(m,n)_{0,0}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{m(n)}r_{j}\right)^{2}\frac{f(d)}{g(d)}+o_{n}(1)

(2.89)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{g(d)f(d)}{G(m,n)_{0,0}}+O\left(\varphi(t_{m(n)})\right)+o_{n}(1),

where we use (
2.55
). Therefore, by (
2.57
),

{\bf h}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T}=\rho_{m,n}+O\left(\varphi(t_{m(n)})\right)+o_{n}(1),

(2.90)

which gives (
2.72
).

In the next theorem we do not require (
2.69
), but we
impose more stringent conditions on the excessive functions
f
and
g
.

Theorem 2.3

When
f,g\in C^{2}(\Delta_{d}(\delta))
, for some
\delta>0
and
f^{\prime}(d)=g^{\prime}(d)=0
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{m,n}=1.

(2.91)

Proof
Follow the proof of Theorem
2.2
through (
2.73
). We now modify (
2.74
) to take account of the fact that
f^{\prime}(d)=0
. By Taylor’s Theorem,

f(t^{\prime}_{j})-f(t^{\prime}_{k})=f^{\prime}(t^{\prime}_{k})\left(t_{j}-t_{k}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{t^{\prime}_{j}}^{t^{\prime}_{k}}f^{\prime\prime}(s)(t^{\prime}_{k}-s)\,ds.

(2.92)

Since
f^{\prime}(d)=0
,

f^{\prime}(t^{\prime}_{k})=\int_{d}^{t^{\prime}_{k}}f^{\prime\prime}(s)\,ds,

(2.93)

so that

|f(t^{\prime}_{j})-f(t^{\prime}_{k})|\leq 2(f^{\prime\prime})^{*}\,\,t_{m(n)}|t_{j}-t_{k}|,\quad\text{where}\quad(f^{\prime\prime})^{*}=\sup_{u\in[d,d+t_{m(n)}]}|f^{\prime\prime}(u)|,

(2.94)

where we take
n
large enough so that
t_{m(n)}<\delta
.

Using Lemma
2.8
we see that,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}|G(m,n)^{j,k}|f(t^{\prime}_{k})-f(t^{\prime}_{j})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle O\left(t_{m(n)}\sum_{k=0}^{m(n)}|t_{j}-t_{k}||G(m,n)^{j,k}|\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle O\left(t_{m(n)}\right).

Similarly,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}|G(m,n)^{j,k}|g(t^{\prime}_{k})-g(t^{\prime}_{j})|\leq O\left(t_{m(n)}\right).

(2.95)

Using these last two inequalities in (
2.74
) and then using (
2.75
)
we get,

g(t^{\prime}_{j})v_{j}\geq r_{j}f(d)+O(t_{m}(n)).

(2.96)

It follows from (
2.35
) that (
2.79
) holds.
The rest of the proof proceeds exactly as in the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

To apply Theorem
2.1
we must find
K(m,n)_{isymi}
. By (
2.66
) and the formula for the inverse of
A(m,n)_{sym}
we see that,

\displaystyle\hskip-28.90755ptK(m,n)_{isymi}

(2.97)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{ ccccc }\nu^{-1}_{m,n}&\nu^{-1}_{m,n}{\bf h}G(m,n)\\
\nu^{-1}_{m,n}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T}&G(m,n)+\nu^{-1}_{m,n}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T}{\bf h}G(m,n)\end{array}\right).

For
i,j=0,1,\ldots,m
set,

(\nu^{-1}_{m,n}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T}{\bf h}G(m,n))_{i,j}=a(m,n)_{i}a(m,n)_{j},

(2.101)

where,

a(m,n)_{j}=\nu^{-1/2}_{m,n}\left({\bf h}G(m,n)\right)_{j}.

(2.102)

(We use the fact that
G(m,n)

is symmetric.)

Note that,

\{(K(m,n)_{isymi})_{j,k};0\leq j,k\leq m\}

(2.103)

is the covariance of the Gaussian sequence,

\widetilde{\eta}=\{\eta(t^{\prime}_{j})+a(m,n)_{j}\,\xi;0\leq j,k\leq m\},

(2.104)

where
\{\eta(t^{\prime}_{j});j=0,\ldots,m\}
is a mean zero Gaussian process with covariance
G(m,n)
and
\xi
is an independent standard normal random variable. Furthermore,

0<a(m,n)_{j}\leq f^{1/2}(d)g^{1/2}(d)+O\left(\varphi^{1/2}\left(t_{m(n)}\right)\right),

(2.105)

and,

|a(m,n)_{j}-a(m,n)_{k}|\leq\frac{f^{1/2}(d)g^{1/2}(d)}{G(m,n)_{0,0}}O\left(\varphi^{1/2}\left(|t_{j}-t_{k}|\right)\right).

(2.106)

To verify (
2.105
) note that,
by the Schwartz Inequality, Lemma
2.2
and (
2.25
),

\sum_{k=0}^{m(n)}h_{k}\leq\left(\sum_{k=0}^{m(n)}r_{k}\sum_{k=0}^{m(n)}v_{k}\right)^{1/2}\leq\frac{f^{1/2}(d)g^{1/2}(d)}{G(m,n)_{0,0}}+O\left(\varphi^{1/2}\left(t_{m(n)}\right)\right).

(2.107)

Using this, Lemma
2.5
and the fact that
\nu_{m,n}\geq 1
we get (
2.105
).

For (
2.106
) we have,

|\left({\bf h}G(m,n)\right)_{j}-\left({\bf h}G(m,n)\right)_{k}|\leq\sum_{i=0}^{m}h_{i}|G(m,n)_{i,j}-G(m,n)_{i,k}|.

(2.108)

Note that by (
2.26
),

\displaystyle|G(m,n)_{i,j}-G(m,n)_{i,k}|=E(\eta_{i}(\eta_{j}-\eta_{k})|

(2.109)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\left(E(\eta^{2}_{i})E(\eta_{j}-\eta_{k})^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq\sup_{0\leq i\leq m}\left(E(\eta^{2}_{i})\right)^{1/2}\varphi^{1/2}(|t_{j}-t_{k}|).

Consequently, using (
2.107
), we see that,

\displaystyle\!|\left({\bf h}G(m,n)\right)_{j}-\left({\bf h}G(m,n)\right)_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\!\!\sup_{0\leq i\leq m}\left(E(\eta^{2}_{i})\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{m}h_{i}\right)\varphi^{1/2}(|t_{j}-t_{k}|)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\!\!\sup_{0\leq i\leq m}\left(E(\eta^{2}_{i})\right)^{1/2}\frac{f^{1/2}(d)g^{1/2}(d)}{G(m,n)_{0,0}}\varphi^{1/2}(|t_{j}-t_{k}|).

Since
\sup_{0\leq i\leq m}\left(E(\eta^{2}_{i})\right)^{1/2}
is bounded we get (
2.106
).

The matrix
K(m,n)_{isymi}
is an
(m+2)\times(m+2)
matrix which we can write as

\{(K(m,n)_{isymi})_{i,j};i,j=-1,0,1,\ldots,m\}
. Define,

\overline{{\cal K}}(m,n)=\{(K(m,n)_{isymi})_{i,j};i,j=0,1,\ldots,m\}.

(2.111)

As we point out in (
2.103
) and (
2.104
) this is the covariance of the Gaussian process,

\{\eta(t_{j})+a(m,n)_{j}\,\xi;j=0,\ldots,m\},

(2.112)

where
\{\eta(t_{j});j=0,\ldots,m\}
is a mean zero Gaussian process with covariance
G(m,n)
and
\xi
is an independent standard normal random variable.

We also consider,

{\cal K}(m,n)=\{K(m,n)_{i,j};i,j=0,1,\ldots,m\},

(2.113)

in (
2.39
).
The next lemma is a restatement of Theorem
2.1
for the
\alpha
–permanental random variables with kernels
{\cal K}_{(m,n)}
and
\overline{{\cal K}}_{(m,n)}
.

Lemma 2.3

Let
\widehat{X}_{\alpha,(m,n)}=\{\widehat{X}_{\alpha,(m,n)}(t^{\prime}_{j}),j=0,1,\ldots m\}
be an
\alpha
–permanental random variable with kernel
{\cal K}(m,n)
and
Y_{\alpha,(m,n)}=\{Y_{\alpha,(m,n)}(t^{\prime}_{j}),j=0,1,\ldots m\}
be an
\alpha
-permanental random variable with kernel
\overline{{\cal K}}(m,n)
. Then for all functions
g_{n}
of
\widehat{X}_{\alpha,(m,n)}
and
Y_{\alpha,(m,n)}
and sets
{\cal B}_{n}
in the range of
g_{n}
,

\displaystyle\nu_{n,m}^{-\alpha}P\left(g_{n}(Y_{\alpha,(m,n)})\in{\cal B}_{n}\right)\leq P\left(g_{n}(\widehat{X}_{\alpha,(m,n)})\in{\cal B}_{n}\right)

(2.114)

\displaystyle\leq 1-\nu_{n,m}^{-\alpha}+\nu_{n,m}^{-\alpha}P\left(g_{n}(Y_{\alpha,(m,n)})\in{\cal B}_{n}\right).

Proof
We use Theorem
2.1
and (
2.68
) with
\alpha
–permanental processes determined by the kernels
K(m,n)
and
K(m,n)_{isymi}
.

Remark 2.1

It is clear from the properties of
{\cal K}(m,n)
and the Kolmogorov extension theorem that there exists an
\alpha
-permanental process
\widehat{X}_{\alpha}=\{\widehat{X}_{\alpha}(t),t\in[d,d+\delta]\}
such that
\{\widehat{X}_{\alpha}(t^{\prime}_{j}),j=0,1,\ldots m\}=\{\widehat{X}_{\alpha,(m,n)}(t^{\prime}_{j}),j=0,1,\ldots m\}
. The process
\widehat{X}_{\alpha}
is the
\alpha
–permanental process mentioned in the paragraph containing (
2.28
), where it is referred to as
X_{\alpha}
, with kernel

\{u_{g,f}(x,y)
,
x,y\in T\}
. In that paragraph we said that we would relate path properties of
X_{\alpha}
to path properties of a mean zero Gaussian process with a covariance that is a minor modification of
u(x,y)
. This is what is done in Lemma
2.3
.

2.1
Some basic calculations

Recall that,

G(m,n)_{*}=\min_{j,k\in[0,m]}G(m,n)_{j,k}\,\text{ and }\,G(m,n)_{**}=\max_{j,k\in[0,m]}G(m,n)_{j,k}.

(2.115)

We often abbreviate
G(m,n)
by just writing
G
.

Lemma 2.4

G_{**}-G_{*}=O\left(\varphi\left(t_{m}\right)\right).

(2.116)

Proof
The matrix
\{G_{j,k}\}_{j,k=0}^{m}
has a finite number of terms, consequently the values
G_{**}
and
G_{*}
are achieved by some of the terms, and since
G_{j,k}\leq G_{j,j}\wedge G_{k,k}
, the value
G_{**}
is achieved by at least one of the diagonal terms. Therefore, there are terms such that,

0\leq G_{**}-G_{*}=G_{j,j}-G_{k,l}.

(2.117)

We note that,

G_{j,j}-G_{k,l}=\left(G_{j,j}-G_{j,k}\right)+\left(G_{j,k}-G_{k,l}\right).

(2.118)

It follows from (
2.26
) that for all
t_{m(n)}
sufficiently small,

G_{j,j}-G_{j,k}\leq c_{2}\varphi(|t_{j}-t_{k}|)\leq c_{2}\varphi(t_{m(n)}),

(2.119)

for some constant
c_{2}
.
In addition, by (
1.12
),

G_{j,k}-G_{k,l}\leq G_{k,k}-G_{k,l}\leq c_{2}\varphi(|t_{k}-t_{l}|)\leq c_{2}\varphi(t_{m}),

(2.120)

so we get (
2.116
).

Lemma 2.5

For all
\{t_{j}\}_{j=0}^{{m(n)}}
,

\frac{1}{G(m,n)_{j,k}}=\frac{1}{G(m,n)_{0,0}}+O\left(\varphi\left(t_{m(n)}\right)\right),\qquad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty.

(2.121)

Proof
Write,

\frac{1}{G_{j,k}}=\frac{1}{G_{0,0}}+\frac{G_{0,0}-G_{j,k}}{G_{j,k}G_{0,0}}.

(2.122)

This implies that,

\frac{1}{G_{0,0}}-\frac{G_{**}-G_{*}}{G_{j,k}G_{0,0}}\leq\frac{1}{G_{j,k}}\leq\frac{1}{G_{0,0}}+\frac{G_{**}-G_{*}}{G_{j,k}G_{0,0}},

(2.123)

Using (
2.116
) and (
2.25
) we get (
2.121
).

Lemma 2.6

When

\left(G\left(m,n\right)_{j,j}-G\left(m,n\right)_{j,k}\right)\geq\frac{2}{C}\varphi\left(|t_{j}-t_{k}|\right),\quad\forall\,k<j,\,\,j,k=0,\ldots,m(n)

(2.124)

for some
C>0
and all
n
sufficiently large it follows that,

\sum_{k=0}^{m(n)}\big|G\left(m,n\right)^{k,j}\big|\,\varphi\left(|t_{j}-t_{k}|\right)\leq C,\quad\forall\,\,j,k=0,\ldots,m(n),

(2.125)

and all
n
sufficiently large.

Proof
We have,

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}G\left(m,n\right)_{j,k}G\left(m,n\right)^{k,j}

(2.126)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\left(m,n\right)_{j,j}G\left(m,n\right)^{j,j}-\sum_{k=0,k\neq j}^{m}G\left(m,n\right)_{j,k}|G\left(m,n\right)^{k,j}|

Continuing this,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\left(m,n\right)_{j,j}\left(G\left(m,n\right)^{j,j}-\sum_{k=0,k\neq j}^{m}|G\left(m,n\right)^{k,j}|\right)

(2.127)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{k=0}^{m}|G\left(m,n\right)^{k,j}|\left(G\left(m,n\right)_{j,j}-G\left(m,n\right)_{j,k}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\left(m,n\right)_{j,j}r_{j}+\sum_{k=0}^{m}|G\left(m,n\right)^{k,j}|\left(G\left(m,n\right)_{j,j}-G\left(m,n\right)_{j,k}\right).

Consequently,

\sum_{k=0}^{m}|G\left(m,n\right)^{k,j}|\left(G\left(m,n\right)_{j,j}-G\left(m,n\right)_{j,k}\right)\leq 1.

(2.128)

Furthermore, since
\left(G\left(m,n\right)_{j,j}-G\left(m,n\right)_{j,k}\right)\geq 0
for all
j,k
it follows that

\sum_{k<j}|G\left(m,n\right)^{k,j}|\left(G\left(m,n\right)_{j,j}-G\left(m,n\right)_{j,k}\right)\leq 1.

(2.129)

Using (
2.124
) we see that

\sum_{k<j}\big|G\left(m,n\right)^{k,j}\big|\,\varphi\left(|t_{j}-t_{k}|\right)\leq C/2.

(2.130)

This is true for all
j
, so in particular, setting
j=k
, we have,

\sum_{i<k}\big|G\left(m,n\right)^{i,k}\big|\,\varphi\left(|t_{k}-t_{i}|\right)\leq C/2,

(2.131)

or, equivalently,

\sum_{j<k}\big|G\left(m,n\right)^{j,k}\big|\,\varphi\left(|t_{k}-t_{j}|\right)\leq C/2.

(2.132)

Since
\{G\left(m,n\right)^{j,k}\}_{j,k=0,\ldots,m(n)}
is symmetric this shows that,

\sum_{j<k}\big|G\left(m,n\right)^{k,j}\big|\,\varphi\left(|t_{j}-t_{k}|\right)\leq C/2.

(2.133)

combining (
2.130
) and (
2.133
) gives (
2.125
).

Recall the condition in (
2.69
),

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{0\leq j\neq k\leq m(n)}m(n)|t_{k}-t_{j}|\left(\varphi\left(|t_{k}-t_{j}|\right)\right)^{-1}=0.

(2.134)

Lemma 2.7

When (
2.124
) and (
2.134
) hold,

\sum_{k=0}^{m(n)}|t_{k}-t_{j}||G(m,n)^{j,k}|=o\left(\frac{1}{m(n)}\right).

(2.135)

Proof
Using Lemma
2.6
and (
2.134
) we get,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m(n)}|t_{k}-t_{j}||G(m,n)^{j,k}|

(2.136)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{m(n)}\sum_{k=0}^{m(n)}|G(m,n)^{j,k}|\varphi\left(|t_{k}-t_{j}|\right)\left\{m(n)|t_{k}-t_{j}|\left(\varphi\left(|t_{k}-t_{j}|\right)\right)^{-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{m(n)}\sup_{0\leq j,k\leq m(n)}m(n)|t_{k}-t_{j}|\left(\varphi\left(|t_{k}-t_{j}|\right)\right)^{-1}=o\left(\frac{1}{m(n)}\right).

The next lemma gives an estimate that is weaker than (
2.135
) but, significantly, it does not require (
2.134
):

Lemma 2.8

\sum_{k=0}^{m(n)}|t_{k}-t_{j}||G(m,n)^{j,k}|=O\left(1\right).

(2.137)

Proof
We have,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m(n)}|t_{k}-t_{j}||G(m,n)^{j,k}|

(2.138)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m(n)}|G(m,n)^{j,k}|\varphi\left(|t_{k}-t_{j}|\right)\left\{|t_{k}-t_{j}|\left(\varphi\left(|t_{k}-t_{j}|\right)\right)^{-1}\right\}.

It follows from (
2.125
) and (
2.27
) that this last term is
O(1)
.

3
Lower bounds for the LIL for
\mathbf{k/2}
permanental processes

Upper bounds for the LILs in Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
are given in
[
13
, Theorem 1.2]
. In this paper we show that they are best possible by finding the lower bounds. We then say that the moduli are exact. I.e., Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
give exact local moduli of continuity for the permanental processes they consider.

The next theorem is a general result for the lower bound of the local modulus of continuity for certain permanental processes. It is used in Section
4
to find lower bounds in Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
.

Theorem 3.1

Let
\cal Y
,
u
,
\eta
and
\sigma^{2}(x,y)
be as described on page
2

and consider the permanental processes
X_{k/2}=\{X_{k/2}(t),t\in T\}
,
k\geq 1
, determined by kernels

u_{g,f}(x,y)=u(x,y)+g(x)f(y),\qquad x,y\in T,

(3.1)

where
g,f
are excessive functions for
\cal Y
.
Fix
d\in T
and assume that
u(d,d)>0
and
g,f\in C^{1}(\Delta_{d}(\delta))
for some
\delta>0
; see (
1.18
).
Furthermore, assume there exists a function that
\varphi_{d}
that satisfies (
2.26
) and (
2.27
) with respect to
\sigma^{2}(x,y)

For
\theta<1
let,

t_{0}=0\hskip 7.22743pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 7.22743ptt_{j}=\theta^{n+1-j},\,\,j=1,\ldots,m(n),

(3.2)

and,

m(n)=n+1-[n^{q}]\text{ for some $0<q<1$}.

(3.3)

Set

t^{\prime}_{j}=d+t_{j},\,\,j=0,1,\ldots,m(n).

(3.4)

Assume that for all
\epsilon>0
,

P\left(\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{\eta(t^{\prime}_{j})-\eta(d)}{(2\sigma^{2}(t^{\prime}_{j},d)\log\log 1/t_{j})^{1/2}}\geq(1-\epsilon)\right)\geq(1-\epsilon),

(3.5)

for all
n
sufficiently large, and that one, or both, of the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{0\leq j\neq k\leq m(n)}m(n)|t_{k}-t_{j}|\left(\varphi_{d}\left(|t_{k}-t_{j}|\right)\right)^{-1}=0;

(3.6)

(ii)

g,f\in C^{2}(\Delta_{d})
and
g^{\prime}(d)=f^{\prime}(d)=0
.

Then
for all integers
k\geq 1
,

\limsup_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{|X_{k/2}(d+t)-X_{k/2}(d)|}{\left(2\sigma^{2}(d+t,d)\log\log 1/t\right)^{1/2}}\geq{\sqrt{2}}X^{1/2}_{k/2}(d)\qquad a.s.

(3.7)

This also holds for
\limsup_{t\uparrow 0}
.

Proof of Theorem
3.1

We use Theorem
2.1
to find sample path properties of permanental processes with kernels that are not symmetric in terms of permanental processes with symmetric kernels. Since the later are chi-square processes they can be analyzed as squares of Gaussian processes using well known techniques. To get almost sure results we must show that,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{m,n}=1.

(3.8)

(See (
2.68
).) When condition
(i)
is satisfied Theorem
2.2
shows that (
3.8
) holds. When condition
(ii)
is satisfied Theorem
2.3
shows that (
3.8
) holds. (These are the only uses of conditions
(i)
and
(ii)
in the proof.)

We proceed with the proof of this theorem:
Set

T_{n}=\{t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{m(n)}\}=\{0,\theta^{n},\theta^{n-1},\ldots,\theta^{[n^{q}]}\},

(3.9)

and

T^{\prime}_{n}=d+T_{n}:=\{d,t^{\prime}_{1},t^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{m(n)}\}.

(3.10)

Also, excluding the initial element
d
, we set,

T_{n,1}=\{t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{m(n)}\},\qquad\text{and}\qquad T^{\prime}_{n,1}=\{t^{\prime}_{1},t^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{m(n)}\}.

(3.11)

We use Lemma
2.3
to get probability estimates for
X_{k/2,(m,n)}=\{X_{k/2}(t),t\in T^{\prime}_{n}\}
, in terms of a chi-square process of order
k
that is determined by a Gaussian sequence with covariance
\overline{{\cal K}}_{(m,n)}
in (
2.111
). Specifically,

(\overline{{\cal K}}_{(m,n)})_{j,k}=G(m,n)_{j,k}+a(m,n)_{{j}}\,\,a(m,n)_{k}:=\overline{u}(t^{\prime}_{j},t^{\prime}_{k}),

(3.12)

where,

G(m(n),n)_{j,k}=u(t^{\prime}_{j},t^{\prime}_{k}).

(3.13)

(See (
2.32
).)
This is the covariance of the Gaussian sequence
\overline{\eta}=\{\overline{\eta}(t^{\prime}_{j});t^{\prime}_{j}\in T^{\prime}_{n}\}
where,

\overline{\eta}({t^{\prime}_{j}})=\eta({t^{\prime}_{j}})+a(m,n)_{j}\,\xi,\qquad j=0,1,\ldots,m,

(3.14)

where

\xi
is an independent standard normal random variable.

Let
\overline{u}
denote the covariance of
\overline{\eta}
. It follows from (
2.105
) that
\overline{u}(d,d)>0.
Using this, (
2.26
), (
2.105
) and (
2.106
) we see that,

\frac{\overline{u}(d,d)-\overline{u}(d,t^{\prime}_{j})}{\overline{u}(d,d)}\leq C\varphi^{1/2}\left(t_{j}\right),

(3.15)

for some constant
C.
Let

\psi_{d}(t)=\left(2\sigma^{2}(d+t,d)\log\log 1/t\right)^{1/2}.

(3.16)

It follows from (
2.27
) that,

\frac{\varphi_{d}^{1/2}\left(t\right)}{\psi_{d}(t)}\to 0,\qquad\mbox{ as }t\to 0.

(3.17)

(We often write
\varphi_{d}
and
\psi_{d}
simply as
\varphi
and
\psi
.)

Let
\{\overline{\eta}_{i},i=1,\ldots,k\}
be independent copies of the Gaussian sequence
\overline{\eta}
.
Consider the chi-square process of order
k

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(t^{\prime}),\qquad t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n}.

(3.18)

Note that,

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}_{i}^{2}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}_{i}^{2}(d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\overline{\eta}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}_{i}(d))(\overline{\eta}_{i}(t^{\prime})+\overline{\eta}_{i}(d))

(3.19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\overline{\eta}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}_{i}(d))(2\overline{\eta}_{i}(d)+(\overline{\eta}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}_{i}(d)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(\overline{\eta}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}_{i}(d))\overline{\eta}_{i}(d)+(\overline{\eta}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}_{i}(d))^{2}.

Consequently,

\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(d))\over 2\psi(t)}\geq\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\overline{\eta}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}_{i}(d))\overline{\eta}_{i}(d)\over\psi(t)},

(3.20)

where, by (
3.10
),

t=t^{\prime}-d.

(3.21)

We write this as,

\displaystyle\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(d))\over 2\psi(t)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\overline{\eta}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\frac{\overline{u}(d,t^{\prime})}{\overline{u}(d,d)}\overline{\eta}_{i}(d)\right)\overline{\eta}_{i}(d)\over\psi(t)}

(3.22)

\displaystyle\,\,-\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\frac{\overline{u}(d,d)-\overline{u}(d,t^{\prime})}{\overline{u}(d,d)}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(d)\over\psi(t)}.

Set,

\delta_{m,n}=\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}\frac{\overline{u}(d,d)-\overline{u}(d,t^{\prime})}{\overline{u}(d,d)\psi(t)},

(3.23)

and note that by (
3.15
) and (
3.17
),

\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{m,n}=0.

(3.24)

With this notation we rewrite (
3.22
) as,

\displaystyle\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(d))\over 2\psi(t)\|\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)\|_{2}}+\delta_{m,n}\|\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)\|_{2}

(3.25)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\overline{\eta}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\frac{\overline{u}(d,t^{\prime})}{\overline{u}(d,d)}\overline{\eta}_{i}(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}\frac{\overline{\eta}_{i}(d)}{\|\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)\|_{2}},

where for
k
dimensional Gaussian vectors we use the notation,
\vec{\overline{\eta}}(t)=\vec{\overline{\eta}}_{k}(t)=(\overline{\eta}_{1}(t),\ldots,\overline{\eta}_{k}(t))
, for
t\in R^{1}.

The next step in the proof is the critical observation. We give it as a lemma.

Lemma 3.1

For all
t^{\prime}\in R^{1}
,

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\overline{\eta}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\frac{\overline{u}(d,t^{\prime})}{\overline{u}(d,d)}\overline{\eta}_{i}(d)\right)\frac{\overline{\eta}_{i}(d)}{\|\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)\|_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle law}}{{=}}\left(\overline{\eta}(t^{\prime})-\frac{\overline{u}(d,t^{\prime})}{\overline{u}(d,d)}\overline{\eta}(d)\right).

(3.26)

Proof
Let
\{\xi_{i}(t^{\prime}),t^{\prime}\in T_{n}\}
,
i=1,\ldots,k
, be independent copies of a mean zero Gaussian process
\{\xi(t^{\prime}),t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n}\}
, and set
\vec{\xi}(t^{\prime})=(\xi_{1}(t^{\prime}),\ldots,\xi_{k}(t^{\prime}))
. Let
\vec{v}\in R^{k}
with
\|\vec{v}\|_{2}=1
. Computing the covariances we see that,

\{\vec{\xi}(t^{\prime})\cdot\vec{v}),t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle law}}{{=}}\{\xi(t^{\prime}),t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n}\}.

(3.27)

Therefore, since

\left(\overline{\eta}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\frac{\overline{u}(d,t^{\prime})}{\overline{u}(d,d)}\overline{\eta}_{i}(d)\right)
and
\overline{\eta}_{i}(d)
are independent for
i=1,\ldots,k
, we see that,

\displaystyle\Big\{\left(\vec{\overline{\eta}}(t^{\prime})-\frac{\overline{u}(d,t^{\prime})}{\overline{u}(d,d)}\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)\right)\cdot\frac{\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)}{\|\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)\|_{2}},t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n}\Big\}

(3.28)

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle law}}{{=}}\Big\{\left(\overline{\eta}(t^{\prime})-\frac{\overline{u}(d,t^{\prime})}{\overline{u}(d,d)}\overline{\eta}(d)\right),t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n}\Big\}.

This gives(
3.26
)

It follows from (
3.26
) that the second line of (
3.25
) is equal in law to

\displaystyle\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\overline{\eta}(t^{\prime})-\frac{\overline{u}(d,t^{\prime})}{\overline{u}(d,d)}\overline{\eta}(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}\left({\left(\overline{\eta}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}+{\overline{u}(d,d)-\overline{u}(d,t^{\prime})\over\overline{u}(d,d)\psi(t)}\overline{\eta}(d)\right)

(3.29)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\overline{\eta}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}-\delta_{m,n}|\overline{\eta}(d)|.

Therefore, using (
3.25
) and (
3.29
) we see that for any
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(d))\over 2\psi(t)\|\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)\|_{2}}\geq 1-\epsilon-\delta_{m,n}\|\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)\|_{2}\right)

(3.30)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\overline{\eta}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}\geq 1-\epsilon-\delta_{m,n}|\overline{\eta}(d)|\right).

Set,

P\left(\delta_{m,n}\|\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)\|_{2}\leq\epsilon/2\right)=1-\epsilon(n).

(3.31)

Then conditioning on the event
\{\delta_{m,n}\|\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)\|_{2}\leq\epsilon/2\}
we see that the left-hand side of (
3.30
),

\leq P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(d))\over 2\psi(t)\|\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)\|_{2}}\geq 1-\epsilon/2\right)(1-\epsilon(n))+\epsilon(n).

(3.32)

Therefore,

\displaystyle P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(d))\over 2\psi(t)\|\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)\|_{2}}\geq 1-\epsilon/2\right)

(3.33)

\displaystyle\quad\geq P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\overline{\eta}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}\geq 1-\epsilon-\delta_{m,n}|\overline{\eta}(d)|\right)-\frac{\epsilon(n)}{1-\epsilon(n)}.

By (
3.14
) for all
t^{\prime}_{j}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}
,

\frac{\overline{\eta}(t^{\prime}_{j})-\overline{\eta}(d)}{\psi(t_{j})}=\frac{\eta(t^{\prime}_{j})-\eta(d)}{\psi(t_{j})}+\frac{a(m,n)_{j}-a(m,n)_{0}}{\psi(t_{j})}\xi.

(3.34)

Consequently,

\displaystyle P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\overline{\eta}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}\geq 1-\epsilon-\delta_{m,n}|\overline{\eta}(d)|\right)

(3.35)
