\displaystyle\geq P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\eta(t^{\prime})-\eta(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}\geq 1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}-\delta_{m,n}|\overline{\eta}(d)|,\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\left.\sup_{1\leq j\leq m}\frac{|a(m,n)_{{j}}-a(m,n)_{0}|}{\psi(t_{j})}|\xi|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad=P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\eta(t^{\prime})-\eta(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}\geq 1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}-\delta_{m,n}|\overline{\eta}(d)|\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 65.04256ptP\left(\sup_{1\leq j\leq m}\frac{|a(m,n)_{{j}}-a(m,n)_{0}|}{\psi(t_{j})}|\xi|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right).

By (
2.106
) and (
3.17
), for all
n
sufficiently large,

\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{|a(m,n)_{{j}}-a(m,n)_{0}|}{\psi(t_{j})}|\xi|\leq\delta^{\prime}_{m,n}|\xi|,

(3.36)

Set,

P\left(\delta^{\prime}_{m,n}|\xi|\leq\epsilon/2\right)=1-\epsilon^{\prime}(n),

(3.37)

so that,

P\left(\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{|a(m,n)_{{j}}-a(m,n)_{0}|}{\psi(t_{j})}|\xi|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)=1-\epsilon^{\prime}(n).

(3.38)

Combining (
3.34
)–(
3.38
) we have,

\displaystyle P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\overline{\eta}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}\geq 1-\epsilon-\delta_{m,n}|\overline{\eta}(d)|\right)

(3.39)

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\epsilon^{\prime}(n)\right)P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\eta(t^{\prime})-\eta(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}\geq 1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}-\delta_{m,n}|\overline{\eta}(d)|\right).

Now set,

P\left(\delta_{m,n}|\overline{\eta}(d)|\leq\epsilon/2\right)=1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime}(n).

(3.40)

This gives,

\displaystyle P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\eta(t^{\prime})-\eta(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}\geq 1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}-\delta_{m,n}|\overline{\eta}(d)|\right)

(3.41)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\eta(t^{\prime})-\eta(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}\geq 1-\epsilon\right)\left(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime}(n)\right).

Using this in (
3.39
) we get,

\displaystyle P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\overline{\eta}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}\geq 1-\epsilon-\delta_{m,n}|\overline{\eta}(d)|\right)

(3.42)

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-(\epsilon^{\prime}(n)+\epsilon^{\prime\prime}(n)\right)P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\eta(t^{\prime})-\eta(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}\geq 1-\epsilon\right),

Using this and (
3.33
) we have,

\displaystyle P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(d))\over 2\psi(t)\|\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)\|_{2}}\geq 1-\epsilon/2\right)

(3.43)

\displaystyle\quad\geq\left(1-(\epsilon^{\prime}(n)+\epsilon^{\prime\prime}(n)\right)P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\eta(t^{\prime})-\eta(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}\geq 1-\epsilon\right)-\frac{\epsilon(n)}{1-\epsilon(n)}.

It follows from this that for any
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, for all
n
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(d))\over 2\psi(t)\|\vec{\overline{\eta}}(0)\|_{2}}\geq 1-\epsilon^{\prime}\right)

(3.44)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq(1-\epsilon^{\prime})P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\left(\eta(t^{\prime})-\eta(d)\right)\over\psi(t)}\geq 1-\epsilon^{\prime}\right)-\epsilon^{\prime}.

Using this and (
3.5
) we get what we need, that for any
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}>0
, for all
n
sufficiently large,

P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(d))\over 2\psi(t)}\geq\|\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)\|_{2}(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})\right)\geq 1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime}.

(3.45)

We can now apply Lemma
2.3
. Note that in the notation of Lemma
2.3
,

{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(t^{\prime})-\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(d))\over 2}=Y_{k/2,(m,n)}(t^{\prime})-Y_{k/2,(m,n)}(d),

(3.46)

and

\|\vec{\overline{\eta}}(d)\|_{2}=\left(2\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\overline{\eta}^{2}_{i}(d)}{2}\right)^{1/2}=\sqrt{2}Y_{k/2,(m,n)}^{1/2}(d).

(3.47)

Therefore,
by (
3.45
) we have,

P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{Y_{k/2,(m,n)}(t^{\prime})-Y_{k/2,(m,n)}(d)\over\psi(t)}\geq\sqrt{2}Y^{1/2}_{k/2,(m,n)}(d)\left(1-\epsilon\right)\right)\geq 1-\epsilon,

(3.48)

and finally,
using Lemma
2.3
with
\widehat{X}_{k/2,(m,n)}
replaced by
X_{k/2,(m,n)}
we see that for all
n
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle P\left(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{n,1}}{X_{k/2,(m,n)}(t^{\prime})-X_{k/2,(m,n)}(d)\over\psi(t)}\geq\sqrt{2}X^{1/2}_{k/2,(m,n)}(d)\left(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 216.81pt\geq\nu_{m,n}^{-k/2}(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime}).

(3.49)

Now suppose that condition
(i)
holds. Then it follows from Theorem
2.2
that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{n,m}=1
.
Using this in (
3.49
) we have that for all
\delta_{j}>0
, for all
\epsilon_{j}>0
sufficiently small,

P\left(\sup_{t\in(0,\delta_{j}]}{X_{k/2}(d+t)-X_{k/2}(d)\over\psi(t)}\geq\sqrt{2}X^{1/2}_{k/2}(d)\left(1-\epsilon_{j}\right)\right)\geq(1-\epsilon_{j}).

(3.50)

Choose subsequences
\{\delta_{j_{i}},\epsilon_{j_{i}}\}
of
\{\delta_{j},\epsilon_{j}\}
such that
\lim_{i\to\infty}\delta_{j_{i}}=0
and
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\epsilon_{j_{i}}<\infty
. It then follows from the Borel-Cantelli Lemma that (
3.7
) holds.

Using Theorem
2.3
the same argument gives (
3.7
) when condition
(ii)
holds.

The last statement in this theorem
has the same proof as the proof of (
3.7
) if we replace
T_{n}
in (
3.9
) by
\{-t_{0},-t_{1},-t_{2},\ldots,-t_{m(n)}\}
.

Remark 3.1

As we point out in (
1.24
), when the Lévy processes in Theorem
1.2
have a Gaussian component,
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\sim{|x|}/{C}
, as
x\to 0
, for some constant
C
. Consequently, by (
2.26
), condition
(i)
in Theorem
3.1
does not hold. Nevertheless we can still use Theorem
3.1
when condition
(ii)
holds.
We can give insight into why we require the condition

f^{\prime}(d)=0
in Theorem
3.1
,
(ii)
.

Consider a permanental vector with kernel,

G(m,n)_{j,k}+f(t_{k}),\qquad j,k=0,1,\ldots,m.

(3.51)

For this vector,

\nu_{n,m}=1+{\bf r}G(m,n){\bf v}^{T}-{\bf h}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T};

(3.52)

see (
2.68
). In our proofs of lower bounds for LILs we require that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{n,m}\newline
=1
, so we can use Theorem
2.1
.
Consider (
1.39
) when
\varphi(x)=x
so that,

u^{(0)}(x,y)=x\wedge y,\qquad x,y\in(0,\infty),

(3.53)

which is the potential of Brownian motion killed the first time it hits
0
, (so that
u(t_{j},t_{k})=G(m,n)_{j,k}=t_{j}\wedge t_{k}
).
The next two lemmas show that for this potential density
\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{n,m}\neq 1
unless take
f^{\prime}(d)=0
.

Lemma 3.2

Let
G(m,n)
be an
(m+1)\times(m+1)
matrix with elements,

G(m,n)_{j,k}=\left(t_{j}\wedge t_{k}\right),\qquad j,k=0,1,\ldots,m,

(3.54)

in which
t_{j}
is a strictly increasing sequence. Then

G(m,n)^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ cccccc cc}{a_{0}}+{a_{1}}&-{a_{1}}&0&\dots&0&0\\
-{a_{1}}&{a_{1}}+{a_{2}}&-{a_{2}}&\dots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\dots&{a_{m-1}}+{a_{m}}&-{a_{m}}\\
0&0&0&\dots&-{a_{m}}&{a_{m}}\end{array}\right),

(3.55)

where

a_{0}=\frac{1}{t_{0}},\quad\mbox{and}\quad a_{j}=\frac{1}{t_{j}-t_{j-1}},\quad j\geq 1.

(3.56)

Proof
It is easy to verify that this is the inverse of
G(m,m)
. See
[
14
, Lemma 2.4]
.

Lemma 3.3

For
G(m,n)
in (
3.51
) with
g\equiv 1
,

\nu_{n,m}=1+\frac{f(d+t_{1})-f(d)}{2t_{1}}.

(3.57)

In particular,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{n,m}=1+\frac{f^{\prime}(d)}{2}.

(3.58)

Obviously, to have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu_{n,m}=1
we must take
f^{\prime}(d)=0
.

Proof

{\bf h}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T}=\sum_{j,k=0}^{m}(r_{j}v_{j})^{1/2}G(m,n)_{j,k}(r_{k}v_{k})^{1/2}=r_{0}v_{0}G(m,n)_{0,0}=v_{0},

(3.59)

since
r_{0}=Ct^{\prime}_{0}
and
G(m,n)_{0,0}=t^{\prime}_{0}/C
. In addition,

\displaystyle{\bf r}G(m,n){\bf v}^{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k=0}^{m}r_{j}G(m,n)_{j,k}v_{k}=r_{0}\sum_{k=0}^{m}G(m,n)_{0,k}v_{k}

(3.60)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{0}G(m,n)_{0,0}\left(v_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}v_{k}\right)=v_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}v_{k},

since
G(m,n)_{0,k}=G(m,n)_{0,0}
.
Consequently,

{\bf r}G(m,n){\bf v}^{T}-{\bf h}G(m,n){\bf h}^{T}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}v_{k}.

(3.61)

It follows from this that,

\sum_{k=1}^{m}v_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{l=0}^{m}G(m,n)^{k,l}f(t^{\prime}_{l})=\sum_{l=0}^{m}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}G(m,n)^{k,l}\right)f(t^{\prime}_{l}).

(3.62)

For each
l
,
S_{l}:=\sum_{k=1}^{m}G(m,n)^{k,l}
is the sum over all elements in the
l
–th column of
G(m,n)^{-1}
, except the first element,
G(m,n)^{0,l}
. Therefore, by Lemma
3.2
, we have

S_{0}=-a_{1}/2
,
S_{1}=-a_{1}/2
and
S_{l}=0
for all
l\geq 2
, where ,

a_{1}=\frac{1}{t^{\prime}_{1}-t^{\prime}_{0}}=\frac{1}{t_{1}}.

(3.63)

This shows that

\sum_{k=1}^{m}v_{k}=-\frac{a_{1}f(t^{\prime}_{0})}{2}+\frac{a_{1}f(t^{\prime}_{1})}{2}=\frac{f(t^{\prime}_{1})-f(t^{\prime}_{0})}{2(t^{\prime}_{1}-t^{\prime}_{0})},

(3.64)

which gives (
3.57
).

4
Proof of Theorems
1.2
–
1.5

4.1
Proof of lower bounds in Theorems
1.2
–
1.4

The increments variance
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)
,
\beta\geq 0
plays a critical role in all these theorems. The next lemma gives some properties of this function.

Lemma 4.1

(i)

For Lévy processes, the Lévy exponent,

\psi(\lambda)=C\lambda^{2}+\psi_{1}(\lambda),

(4.1)

for some
C\geq 0
, where
\psi_{1}(\lambda)=o(\lambda^{2})
as
\lambda\to\infty
;

(ii)

For
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)
in (
1.28
), when
C>0
,

\frac{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)}{|x|}\leq\frac{1}{C},\qquad\forall\,\beta\geq 0,

(4.2)

and,

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|x|}{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)}=C,\qquad\forall\,\beta\geq 0;

(4.3)

(iii)

When
C=0
, in which case in the Lévy process does not have a Gaussian component,

\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{|x|}{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)}=0,\qquad\forall\,\beta\geq 0.

(4.4)

Proof
The statement in
(i)
is well known. (See e.g.,
[
9
, page 138]
.)

The statement in (
4.3
) is given in (
1.24
). The statement in (
4.2
) is trivial since

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\leq\frac{4}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}\lambda x/2}{C\lambda^{2}}\,d\lambda,

(4.5)

and by
[
7
, 3.741.3]
,

\frac{4}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}\lambda x/2}{C\lambda^{2}}\,d\lambda=\frac{4|x|}{C\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}s/2}{s^{2}}\,ds=\frac{|x|}{C}.

(4.6)

We now obtain (
4.4
).
Consider

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)=\frac{4}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}\lambda x/2}{\beta+\psi_{1}(\lambda)}\,d\lambda\\

(4.7)

Let,

M_{\epsilon}=\sup\{\lambda:\beta+\psi_{1}(\lambda)\geq\epsilon\lambda^{2}\},

(4.8)

and write (
4.7
) as,

\displaystyle\frac{4}{\pi}\int_{0}^{M_{\epsilon}}\frac{\sin^{2}\lambda x/2}{\beta+\psi_{1}(\lambda)}\,d\lambda+\frac{4}{\pi}\int_{M_{\epsilon}}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}\lambda x/2}{\beta+\psi_{1}(\lambda)}\,d\lambda\qquad

(4.9)

\displaystyle\qquad:=(\sigma_{1}^{\beta})^{2}(x)+(\sigma_{2}^{\beta})^{2}(x).

We have,

(\sigma_{2}^{\beta})^{2}(x)\geq\frac{4}{\epsilon\pi}\int_{M_{\epsilon}}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}\lambda x/2}{\lambda^{2}}\,d\lambda=\frac{4|x|}{\epsilon\pi}\int_{xM_{\epsilon}}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}s/2}{s^{2}}\,ds.

(4.10)

Consequently,

\frac{|x|}{(\sigma_{2}^{\beta})^{2}(x)}\leq\epsilon\left(\frac{4}{\pi}\int_{xM_{\epsilon}}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}s/2}{s^{2}}\,ds\right)^{-1}.

(4.11)

Using this, (
4.6
) and the fact that
(\sigma_{1}^{\beta})^{2}(x)>0
we see that,

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|x|}{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)}\leq\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|x|}{(\sigma_{2}^{\beta})^{2}(x)}\leq\epsilon.

(4.12)

Since this holds for all
\epsilon>0
, we get (
4.4
).

Lemma 4.2

When
\varphi(x)

satisfies the first line of (
2.26
) it satisfies (
2.27
), i.e.

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|x|}{\varphi(x)}=O(1).

(4.13)

Proof
This follows immediately from (
4.3
) and (
4.4
).

We show that the potentials considered in Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
satisfy (
2.26
) and show what
\varphi_{d}(x)
is. In the case of Theorem
1.2
this is simple because,

u^{\beta}(x,x)-u^{\beta}(x,y)=\frac{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)}{2},\qquad\forall x,y\in\Delta_{d},\,\forall d\geq 0.

(4.14)

By hypothesis,
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)
is a regularly varying function at 0 with index
0<r\leq 1
. It follows from
[
9
, Lemma 7.2.4]
, in particular (7.125), that a regularly varying function with index not equal to 0, is asymptotic to a function with a positive derivative in some neighborhood of
(0,\delta]
, for some
\delta>0
. Label this function
(\rho^{\beta})^{2}(x)
,
For each
\beta>0
we take

\varphi(x)=(\rho^{\beta})^{2}(x).

(4.15)

Therefore in the cases we consider, in some neighborhood of 0,
\varphi
is a continuous strictly increasing regularly varying function with index
0<r\leq 1
.
(Actually as we point out in (
1.29
),
under mild regularity on
\psi
, all the functions
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)
,
\beta\geq 0
, are asymptotically equivalent at 0.)

In Theorem
1.3
we consider potential densities,

v^{\beta}(x,y)=u^{\beta}(x,y)-\frac{u^{\beta}(x)u^{\beta}(y)}{u^{\beta}(0,0)},\qquad x,y\in\Delta_{d}(\delta),

(4.16)

for some
\delta>0
, which may depend on
x
and
y
and where
\{u^{\beta}(x,y)\}
is translation invariant. The next lemma shows the relationship between
v^{\beta}(x,y)
and
u^{\beta}(x,y)
when
\psi\left(\lambda\right)
does not have a Gaussian component. This is the case in Part I, since (
1.32
) implies that

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|x|}{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)}=0,\qquad\forall\,\beta\geq 0;

(4.17)

which contradicts (
4.3
).

Lemma 4.3

Let
v^{\beta}(x,y)
,
u^{\beta}(x,y)
and
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)
be as in Theorem
1.3
, in which
\beta>0
, and assume that
\psi\left(\lambda\right)
does not have a Gaussian component. Then for any
d\neq 0
,

\frac{v^{\beta}(x,x)-v^{\beta}(x,y)}{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)}\sim\frac{1}{2},\qquad\mbox{as }\,\,x,y\to d,\,\,x\neq y,

(4.18)

and consequently,

\frac{v^{\beta}(x,x)+v^{\beta}(y,y)-2v^{\beta}(x,y)}{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)}\sim 1,\qquad\mbox{as }\,\,x,y\to d,\,\,x\neq y,

(4.19)

Proof

\displaystyle v^{\beta}(x,x)-v^{\beta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)}{2}-\frac{u^{\beta}(x)\left(u^{\beta}(x)-u^{\beta}(y)\right)}{u^{\beta}(0)}

(4.20)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)}{2}-\frac{u^{\beta}(x)\left((\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(y)-(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\right)}{2u^{\beta}(0)}.

By hypothesis,
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}\left(x\right)\in C^{1}\left(T\right)
. Therefore there exists a constant
C
such that,

|(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(y)-(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)|\leq C|x-y|,\hskip 14.45377pt\forall x,y\in[d,d+\delta].

(4.21)

Using (
4.4
) we see that,

\lim_{|x-y|\to 0}\frac{|x-y|}{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)}=0.

(4.22)

This gives (
4.18
).

When
\varphi(x)=(\rho^{\beta})^{2}(x)
as in (
4.15
), (
2.26
) holds.

In Theorem
1.3
, II we add the hypothesis that the Lévy exponent
\psi(\lambda)
of
\,Y^{\prime}
is a stable mixture. In this case (
2.26
) follows from the next lemma:

Lemma 4.4

When the Lévy exponent
\psi(\lambda)
of
\,Y^{\prime}
is a stable mixture, for all
\beta>0
and all
d>0
there exists a
\delta>0
such that,

v^{\beta}(x,x)-v^{\beta}(x,y)\asymp(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y),\qquad\forall x,y\in\Delta_{d}(\delta),

(4.23)

where
\delta
may depend on
d
.

Proof
We have,

\displaystyle v^{\beta}(x,x)-v^{\beta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)}{2}-\frac{u^{\beta}(x)\left(u^{\beta}(x)-u^{\beta}(y)\right)}{u^{\beta}(0)}

(4.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)}{2}-\frac{u^{\beta}(x)\left((\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(y)-(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\right)}{2u^{\beta}(0)}.

so that,

\frac{v^{\beta}(x,x)-v^{\beta}(x,y)}{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)}=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{u^{\beta}(x)\left((\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(y)-(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\right)}{2u^{\beta}(0)(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)}.

(4.25)

Note that for all
x\in R^{1}-\{0\}
there exists an
\epsilon>0
such that,

\frac{u^{\beta}(x)}{u^{\beta}(0)}\leq 1-\epsilon,

(4.26)

this follows from the definition of
u^{\beta}
, see (
1.16
), and the fact that
\psi(\lambda)
is a continuous function. Therefore to get the lower bound in (
4.23
) it suffices to show that for any
d>0
we can find a
\delta>0
, which may depend on
d
, with

\frac{|(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(y)-(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)|}{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)}\leq 1+\frac{\epsilon}{2},\qquad\forall x,y\in\Delta_{d}(\delta).

(4.27)

Since
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\in C^{1}\left(T\right)
there exists at least one
d^{*}\in\Delta_{d}(\delta)
for which,

|((\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(d^{*}))^{\prime}|=\max_{0\leq u\leq|x-y|}\big|((\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(u))^{\prime}\big|,

(4.28)

which implies that,

\big|(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(y)-(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\big|\leq|((\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(d^{*}))^{\prime}||x-y|.

(4.29)

If
\psi\left(\lambda\right)
in (
4.1
) is such that
C=0
, it follows from (
4.4
) that

\frac{|x-y|}{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)}\to 0,\qquad\text{as $|x-y|\to 0$ },

(4.30)

which gives (
4.27
).

If
\psi\left(\lambda\right)
in (
4.1
) is such that
C>0
, it follows from (
8.20
) and (
4.2
) that,

\big|(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(y)-(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\big|\leq\frac{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(d^{*})}{d^{*}}|x-y|\\
\leq\frac{|x-y|}{C}.

(4.31)

Set
x=d+x^{\prime}
and
y=d+y^{\prime}
. Then, using (
4.3
) we see that,

\limsup_{|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|\to 0}\frac{\big|(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(y)-(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\big|}{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)}=1,

(4.32)

which gives (
4.27
).

In Theorem
1.4
the Gaussian process
\eta=\{\eta(x),x\in R^{1}\}
defined in (
1.38
)–(
1.42
) has covariance,

u^{(0)}(x,y)=\phi(x)+\phi(y)-\phi(x-y),\qquad x,y\in R^{1}.

(4.33)

In this case,

\displaystyle(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\left(\eta(x)-\eta(y)\right)^{2}

(4.34)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{(0)}(x,x)+u^{(0)}(y,y)-2u^{(0)}(x,y)=2\phi(x-y).

Since
u^{(0)}(0,0)=0
it follows from (
1.13
) that we restrict
d
to
R^{1}-\{0\}
. Then,

u^{(0)}(d+x,d+x)-u^{(0)}(d+x,d+y)=\phi(d+x)-\phi(d+y)+\phi(x-y).

(4.35)

If
\phi(x)\in C^{1}(\Delta_{d})
for some
d\neq 0
, then

\phi(d+x)-\phi(d+y)\asymp\phi^{\prime}(d)|x-y|,\qquad\text{as}\quad|x-y|\to 0,

(4.36)

and

u^{(0)}(d+x,d+x)-u^{0}(d+x,d+y)\asymp\phi^{\prime}(d)|x-y|+\phi(x-y).

(4.37)

In Theorem
1.4
, I, it follows from (
1.43
) that,

\limsup_{|x-y|\to 0}\frac{|x-y|}{\phi(x-y)}=0

(4.38)

and we have,

\displaystyle u^{(0)}(x,x)-u^{(0)}(x,y)

\displaystyle\asymp

\displaystyle\phi(x-y),\,\,\text{and,}

(4.39)

\displaystyle(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\phi(x-y),\qquad\forall x,y\in\Delta_{d}(\delta),

for some
\delta>0
sufficiently small.

By hypothesis,
(\sigma^{0})^{2}(x)=2\phi(x)
is a regularly varying function at 0 with index
0<r\leq 1
. Such a function is asymptotic to a strictly increasing function at zero, say
(\rho^{0})^{2}(x)
. (See e,g,
[
9
, Lemma 7.2.4]
.) Therefore, it follows from (
4.39
) that (
2.26
) holds with

\varphi(x)=(\rho^{0})^{2}(x).

(4.40)

It follows from (
1.39
) that,

u^{(0)}(x,x)-u^{(0)}(x,y)=\phi(x)-\phi(y)+\phi(x-y),\qquad x,y\in R^{1}-\{0\}.

(4.41)

In Theorem
1.4
, II we add the condition that
(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x)=2\phi(x)
is increasing in some neighborhood of
d
. Therefore for
x,y

in this neighborhood,

u^{(0)}(x,x)-u^{(0)}(x,y)\geq\phi(x-y),\qquad y\leq x.

(4.42)

Since
2\phi(x-y)=(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x,y)
, defining
\varphi(x)
as in (
4.40
), we see that (
2.26
) is satisfied.
This completes the proof that the potentials considered in Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
satisfy (
2.26
).

We now show that for the Gaussian processes with covariances given by the potentials in Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
, (
3.5
) holds. The inequalities in the next two lemmas are critical because they allow us to use Slepian’s Lemma.

Lemma 4.5

Let
\{\eta(x),x\in R^{1}\}
be a stationary Gaussian process with covariance
u^{\beta}(x-y)=u^{\beta}(x,y)
,
\beta>0
as in Theorem
1.2
. Set

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}\left(x\right)=E\left(\eta(x)-\eta(0)\right)^{2}=2\left(u^{\beta}(0)-u^{\beta}(x)\right).

(4.43)

Then for
0<x\leq y\leq\Delta^{\prime}
,

\frac{E\left(\left(\eta(d+x)-\eta(d)\right)\left(\eta(d+y)-\eta(d)\right)\right)}{\sigma^{\beta}(x)\sigma^{\beta}(y)}\leq C_{\Delta^{\prime}}\frac{\sigma^{\beta}(x)}{\sigma^{\beta}(y)},

(4.44)

for some constant
C_{\Delta^{\prime}}
depending on
\Delta^{\prime}
.

Proof
Since
\eta
is a stationary Gaussian process it suffices to show (
4.44
) for
d=0
, i.e., that,

\frac{E\left(\left(\eta(x)-\eta(0)\right)\left(\eta(y)-\eta(0)\right)\right)}{\sigma^{\beta}(x)\sigma^{\beta}(y)}\leq C_{\Delta^{\prime}}\frac{\sigma^{\beta}(x)}{\sigma^{\beta}(y)}.

(4.45)

Note that,

E\left(\left(\eta(x)-\eta(0)\right)\left(\eta(y)-\eta(0)\right)\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left((\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)+(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(y)-(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(y-x)\right).

(4.46)

It follows from
[
9
, Lemma 7.4.2]
and (
1.13
) with
T=R^{1}
that when
\beta>0
,

\displaystyle(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(y)-(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(y-x)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2u^{\beta}(0)}{u^{\beta}(x)+u^{\beta}(y)}(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x).

(4.47)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{\Delta^{\prime}}\,(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x),

for some constant
C^{\prime}_{\Delta^{\prime}}
depending on
\Delta^{\prime}
.
Using (
4.46
) and (
4.47
) we get (
4.45
).

Lemma 4.6

Let

\{\eta(x),x\in R^{1}\}
be a Gaussian process with covariance

\{u^{(0)}(x,y),x,y\in R^{1}\}
as in Theorem
1.4
. Let

(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x,y)=E(\eta(x)-\eta(y))^{2},

(4.48)

and note that
(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x,y)=(\sigma^{0})^{2}(x-y)
.
Then for all
x,y,d\in R^{1}-\{0\}
,

\frac{E\left(\left(\eta(d+x)-\eta(d)\right)\left(\eta(d+y)-\eta(d)\right)\right)}{\sigma^{0}(d+x,d)\sigma^{0}(d+y,d)}\leq\frac{\sigma^{0}(x)}{\sigma^{0}(y)}.

(4.49)

Proof
Since
\eta
is a Gaussian process with stationary increments and
\eta(0)=0
, the left–hand side of (
4.49
) is equal to,

\frac{E\left(\eta(x)\eta(y)\right)}{\sigma^{0}(x)\sigma^{0}(y)}=\frac{u^{(0)}(x,y)}{\sigma^{0}(x)\sigma^{0}(y)}.

(4.50)

By hypothesis
u^{(0)}=\{u^{(0)}(x,y),x,y\in T\}
is the potential density of a symmetric Borel right process with state space
R^{1}-\{0\}
. Therefore, by (
1.12
) for all
x,y\in T
,

\frac{u^{(0)}(x,y)}{\sigma^{0}(x)\sigma^{0}(y)}\leq\frac{u^{(0)}(x,x)}{\sigma^{0}(x)\sigma^{0}(y)}=\frac{\sigma^{0}(x)}{\sigma^{0}(y)},

(4.51)

where we use (
1.39
) and (
1.42
).

We now show that condition (
3.5
) in Theorem
3.1
is satisfied by the Gaussian processes considered in Lemma
4.5
. We write it with the following change of notation.
For all
\epsilon>0
,

P\left(\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{\eta(d+t_{j})-\eta(d)}{\sigma^{\beta}(t_{j})(2\log\log 1/t_{j})^{1/2}}\geq 1-\epsilon\right)\geq 1-\epsilon,

(4.52)

for all
n
sufficiently large.

It follows from Lemma
4.5
that for
j<k
, which implies that
t_{j}<t_{k}
,

\frac{E\left(\left(\eta(d+t_{j})-\eta(d)\right)\left(\eta(d+t_{k})-\eta(d)\right)\right)}{\sigma^{\beta}(t_{j})\sigma^{\beta}(t_{k})}\leq C\frac{\sigma^{\beta}(t_{j})}{\sigma^{\beta}(t_{k})}.

(4.53)

Since
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)
is regularly varying at
0
with index greater than 0,

\frac{\sigma^{\beta}(t_{j})}{\sigma^{\beta}(t_{k})}\leq\sup_{1<q\leq t_{m(n)}}\frac{\sigma^{\beta}(\theta^{q})}{\sigma^{\beta}(\theta^{q-1})}\leq\epsilon(\theta).

(4.54)

Therefore, the left–hand side of
(
4.53
) is bounded by
C\epsilon(\theta)
.
This term goes to 0 as
\theta\to 0
and
n\to\infty.

This allows us to use Slepian’s Lemma. Let,

\zeta_{j}=\frac{\eta(d+t_{j})-\eta(d)}{\sigma^{\beta}(t_{j})},\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,m(n)\}.

(4.55)

It follows from (
4.53
) and (
4.54
) that for any
0<\theta<1
and
\epsilon^{\prime}(\theta)
,

E\zeta_{j}\zeta_{k}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}(\theta),\qquad\forall\,j,k\in\{1,\ldots,t_{m(n)}\},

(4.56)

for all
n
sufficiently large.
Let
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j=0}^{m(n)}
be independent standard normal random variables and consider the sequence,

\widetilde{\xi}_{j}=(1-\epsilon^{\prime}(\theta))^{1/2}\xi_{j}+\epsilon^{\prime}(\theta)^{1/2}\xi_{0},\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,t_{m(n)}\}.

(4.57)

Note that,

E\zeta_{j}^{2}=E\widetilde{\xi}_{j}^{2},\qquad\text{and}\qquad E\zeta_{j}\zeta_{k}\leq E\widetilde{\xi}_{j}\widetilde{\xi}_{k},\qquad\forall\,j,k\in\{1,\ldots,t_{m(n)}\}.

(4.58)

Set,

b_{j}=\left(2\log\log 1/t_{j}\right)^{1/2}.

(4.59)

Using (
4.58
), it follows from Slepian’s Lemma, (see e.g.
[
9
, Lemma 5.5.1]

), that,

P\left(\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{\zeta_{j}}{b_{j}}\geq(1-\epsilon^{\prime}(\theta))\right)\geq P\left(\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{\widetilde{\xi}_{j}}{b_{j}}\geq(1-\epsilon)\right)

(4.60)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq P\left(\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{\left(1-\epsilon^{\prime}(\theta)\right)^{1/2}\xi_{j}}{b_{j}}\geq(1-\epsilon/2),\epsilon^{\prime}(\theta)^{1/2}\xi_{0}<\epsilon/2\right).

Therefore, by taking
\theta>0
sufficiently small we have,

\displaystyle P\left(\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{\zeta_{j}}{b_{j}}\geq(1-\epsilon)\right)

(4.61)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq(1-\epsilon)P\left(\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{\xi_{j}}{b_{j}}\geq(1-\epsilon/2)\right).

Lemma 4.7

Let
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m(n)}
be independent standard normal random variables, Then for all
\epsilon>0
, for all
n
sufficiently large,

P\left(\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{\xi_{j}}{b_{j}}\geq(1-\epsilon)\right)\geq 1-e^{-n^{\epsilon}}.

(4.62)

Proof
Refer to (
4.59
) and (
3.9
)
to see that,

\displaystyle b_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(2\log\log\left(\frac{1}{\theta^{n+1-j}}\right)\right)^{1/2}

(4.63)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(2\log(n+1-j)+2\log\log\left(\frac{1}{\theta}\right)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(2\log(n+1-j)+h(\theta)\right)^{1/2}.

Note that for all
\theta
sufficiently small,

\inf_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}b_{j}\geq\left(2\log(n+1-[n^{q}])\right)^{1/2}.

(4.64)

Let
\widetilde{b}_{j}=(1-\epsilon)b_{j}
. It follows from
[
9
, (5.19)]
and the fact that
P(\xi_{j}\geq\widetilde{b}_{j})=(1/2)P(|\xi_{j}|\geq\widetilde{b}_{j})
that for all
\theta
and
\epsilon
sufficiently small, for all
1\leq j\leq m(n)
, for all
n
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle P\left(\xi_{j}\geq\widetilde{b}_{j}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-\widetilde{b}_{j}^{2}/2}}{2\sqrt{2\pi}\,\,\widetilde{b}_{j}}=\frac{e^{-(1-\epsilon)^{2}(\log(n+1-j)+h(\theta)/2)}}{2\sqrt{2\pi}\,\,(1-\epsilon)\left(2\log(n+1-j)+h(\theta)\right)^{1/2}}

(4.65)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-(1-\epsilon)^{2}h(\theta)/2}\,e^{-(1-\epsilon)^{2}\log(n+1-j)}}{2\sqrt{\pi}(1-\epsilon)[(2\log(n+1-j)^{1/2}+h^{1/2}(\theta)]}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-(1-\epsilon)^{2}h(\theta)/2}\,e^{-(1-\epsilon)^{2}\log(n+1-j)}}{4\sqrt{\pi}(1-\epsilon)(\log n)^{1/2}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{C(\theta,\epsilon)}{(\log n)^{1/2}(n+1-j)^{(1-\epsilon)^{2}}}.

We now note that,

\displaystyle P\left(\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{\xi_{j}}{\widetilde{b}_{j}}\geq 1\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\prod_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}P\left(\xi_{j}<\widetilde{b}_{j}\right)

(4.66)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\prod_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\left(1-P\left(\xi_{j}\geq\widetilde{b}_{j}\right)\right).

Let
x_{j}=P(\xi_{j}\geq\widetilde{b}_{j})
. By the well known estimate, (see e.g.,
[
9
, (5.18)]
),

\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}x_{j}\leq\frac{1}{(n+1-[n^{q}])^{1-\epsilon}}.

(4.67)

Since this implies that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}x_{j}=0

we see that, for all
\varepsilon>0
, for all
n
sufficiently large

\prod_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}(1-x_{j})=\exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m(n)}\log(1-x_{j})\right)\leq\exp\left(-(1-\varepsilon)\sum_{j=1}^{m(n)}x_{j}\right).

(4.68)

Using (
4.1
) we see that,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{m(n)}x_{j}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{C(\theta,\epsilon)}{(\log n)^{1/2}}\sum_{j=1}^{m(n)}\frac{1}{(n+1-j)^{(1-\epsilon)^{2}}}

(4.69)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{C(\theta,\epsilon)}{(\log n)^{1/2}}\sum_{k=[n]^{q}}^{n}\frac{1}{k^{(1-\epsilon)^{2}}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{C(\theta,\epsilon)}{2\epsilon(\log n)^{1/2}}n^{2\epsilon-\epsilon^{2}},

for all
n
sufficiently large. Using this in (
4.68
) and then in (
4.66
) we get (
4.62
).

Combining Lemma
4.7
and (
4.61
) we get that for all
\epsilon>0,

P\left(\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{\zeta_{j}}{(2\log\log 1/t_{j})^{1/2}}\geq(1-\epsilon)\right)\geq(1-\epsilon)\left(1-e^{-n^{\epsilon}}\right),

(4.70)

or equivalently,

\displaystyle P\left(\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{\eta(d+t_{j})-\eta(d)}{\sigma^{\beta}(t_{j})(2\log\log 1/t_{j})^{1/2}}\geq(1-\epsilon)\right)

(4.71)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\geq(1-\epsilon)\left(1-e^{-n^{\epsilon}}\right),

for all
n
sufficiently large. This gives (
4.52
) or , equivalently, (
3.5
).

It should be obvious that, using Lemma
4.6
exactly the same proof shows that condition (
3.5
) in Theorem
3.1
is satisfied by the Gaussian processes considered in Lemma
4.6
, and that it shows that
for all
\epsilon>0
,

P\left(\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{\eta(d+t_{j})-\eta(d)}{\sigma^{0}(t_{j})}\geq(1-\epsilon)(2\log\log 1/t_{j})^{1/2}\right)\geq(1-\epsilon),

(4.72)

for all
n
sufficiently large.

Now let
\{\widehat{\eta}(x);x\in R^{1}\}
be a Gaussian process with covariance
v^{\beta}(x,y)
,
\beta>0,
as in Theorem
1.3
. Note that,

\widehat{\eta}(x)=\eta(x)-\frac{u^{\beta}(x)}{u^{\beta}(0)}\eta(0),\qquad x\in R^{1}-\{0\},

(4.73)

so that,

\displaystyle\widehat{\eta}(d+t_{j})-\widehat{\eta}(d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta(d+t_{j})-\eta(d)-\frac{u^{\beta}(d+t_{j})-u^{\beta}(d)}{u^{\beta}(0)}\eta(0)

(4.74)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta(d+t_{j})-\eta(d)-\frac{(\sigma^{\beta}(t_{j}))^{2}}{2u^{\beta}(0)}\eta(0).

Set,

(\widehat{\sigma}^{\beta})^{2}\left(d+t_{j},d\right)=E(\widehat{\eta}(d+t_{j})-\widehat{\eta}(d))^{2}.

(4.75)

It follows from Lemma
4.3
that.

\frac{(\widehat{\sigma}^{\beta})^{2}\left(d+t_{j},d\right)}{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}\left(t_{j}\right)}\sim 1,\quad\mbox{as }\,\,t_{j}\to 0.

(4.76)

Also, since obviously,

\frac{\sigma^{\beta}(t_{j})}{(\log\log 1/t_{j})^{1/2}}\to 0,\qquad\forall\,1\leq j\leq m(n),\quad\text{as }\,n\to\infty,

(4.77)

it follows from (
4.52
) that
for all
\epsilon>0
,

P\left(\sup_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{\widehat{\eta}(d+t_{j})-\widehat{\eta}(d)}{\widehat{\sigma}^{\beta}(d+t_{j},d)(2\log\log 1/t_{j})^{1/2}}\geq 1-\epsilon\right)\geq 1-\epsilon,

(4.78)

for all
n
sufficiently large.
Using Theorem
3.1
we see that (
3.7
) holds for the permanental processes considered in Theorem
1.3
. Using (
4.76
) we see that this is the same as (
1.34
).

To complete the proof of Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
it remains to show that either conditions
(i)
or
(ii)
of Theorem
3.1
is satisfied.
In Part I, condition
(ii)
of these theorems we assume that for some
p>1
and
\delta>0
,

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\geq x\left(\log 1/x\right)^{p},\qquad x\in[0,\delta],

(4.79)

for some
\beta\geq 0
.
It follows from (
2.26
), which we. have shown holds for
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}
that

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\leq C\varphi(x),\qquad\text{as $x\to 0.$}

(4.80)

This implies that,

\lim_{n\to\infty}m(n)\sup_{1\leq j\neq k\leq m(n)}|t_{k}-t_{j}|\left(\varphi\left(|t_{k}-t_{j}|\right)\right)^{-1}=0,

(4.81)

since, for
n+1-[n^{q}]\geq n/2
,

\displaystyle m(n)|t_{k}-t_{j}|\left(\varphi\left(|t_{k}-t_{j}|\right)\right)^{-1}

(4.82)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq n\sup_{|x|\leq\theta^{n/2}}\frac{x}{\varphi(x)}\leq Cn\sup_{|x|\leq\theta^{n/2}}\frac{x}{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq Cn\sup_{|x|\leq\theta^{n/2}}\frac{1}{(\log 1/x)^{p}}=\frac{C2^{p}}{n^{p-1}\log^{p}(1/\theta)}.

Therefore, condition
(i)
of Theorem
3.1
is satisfied by the processes considered in Part I of Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
.

Finally, it is clear that condition
(ii)
of Theorem
3.1
is satisfied by the processes considered in Part II of Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
because the hypotheses in Part II of Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
simply repeat condition
(ii)
of Theorem
3.1
. (The additional conditions in Part II of Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
are used to show that (
2.26
) holds in these cases.)

In Section
8
we show that there are many examples of the functions
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}

that satisfy the hypotheses in Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
. In Section
9
we show the same thing for the excessive functions considered in these theorems.

4.2
Upper bounds in Theorems
1.2
–
1.4

The following theorem follows from
[
13
, Theorems 1.1 and 1.2]
.

Theorem 4.1

Let
\widetilde{X}_{\alpha}=\{\widetilde{X}_{\alpha}(t),t\in\Delta_{d}(\delta)\}
, (see (
1.18
)), be an
\alpha-
permanental process with kernel
\{v(x,y);x,y\in\Delta_{d}(\delta)\}

and assume that,

v(x,x)+v(y,y)-2(v(x,y)v(y,x))^{1/2}\leq\varphi^{2}(|x-y|),\qquad x,y\in\Delta_{d}(\delta),

(4.83)

for some
\delta>0
sufficiently small, where
\varphi^{2}(|x|)
is a regularly varying function at
0
with index
0<p\leq 1
. Then

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|\widetilde{X}_{\alpha}(d+x)-\widetilde{X}_{\alpha}(d)|}{(2\varphi^{2}(x)\log\log 1/|x|)^{1/2}}\leq\sqrt{2}X^{1/2}_{\alpha}(d)\qquad a.s.

(4.84)

Proof
In
[
13
]
Theorem 1.1 is used to prove Theorem 1.2 which is the same as Theorem
4.1
except that
\Delta_{d}(\delta)
is replaced by
[0,1]
.
The same proof gives the statement in Theorem
4.1
.

We now use this theorem to get upper bounds
for the permanental processes in Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
. To begin we must estimate (
4.83
) for the kernels of the permanental processes in these theorems.
Let
X_{\alpha}=\{X_{\alpha}(t),t\in[d,d+\delta]\}
,
d\in T
, be an
\alpha-
permanental process with kernel

u_{f,g}(x,y)=u(x,y)+g(x)f(y),\qquad x,y\in[d,d+\delta].

(4.85)

Set,

\sigma^{2}(x,y)=u(x,x)+u(y,y)-2u(x,y)

(4.86)

and

\widehat{\sigma}_{g,f}^{2}(x,y)=u_{g,f}(x,x)+u_{g,f}(y,y)-2\left(u_{g,f}(x,y)u_{g,f}(y,x)\right)^{1/2}.

(4.87)

Note that by (
1.13
) we can choose
\delta
so that,

\sup_{x,y\in[d,d+\delta]}u\left(x,y\right)>0.

(4.88)

The next lemma generalizes part of
[
13
, Corollary 4.6]
.

Lemma 4.8

Let
d,\delta
be such that (
4.88
) holds. Then,
if
g,f\in C^{1}([d,d+\delta])
,

\widehat{\sigma}_{g,f}^{2}(x,y)\leq\sigma^{2}(x,y)+C_{d,\delta}|y-x|^{2},\qquad x,y\in[d,d+\delta].

(4.89)

for some constant
C_{d,\delta}
depending on
d
and
\delta
.

Proof
Add and subtract
u_{g,f}(x,y)+u_{g,f}(y,x)
from the right–hand side of (
4.87
) to see that,

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}_{g,f}^{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u_{g,f}(x,x)+u_{g,f}(y,y)

(4.90)

\displaystyle-u_{g,f}(x,y)-u_{g,f}(y,x)+\left(u_{g,f}^{1/2}(x,y)-u_{g,f}^{1/2}(y,x)\right)^{2}.

Note that,

\displaystyle u_{g,f}(x,x)+u_{g,f}(y,y)-u_{g,f}(x,y)-u_{g,f}(y,x)

(4.91)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sigma^{2}(x,y)+(f(x)-f(y))(g(x)-g(y))

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sigma^{2}(x,y)+O|x-y|^{2},

and

\displaystyle\left(u_{g,f}^{1/2}(x,y)-u_{g,f}^{1/2}(y,x)\right)^{2}

(4.92)

\displaystyle\qquad=\left(\left(u(x,y)+g(x)f(y)\right)^{1/2}-\left(u(y,x)+g(y)f(x)\right)^{1/2}\right)^{2}.

Using the simple fact that for
a,b>0
,
|b^{1/2}-a^{1/2}|=|b-a|/(a^{1/2}+b^{1/2})
,
we see that,

\left(u_{g,f}^{1/2}(x,y)-u_{g,f}^{1/2}(y,x)\right)^{2}\leq\frac{|g(x)f(y)-g(y)f(x)|^{2}}{2(a^{*}(d,\delta))^{2}}=O\left(|x-y|^{2}\right),

(4.93)

where,

(a^{*}(d,\delta))^{2}=\inf_{x,y\in[d,d+\delta]}u(x,y)+\inf_{x\in[d,d+\delta]}(g(x)\wedge f(x)).

(4.94)

Since
u^{\beta}
is continuous and
u^{\beta}(d,d)>0
, (see (
1.13
)), and
g,f\geq 0
,
a^{*}(d,\delta)>0
, for all
\delta
sufficiently small.
Putting all this together gives (
4.89
).

Proof of upper bounds in Theorem
1.2

In this theorem
u^{\beta}
is translation invariant. Therefore,
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x,y)=(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(y-x,0)
, which we write simply as
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(y-x)
. Therefore, by (
4.89
), for any
\epsilon>0
,

(\widehat{\sigma}^{\beta})^{2}(x,y)\leq(1+\epsilon)(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y),\qquad\text{as $x,y\to d.$}

(4.95)

Here we use the fact that in this theorem,

\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)}\to 0,\qquad\text{as $x,y\to d,\,\,x\neq y.$}

(4.96)

It follows from (
4.95
) that for any
\epsilon>0
, (
4.83
) holds for all
\delta
sufficiently small with
\varphi^{2}(|x-y|)=(1+\epsilon)(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)
. Using this in Theorem
4.1
we get the upper bounds in Theorem
1.2
and see that they hold for
\alpha-
permanental processes with kernel

u^{\beta}_{g,f}(x,y)
for all
\alpha>0
.

Proof of upper bounds in Theorems
1.4

In this case
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x,y)
in (
4.86
)
is equal to
2\phi(x-y)
. Consequently the proof proceeds exactly as in the proof above with
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)
replaced by
2\phi(x-y)
.

Proof of upper bounds in Theorem
1.3

In this case
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x,y)
in (
4.86
)
is equal to,

v^{\beta}(x,x)+v^{\beta}(x,x)-2v^{\beta}(x,y).

(4.97)

By Lemma
4.3
,

\frac{v^{\beta}(x,x)+v^{\beta}(x,x)-2v^{\beta}(x,y)}{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-y)}\to 1,\qquad\mbox{as }\,\,x,y\downarrow d,\,\,x\neq y.

(4.98)

Therefore the proof follows as in the proof of the upper bounds in Theorem
1.2
.

Remark 4.1

Let
\eta
be a Gaussian process with stationary increments and
\eta(0)=0.
For a very large class of Gaussian processes; see e.g.,
[
9
, Theorem 7.2.15]
,

\limsup_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{|\eta(t)|}{\left(2\sigma^{2}\left(t\right)\log\log 1/t\right)^{1/2}}=1\qquad a.s.,

(4.99)

where
\sigma^{2}\left(t\right)=E(\eta^{2}(t)).

Consequently,

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{(\eta^{2}(t)-\eta^{2}(0))/2}{\left(\sigma^{2}\left(t\right)\log\log 1/t\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1\qquad a.s.,

(4.100)

This is the result we expect to get for permanental process with kernels of the form of (
4.85
) when
u(x,y)
is the covariance of
\eta.

The following remark relates to statements made on pages
1
and
.

Remark 4.2

Let
\xi=\{\xi(x);x\in R^{1}\}
be a Gaussian process with
\xi(0)=0
and the property that,

E\left(\xi(x)-\xi(y)\right)^{2}=\rho(x-y),\qquad x,y\in R^{1},

(4.101)

for some positive function
\rho
. It is well known and easy to show that
\xi
has stationary increments.
Using the equality,

\frac{1}{2}(a-b)(c-d)=-(a-c)^{2}+(a-d)^{2}+(b-d)^{2}-(b-c)^{2},

(4.102)

we see that since for any
x,y,z,w\in R^{1}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}E\left(\left(\xi(x)-\xi(y)\right)\left(\xi(z)-\xi(w)\right)\right)

(4.103)

\displaystyle\qquad=-\rho(x-z)+\rho(x-w)+\rho(y-z)-\rho(y-w),

which shows that the covariance of the increments of
\xi
are translation invariant. Since the joint distributions Gaussian process are determined by it’s covariance we see that
\xi
has stationary increments.
We might also note that (
4.101
) also shows that,

E(\xi(x)\xi(y))=\frac{1}{2}\left(\rho(x)+\rho(y)-\rho(x-y)\right).

(4.104)

4.3
Proof of Theorem
1.5

Note that the conditions imposed on the excessive functions
f

and
g
in Theorem
1.5
are the same as those in condition
(ii)
in Theorem
3.1
. Therefore to prove this theorem it remains to show that
the potentials in (
1.46
), (
1.47
) and (
1.49
) satisfy the conditions in (
2.26
) and (
2.27
) and that (
3.7
) holds for the Gaussian processes with covariances that are these potentials. This follows immediately from the following computations:

For
x,y\in R^{1}
define,

\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}p(x)q(y),&\quad x\leq y\\
q(x)p(y),&\quad y\leq x\end{array}\right.,

(4.105)

where
p,q\in C^{2}(R^{1})
are positive convex functions such that
p
is strictly increasing and

q
is strictly decreasing. Note that

\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,x)-\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}p(x)\left(q(x)-q(y)\right),&\quad x\leq y\\
q(x)\left(p(x)-p(y)\right),&\quad y\leq x\end{array}\right..

(4.106)

It follows from
[
9
, Lemma 5.1.8]
that
\{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y),x,y\in R^{1}\}
is a strictly positive definite function on
R^{1}\times R^{1}
. Let
\widetilde{\eta}=\{\widetilde{\eta}(x),x\in R^{1}\}
be a Gaussian process with covariance
\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)
and set,

(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\beta})^{2}(x,y)=E\left(\widetilde{\eta}(x)-\widetilde{\eta}(y)\right)^{2},

(4.107)

so that

(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\beta})^{2}(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}p(x)\left(q(x)-q(y)\right)+\left(p(y)-p(x)\right)q(y),&\quad x\leq y\\
q(x)\left(p(x)-p(y)\right)+p(y)\left(q(y)-q(x)\right),&\quad y\leq x.\end{array}\right.

(4.108)

Lemma 4.9

For
x,y\in R^{1}
,

(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\beta})(x,y)\sim\tau(x)|x-y|,\qquad\text{as $|x-y|\to 0$},

(4.109)

where,

\tau(x)=\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,x)\frac{d}{dx}\left(\log\frac{p(x)}{q(x)}\right)>0.

(4.110)

In addition for
y\leq x
,

\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,x)-\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)\sim q(x)p^{\prime}(x)|x-y|,\qquad\text{as $|x-y|\to 0$}.

(4.111)

Proof
Note that,

\displaystyle q(x)-q(y)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle q^{\prime}(x)(x-y),\qquad\text{as $|x-y|\to 0$}

(4.112)

\displaystyle p(y)-p(x)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle p^{\prime}(x)(y-x),\qquad\text{as $|x-y|\to 0$}.

Therefore, by (
4.108
), when
x\leq y
, as
|y-x|\to 0
,

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\beta})^{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p(x)\left(q(x)-q(y)\right)+q(y)\left(p(y)-p(x)\right)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle p(x)q^{\prime}(x)(x-y)+(q(x)-q^{\prime}(x)(x-y))p^{\prime}(x)(y-x)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\left(q(x)p^{\prime}(x)-p(x)q^{\prime}(x)\right)(y-x)=\tau(x)(y-x).

Since
(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\beta})^{2}
is symmetric we get (
4.109
). Furthermore, since
p(x)/q(x)
is strictly increasing we see that (
4.110
) holds.

When
y\leq x
,

\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,x)-\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)=q(x)(p(x)-p(y))\sim q(x)p^{\prime}(x)|x-y|,\quad\text{as $|x-y|\to 0$},

(4.113)

which is (
4.111
).

Lemma 4.10

The increments of
\widetilde{\eta}
are negatively correlated, i.e., for
x<y<z<w
,

E(\widetilde{\eta}(y)-\widetilde{\eta}(x))(\widetilde{\eta}(w)-\widetilde{\eta}(z))\leq 0.

(4.114)

Consequently, for
d,x,y\in T
where
x\leq y
,

\frac{E\left(\left(\widetilde{\eta}(d+x)-\widetilde{\eta}(d)\right)\left(\widetilde{\eta}(d+y)-\widetilde{\eta}(d)\right)\right)}{(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\beta})(d,d+x)(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\beta})(d,d+y)}\leq\frac{(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\beta})(d,d+x)}{(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\beta})(d,d+y))}.

(4.115)

Proof
If follows from (
4.105
) that

E(\widetilde{\eta}(y)-\widetilde{\eta}(x))(\widetilde{\eta}(w)-\widetilde{\eta}(z))=(p(y)-p(x))(q(w)-q(z))\leq 0.

(4.116)

Let

\widetilde{v}^{\beta}(x,y)=\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)-\frac{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,0)\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,y)}{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,0)}.

(4.117)

This is the covariance of the Gaussian process,
\widetilde{\xi}=\{\widetilde{\xi}(x),x\in R^{1}\}
where,

\widetilde{\xi}(x)=\widetilde{\eta}(x)-\frac{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,0)}{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,0)}\widetilde{\eta}(0).

(4.118)

Note that
\widetilde{\xi}(0)=0
.

Set,

(\overline{\sigma}^{\beta})^{2}(x,y):=E\left(\widetilde{\xi}(x)-\widetilde{\xi}(y)\right)^{2}=(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\beta})^{2}(x,y)-\frac{\left(\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,0)-\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,y)\right)^{2}}{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,0)}.

(4.119)

Lemma 4.11

For
x,y>0
,

(\overline{\sigma}^{\beta})^{2}(x,y)\sim(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\beta})^{2}(x,y)\sim\tau(x)|x-y|,\qquad\text{as $\,|x-y|\to 0$}.

(4.120)

When
0<y\leq x
,

\widetilde{v}^{\beta}(x,x)-\widetilde{v}^{\beta}(x,y)\sim C_{x}|x-y|,\qquad\text{as $\,|x-y|\to 0$},

(4.121)

where
C_{x}>0.

Proof
Consider (
4.119
) and note that,

\frac{\left(\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,0)-\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,y)\right)^{2}}{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,0)}=\frac{p(0)}{q(0)}(q(x)-q(y))^{2}.

(4.122)

When
0<y\leq x
,

\frac{p(0)}{q(0)}(q^{\prime}(x))^{2}(x-y)^{2}\leq\frac{\left(\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,0)-\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,y)\right)^{2}}{u^{\beta}(0,0)}\leq\frac{p(0)}{q(0)}(q^{\prime}(y))^{2}(x-y)^{2}.

(4.123)

Using this and (
4.109
) we get (
4.120
).

When
0<y\leq x
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{v}^{\beta}(x,x)-\widetilde{v}^{\beta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,x)-\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)-\frac{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,0)(\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,0)-\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,y))}{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,0)}

(4.124)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,x)-\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)-\frac{p(0)}{q(0)}q(x)(q(x)-q(y)).

As
|x-y|\to 0
this is,

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle q(x)p^{\prime}(x)|x-y|-\frac{p(0)}{q(0)}q(x)q^{\prime}(x)|x-y|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(p^{\prime}(x)-\frac{p(0)}{q(0)}q^{\prime}(x)\right)q(x)|x-y|,

where we use (
4.112
). Since
p^{\prime}>0
and

q^{\prime}>0
we get (
4.121
).

Lemma 4.12

The increments of
\widetilde{\xi}
are negatively correlated, i.e., for
0<x<y<z<w
,

E(\widetilde{\xi}(y)-\widetilde{\xi}(x))(\widetilde{\xi}(w)-\widetilde{\xi}(z))\leq 0.

(4.125)

Consequently, for
d,x,y\in T
where
0<x\leq y
,

\frac{E\left(\left(\widetilde{\xi}(d+x)-\widetilde{\xi}(d)\right)\left(\widetilde{\xi}(d+y)-\widetilde{\xi}(d)\right)\right)}{(\overline{\sigma})^{\beta}(d,d+x)(\overline{\sigma})^{\beta}(d,d+y)}\leq\frac{(\overline{\sigma})^{\beta}(d,d+x)}{(\overline{\sigma})^{\beta}(d,d+y))}.

(4.126)

Proof

\displaystyle\widetilde{\xi}(y)-\widetilde{\xi}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}(x)-\widetilde{\eta}(y)-\frac{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,y)-\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,x)}{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,0)}\widetilde{\eta}(0)

(4.127)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\xi}(w)-\widetilde{\xi}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}(w)-\widetilde{\eta}(z)-\frac{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,w)-\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,z)}{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,0)}\widetilde{\eta}(0)

Therefore, using Lemma
4.10
, the left–hand side
of (
4.125
) is equal to,

\displaystyle E(\widetilde{\eta}(y)-\widetilde{\eta}(x))(\widetilde{\eta}(w)-\widetilde{\eta}(z))-\frac{(\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,y)-\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,x))(\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,w)-\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,z))}{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,0)}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 0-\frac{p(0)}{q(0)}\left(q(y)-q(x)\right)\left(q(w)-q(z)\right)\leq 0.

(4.128)

Let

w(x,y)=s(x)\wedge s(y),\qquad x,y>0.

(4.129)

where
s\in C^{2}(R^{+}-\{0\})
and is positive and strictly increasing.
Therefore, for
x\leq y

w(x,x)-w(x,y)=0

(4.130)

and for
y\leq x

w(x,x)-w(x,y)=s(x)-s(y)\geq 0.

(4.131)

Obviously,
w(x,y)
is the covariance of a time changed Brownian motion
B=\{B(s(x)),x\in R^{+}-\{0\})\}
.
Therefore,

\widehat{\sigma}^{2}(x,y):=E\left(B(s(y))=B(s(x))\right)^{2}=|s(x)-s(y)|.

(4.132)

and

\widehat{\sigma}^{2}(x,y)\sim s^{\prime}(x)|x-y|,\qquad\text{as $|x-y|\to 0.$}\hskip 14.45377pt

(4.133)

Since
B
has independent increments we have:

Lemma 4.13

For
d,x,y\in T
where
0<x\leq y
,

\frac{E\left(\left(B(d+x)-B(d)\right)\left(B(d+y)-B(d)\right)\right)}{\widehat{\sigma}(d,d+x)\widehat{\sigma}(d,d+y)}=\frac{\widetilde{\sigma}(d,d+x)}{\widehat{\sigma}(d,d+y))}.

(4.134)

We now note that it follows from Lemmas
4.9
and
4.11
and (
4.133
) that we can take,

\varphi_{d}(|x-y|)=|x-y|

(4.135)

for
(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\beta})^{2}
,
(\overline{\sigma}^{\beta})^{2}
and
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
in the first line of (
2.26
) and, obviously (
2.27
) is satisfied. The second line of (
2.26
) for each of the different potentials follows from (
4.111
), (
4.121
), and (
4.131
).

The proof that (
3.7
) holds for the Gaussian processes with covariances that are the potentials that we are studying follows from the inequalities in Lemmas
4.10
and
4.13
and (
4.118
) exactly as in the proof of Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
.
This completes the proof of the lower bounds in Theorem
1.5
.

The proof of the upper bounds in Theorem
1.5
is exactly the same as the proof of the upper bounds in Theorem
1.4
. For the processes we are studying
\phi(x)=C|x|
, for some constant
C
.

5
Exact moduli of continuity for Markov local times

Let
Z=(\Omega,{\cal F}_{t},Z_{t},\theta_{t},P^{x})
be a Borel right process on a locally compact metric state space
(S,d)

strictly positive
0
–potential densities

\{v(x,y),x,y\in S\}
with respect to a
\sigma
–finite measure
m
. Let

L=\{L^{y}_{t}\,,\,(y,t)\in S\times R_{+}\}

denote the local times for

Z
normalized so that,

E^{x}\left(L^{y}_{\infty}\right)=v(x,y).

(5.1)

Let
X_{1/2}=\{X_{1/2}(y),y\in S\}
denote a
1/2
–permanental process with kernel
\{v(x,y),x,y\in S\}
.

We assume that
L
is jointly continuous on
S\times[0,\zeta)
, where
\zeta
is the lifetime of
Z

and that
X_{1/2}
is continuous almost surely.

The next theorem uses the Eisenbaum–Kaspi isomorphism theorem
[
5
]
and the local modulus of continuity of
X_{1/2}
to obtain the local modulus of continuity of
L
.

Theorem 5.1

Let
\phi
be a positive continuous real valued function. Suppose that for some
v\in(S,d)
,

\limsup_{u\to v}\frac{|X_{1/2}(u)-X_{1/2}(v)|}{\phi(d(u,v))}=\left(X_{1/2}(v)\right)^{1/2},\qquad a.s.

(5.2)

Then

\limsup_{u\to v}\frac{|L^{u}_{t}-L^{v}_{t}|}{\phi(d(u,v))}=\left(L^{v}_{t}\right)^{1/2},\qquad\text{for a.e. $t<\zeta$},

(5.3)

P^{x}
almost surely, for all
x\in S
.

We state the Eisenbaum–Kaspi theorem for the convenience of the reader:

Theorem 5.2
(Eisenbaum and Kaspi,
[
5
]
)

Let
Z=(\Omega,{\cal F}_{t},Z_{t},\theta_{t},P^{x})
be a Borel right process in
S
with

0
–potential density

v(x,y)
. Let
h_{x}(z)=v(z,x)
and assume that
h_{x}(z)>0
for all
x,z\in S
. Let

L=\{L^{y}_{t}\,,\,(y,t)\in S\times R_{+}\}

denote the local times for

Z
, normalized so that

E^{x}\left(L^{y}_{\infty}\right)=v(x,y),

(5.4)

as defined above. Let
X_{1/2}=\{X_{1/2}(y),y\in S\}
denote a
1/2
–permanental process with kernel
v(x,y)
. Then, for any
countable subset
D\subseteq S
,

\Big\{L^{u}_{\infty}+X_{1/2}(u),u\in D,P^{y/h_{y}}\times P_{X_{1/2}}\Big\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle law}}{{=}}\Big\{X_{1/2}(u),u\in D\,,\,\frac{2X_{1/2}(y)}{v(y,y)}P_{X_{1/2}}\Big\},

(5.5)

Equivalently, for all
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
in
S
and bounded
measurable functions

F
on
R^{n}_{+}
, for all

n
,

E^{y/h_{y}}E_{X_{1/2}}\left(F\left(L^{x_{i}}_{\infty}+X_{1/2}(x_{i})\right)\right)=E_{X_{1/2}}\left(\frac{2X_{1/2}(y)}{v(y,y)}F\left(X_{1/2}(x_{i})\right)\right),

(5.6)

where we use the notation
F(f(x_{i}))=F(f(x_{1}),\ldots,f(x_{n}))
.

The probability measures
P^{x/h_{y}}
are the probability measures for the
h_{y}
–transform of
Z
, see
[
19
, (62.24)]
or
[
6
, (2.22)]
. In this proof we express them using Azema’s formula
[
1
]
, as

P^{x/h_{y}}(A)=\frac{1}{h_{y}(x)}P^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{A}\circ k_{s}\,d_{s}L_{s}^{y}\right),\qquad A\in\Omega,

(5.7)

where
k_{s}
is the killing operator for
Z
, i.e.,
k_{s}\omega(r)=\omega(r)
when
r<s
, and
k_{s}\omega(r)=\Delta
when
r\geq s
.

Proof of Theorem
5.1

Let
F(\mathcal{C})
be a set of functions on a countable dense subset
\mathcal{C}
of
S
and let,

A=\Big\{g\in F(\mathcal{C})\,\Big|\,\limsup_{u\in\mathcal{C},u\to v}\frac{|g(u)-g(v)|}{\phi(d(u,v))}=\left({g(v)}\right)^{1/2}\Big\}.

(5.8)

Since
X_{1/2}
is continuous and satisfies (
5.2
) it follows that,

P_{X_{1/2}}\left(X_{1/2}\in A\right)=1.

(5.9)

Note that it follows from (
1.1
) that
E_{X_{1/2}}\left(X_{1/2}(y)\right)=v(y,y)/2
.
Therefore by Theorem
5.2
, for almost all
\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega_{X_{1/2}}
, the probability space of
X_{1/2}
,

\limsup_{u\in\mathcal{C},u\to v}\Bigg|\frac{L^{u}_{\infty}-L^{v}_{\infty}}{\phi(d(u,v))}\!+\frac{X_{1/2}(u,\omega^{\prime})-X_{1/2}(v,\omega^{\prime})}{\phi(d(u,v))}\Bigg|=\,\left({L^{v}_{\infty}+X_{1/2}(v,\omega^{\prime})}\right)^{1/2},

(5.10)

P^{y/h_{y}}
almost surely, for all
y\in S
.
Using (
5.9
) we see that for almost all

\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega_{X}
with respect to
P_{X}
,

\limsup_{u\in\mathcal{C},u\to v}\frac{|X_{1/2}(u,\omega^{\prime})-X_{1/2}(v,\omega^{\prime})|}{\phi(d(u,v))}=\left(X_{1/2}(v,\omega^{\prime})\right)^{1/2}.

(5.11)

As we point out immediately after the statement of Theorem
1.2
,
X_{1/2}(d)
is the square of a mean zero Gaussian random variable. Consequently, it takes values arbitrarily close to 0 with probability greater than 0. Therefore it follows from (
5.10
) and the triangle inequality that,

\limsup_{u\in\mathcal{C},u\to v}\Bigg|\frac{L^{u}_{\infty}-L^{v}_{\infty}}{\phi(d(u,v))}\Bigg|=\left(L^{v}_{\infty}\right)^{1/2},

(5.12)

P^{y/h_{y}}
almost surely, for all
y\in S
.
(A similar argument is used in
[
9
, Theorem 9.5.1]
.)

For
t\in R^{1}_{+}
, including
t=\infty
, set

D_{t}=\Bigg\{\limsup_{u\in\mathcal{C},u\to v}\Bigg|\frac{L^{u}_{t}-L^{v}_{t}}{\phi(d(u,v))}\Bigg|=\,\left(L^{v}_{t}\right)^{1/2}\Bigg\}.

(5.13)

It follows from (
5.12
) that

P^{y/h_{y}}\left(D_{\infty}\right)=1.

(5.14)

We now show that (
5.14
) implies that

\limsup_{u\in\mathcal{C},\,u\to v}\frac{|L^{u}_{s}-L^{v}_{s}|}{\phi(d(u,v))}=\left(L_{s}^{v}\right)^{1/2},

(5.15)

for almost every
s<\zeta
,
P^{x}
almost surely, for all
x\in S
. Then, since by hypothesis
L
is jointly continuous on
S\times[0,\zeta)
,
we get (
5.3
).

To obtain (
5.15
), we first note that by (
5.7
) and (
5.14
),

\frac{1}{h_{y}(y)}P^{y}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{\{D^{c}_{\infty}\}}\circ k_{s}\,d_{s}L_{s}^{y}\right)=0.

(5.16)

Since
1_{\{D^{c}_{\infty}\}}\circ k_{s}=1_{\{D^{c}_{s}\}}
and
h_{y}(y)>0
this implies that,

P^{y}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{\{D^{c}_{s}\}}\,d_{s}L^{y}_{s}\right)=0.

(5.17)

Let
T_{y}
denote the first hitting time of
y
and
\theta_{T_{y}}
denote the shift operator. Then for any
x\in S
,

\displaystyle P^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{\{D^{c}_{s}\}}\,d_{s}L^{y}_{s}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{x}\left(\int_{T_{y}}^{\infty}1_{\{D^{c}_{s}\}}\,d_{s}L^{y}_{s}\right)

(5.18)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{x}\left(\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{\{D^{c}_{s}\}}\,d_{s}L^{y}_{s}\right)\circ\theta_{T_{y}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{x}\left(T_{y}<\infty\right)P^{y}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{\{D^{c}_{s}\}}\,d_{s}L^{y}_{s}\right)=0,

by (
5.17
).

We now examine (
5.18
).
We first note that by the occupation density formula
[
6
, (2.28)]
,

\int_{S}L^{y}_{s}\,dm(y)=s,\hskip 14.45377pt\forall s<\zeta\quad a.s.

(5.19)

Therefore, using (
5.18
) we have,

P^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\zeta}1_{\{D^{c}_{s}\}}\,ds\right)=\int_{S}P^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{\{D^{c}_{s}\}}\,d_{s}L^{y}_{s}\right)\,dm(y)=0.

(5.20)

Here we have used the fact that for any bounded measurable

f(\omega,s)

P^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\zeta}f(\omega,s)\,ds\right)=\int_{S}P^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}f(\omega,s)\,d_{s}L^{y}_{s}\right)\,dm(y).

(5.21)

To see this, as in the proof of Fubini’s theorem, it suffices to prove it for
f(\omega,s)
of the form
g(\omega)h(s)
, in which case, using Fubini’s theorem, (
5.21
) becomes

P^{x}\left(g(\omega)\int_{0}^{\zeta}h(s)\,ds\right)=P^{x}\left(g(\omega)\int_{S}\int_{0}^{\infty}h(s)\,d_{s}L^{y}_{s}\,dm(y)\right).

(5.22)

Therefore, to obtain (
5.21
)
it only remains to show that,

\int_{0}^{\zeta}h(s)\,ds=\int_{S}\int_{0}^{\infty}h(s)\,d_{s}L^{y}_{s}\,dm(y),\qquad a.s.

(5.23)

Moreover, to show this it suffices to verify it for functions of the form
h(s)=1_{[0,a]}(s)
with
a<\zeta
. This is obvious, since by (
5.19
),

a=\int_{S}L^{y}_{a}\,dm(y)=\int_{S}\int_{0}^{a}\,d_{s}L^{y}_{s}\,dm(y)=\int_{S}\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{[0,a]}(s)\,d_{s}L^{y}_{s}\,dm(y).

(5.24)

Using (
5.20
) we now see that,

\int_{0}^{\zeta}1_{\{D_{s}^{c}\}}\,ds=0,\qquad P^{x}\quad a.s.

(5.25)

Therefore, for
P^{x}
almost every
\omega
we have
\omega\in D_{s}
for almost every
s\in[0,\zeta(\omega))
,
which is (
5.15
).

Remark 5.1

Considering the importance of Azéma’s formula in the proof of Theorem
5.1
, we show that Azéma’s formula,

P^{x/h_{y}}(A)=\frac{1}{h_{y}(x)}P^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{A}\circ k_{s}\,d_{s}L_{s}^{y}\right),

(5.26)

satisfies the more common definition of
P^{y/h_{y}}(A)
; i.e., for
A_{t}\in\mathcal{F}_{t}
,

P^{x/h_{y}}(A_{t}1_{\{t<\zeta\}})=\frac{1}{h_{y}(x)}P^{x}\left(A_{t}h_{y}(Z_{t})\right).

(5.27)

This follows since

\left(A_{t}1_{\{t<\zeta\}}\right)\circ k_{s}=A_{t}1_{\{t<s\wedge\zeta\}},

(5.28)

and
\int_{t}^{\infty}\,d_{s}L_{s}^{y}=L_{\infty}^{y}-L_{t}^{y}=L_{\infty}^{y}\circ\theta_{t}
,
so that

\displaystyle P^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{A_{t}}\circ k_{s}\,d_{s}L_{s}^{y}\right)=P^{x}\left(1_{A_{t}}\int_{t}^{\infty}\,d_{s}L_{s}^{y}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=P^{x}\left(1_{A_{t}}L_{\infty}^{y}\circ\theta_{t}\right)=P^{x}\left(1_{A_{t}}E^{Z_{t}}(L_{\infty}^{y})\right)=P^{x}\left(A_{t}h_{y}(Z_{t})\right).

The assumption in Theorem
5.2
that
Z
has a finite
0
–potential density implies that
Z
is transient. The next theorem gives the local modulus of continuity for recurrent processes.

Theorem 5.3

Let
\widetilde{Z}\!=\!(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\cal F}_{t},\widetilde{Z}_{t},\widetilde{P}^{x})
be a recurrent Borel right process with locally compact metric state space
(S,d)
, and
strictly positive
p
–potential density

v(x,y)
with respect to a
\sigma
–finite measure
m
on
S
, where
p>0
. Let

\widetilde{L}=\{\widetilde{L}^{y}_{t}\,,\,(y,t)\in S\times R_{+}\}

denote the local times for

\widetilde{Z}
, normalized so that

\widetilde{E}^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-pt}d_{t}\widetilde{L}^{y}_{t}\right)=v(x,y),

(5.29)

and assume that
\widetilde{L}
is jointly continuous on
S\times R_{+}
.

Let
X_{1/2}=\{X_{1/2}(y),y\in S\}
denote a
1/2
–permanental process with kernel
v(x,y)
and assume that
X_{1/2}
is continuous almost surely. Then,
if (
5.2
) holds for
X_{1/2}
then (
5.3
) holds for
\widetilde{L}
for almost every
t\in R_{+}
,
\widetilde{P}^{x}
almost surely, for all
x\in S
.

Proof
Let
Z=(\Omega,{\cal F}_{t},Z_{t},\theta_{t},P^{x})
be a Borel right process obtained by killing
\widetilde{Z}
at an independent
exponential time
\lambda
with mean
1/p
. Since the 0–potential densities for
Z
are the
p
–potential densities
for
\widetilde{Z}
,
Z
is transient
Borel right process with
continuous strictly positive 0–potential densities
v(x,y)
.

The process
Z
is defined on a product space
\Omega=\widetilde{\Omega}\times[0,\infty)
with probability
P^{x}=\widetilde{P}^{x}\times\overline{P}
where
\overline{P}
has probability density
pe^{-ps}

and

\begin{array}[]{ll }Z_{t}(\omega,s)=\widetilde{Z}_{t}(\omega),&\qquad t<s\\
Z_{t}(\omega,s)=\Delta,&\qquad t\geq s\end{array}.

(5.30)

Let
\zeta(\omega,s)
denote the lifetime and
L^{y}_{t}
the local time of
Z
. We see from (
5.30
) that
\zeta(\omega,s)=s
which implies that
L^{y}_{t}(\omega,s)=\widetilde{L}_{t\wedge s}^{y}(\omega)
. (This is
[
9
, Remark 3.6.4, (3)]
).
Therefore, by Theorem
5.1
,

\limsup_{u\to v}\frac{|L^{u}_{t}(\omega,s)-L^{v}_{t}(\omega,s)|}{\phi(d(u,v))}=\left(L^{v}_{t}(\omega,s)\right)^{1/2},\qquad\text{for a.e. $t<s$},

(5.31)

\widetilde{P}^{x}\times pe^{-ps}\,ds
almost surely, for all
x\in S
. Using Fubini’s Theorem we then see that for a.e.
s
,

\limsup_{u\to v}\frac{|L^{u}_{t}(\omega,s)-L^{v}_{t}(\omega,s)|}{\phi(d(u,v))}=\left(L^{v}_{t}(\omega,s)\right)^{1/2},\qquad\text{for a.e. $t<s$},

(5.32)

\widetilde{P}^{x}
almost surely, for all
x\in S
. Using this for a sequence
s_{n}\to\infty
shows that (
5.3
) holds for
\widetilde{L}
for almost every
t
,
\widetilde{P}^{x}
almost surely, for all
x\in S
.

An analogue of Theorem
5.1
also holds for the uniform modulus of continuity:

Theorem 5.4

Let
\xi
be a positive continuous real valued function. Suppose that there exists a compact set
K\subset S
such that,

\lim_{h\to 0}\sup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u,v\in K}}{{d(u,v)\leq h}}}\frac{|X_{1/2}(u)-X_{1/2}(v)|}{\xi(d(u,v))}=\sup_{v\in K}\left(X_{1/2}(v)\right)^{1/2},\qquad a.s.

(5.33)

and for all
\epsilon>0
,

P\left(\sup_{v\in K}X_{1/2}(v)<\epsilon\right)>0.

(5.34)

Then,

\lim_{h\to 0}\sup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u,v\in K}}{{d(u,v)\leq h}}}\frac{|L^{u}_{t}-L^{v}_{t}|}{\xi(d(u,v))}=\sup_{v\in K}\left(L^{v}_{t}\right)^{1/2},\qquad\text{for a.e. $t<\zeta$},

(5.35)

P^{x}
almost surely, for all
x\in S
.

Proof
The proof, with obvious modifications, is exactly the same except for one point. In the proof of Theorem
5.1
we use the fact that
X_{1/2}(d)
is the square of a mean zero Gaussian random variable to see that for all
\epsilon>0
,
P\left(X_{1/2}(d)<\epsilon\right)>0
. Here, in (
5.34
), we add the analogue of this for
\sup_{v\in K}X_{1/2}(v)
as an hypothesis.

Similarly, under the conditions of Corollary
5.3
we can show that
if (
5.33
) and (
5.34
) hold for
X_{1/2}
then (
5.35
) holds for
\widetilde{L}
for almost every
t
,
\widetilde{P}^{x}
almost surely, for all
x\in S
.

At this point we can not show that there are any 1/2–permanental processes that are not symmetric that satisfy (
5.33
).

6
Partial rebirthing of transient Borel right processes

In this section we apply Theorem
5.1
and the LILs for the permanental processes in Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
to obtain LILs for the local times of related Borel right processes.

Let
T
a be locally compact set with a countable base.
Let
Y=(\Omega,{\cal F}_{t},Y_{t},\theta_{t},\newline
P^{x})
be a transient Borel right process with state space
T
, and continuous strictly positive potential densities
u(x,y)
with respect to some
\sigma
–finite measure
m
on
T
. Let
\mu
be a finite positive measure on
T
with mass
|\mu|=\mu(T)
.
We call the function

f(x)=\int_{T}u(y,x)\,d\mu(y)

(6.1)

a left potential for
Y
. (It follows from
[
2
, Chapter 1, Proposition 14]
that for the Markov processes
Y
considered in Theorems
1.2
, all integrable excessive functions are of this form.)
The following is
[
13
, Theorem 6.1]
.

Theorem 6.1

Let
Y=(\Omega,{\cal F}_{t},Y_{t},\theta_{t},P^{x})
be a transient Borel right process with state space
T
, as above. Then for any left potential
f
for
Y
, there exists a transient Borel right process

\widetilde{Z}\!=\!(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\cal F}_{t},\widetilde{Z}_{t},\widetilde{\theta}_{t},\widetilde{P}^{x})
with state space
S=T\cup\{\ast\}
, where
\ast
is an isolated point, such that
\widetilde{Z}
has potential densities,

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}(x,y)=u(x,y)+f(y),\hskip 14.45377ptx,y\in T

(6.2)

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}(\ast,y)=f(y),\hskip 14.45377pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 14.45377pt\widetilde{u}(x,\ast)=\widetilde{u}(\ast,\ast)=1,

with respect to the measure
\widetilde{m}
on
S
which is equal to
m
on
T
and assigns
a unit mass to
\ast
.

Note that in the proof of Theorem
1.1
we actually show that there exists a
1/2
–permanental process
X_{1/2}=\{X_{1/2}(y)\,,\,y\in S\}
with kernel
\widetilde{u}(x,y)
.
All the permanental processes considered in Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
are actually defined on
S=T\cup\{*\}
and we can extend the metric on
T
to
S
so that
*
is an isolated point.

Remark 6.1

The process
\widetilde{Z}
is a partial rebirthing of
Y
. When
\widetilde{Z}
starts in
T
, it proceeds just like
Y
until the lifetime of
Y
. It then goes to the isolated point
*
. It remains in
*
for an independent exponential time with mean,

\frac{1}{1+|\mu|}.

(6.3)

Then it returns to
T
with law
\mu/(1+|\mu|)
. (The probability that
\widetilde{Z}
returns to
T
is
|\mu|/(1+|\mu|)
. With probability
1/(1+|\mu|)
it goes to the cemetery state.) We continue to repeat this procedure. At each stage the process
\widetilde{Z}
is “reborn”, but not with probability
1
. We call this a partial rebirthing. (This is explained in greater detail in the proof of

[
13
, Theorem 6.1]
.)

The analogues of
u(x,y)
, in (
6.2
), in the kernels of the permanental processes in Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
are symmetric. The next lemma uses this property.

Lemma 6.1

Let
Y
be a strongly symmetric transient Borel right process with transition densities
p_{s}(x,y)
with respect to a
\sigma
–finite measure
m
. Then
f=\{f(x),x\in T\}
, in (
6.1
), is an excessive function for
Y
.

Proof
We show that

E^{x}\left(f(Y_{t})\right)\uparrow f(x),\quad\text{as $t\downarrow 0$,}\quad\forall\,x\in T.

(6.4)

The condition that
Y
is strongly symmetric implies that
u(x,y)=u(y,x)
, for all
x,y\in T
. We also note that,

u(x,y)=\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{s}(x,y)\,ds.

(6.5)

Therefore, for any finite measure
\mu
,

f(x)=\int_{T}u(y,x)\,d\mu(y)=\int_{T}u(x,y)\,d\mu(y)=\int_{T}\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{s}(x,y)\,ds\,d\mu(y).

(6.6)

We have,

\displaystyle E^{x}\left(f(Y_{t})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{T}p_{t}(x,z)f(z)\,dm(z)

(6.7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{T}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{T}p_{t}(x,z)p_{s}(z,y)\,dm(z)\right)\,ds\,\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{T}\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{s+t}(x,y)\,ds\,\,d\mu(y)=\int_{T}\int_{t}^{\infty}p_{s}(x,y)\,ds\,\,d\mu(y).

Since,

\int_{T}\int_{t}^{\infty}p_{s}(x,y)\,ds\,\,d\mu(y)\uparrow\int_{T}\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{s}(x,y)\,ds\,d\mu(y)=f(x),\quad\mbox{as }t\downarrow 0,

(6.8)

we get (
6.4
).

The next theorem is an immediate application of Theorem
5.1
. It gives LILs for the local times of the partially rebirthed Markov processes
\widetilde{Z}
in Theorem
6.1
. The spaces
S
and
T
are as defined in Theorem
6.1
.

Theorem 6.2

Let
\widetilde{L}=\{\widetilde{L}_{t}^{y},(y,t)\in S\times R_{+}^{1}\}
be the local times of
\widetilde{Z}
, normalized so that

\widetilde{E}^{x}\left(\widetilde{L}^{y}_{\infty}\right)=\widetilde{u}(x,y).

(6.9)

If a permanental process with kernel,

\widetilde{u}_{T}(x,y)=u(x,y)+f(y),\hskip 14.45377ptx,y\in T,

(6.10)

satisfies the conditions in any of Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
,
then
\widetilde{L}_{t}^{y}
is jointly continuous on
S\times[0,\zeta)
and for any
d\in T
for which the LIL for the permanental process holds,

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|\widetilde{L}_{t}^{d+x}-\widetilde{L}_{t}^{d}|}{(2\sigma^{2}(d+x,d)\log\log 1/|x|)^{1/2}}=\left({2\widetilde{L}_{t}^{d}}\right)^{1/2}\qquad a.s.\mbox{ for a.e. }t<\zeta,

(6.11)

\widetilde{P}^{v}
almost surely, for all
v\in T
, where

\sigma^{2}(x,y)=u(x,x)+u(y,y)-2u(x,y).

(6.12)

(The relationship between
u
and
\widetilde{u}
is given in (
6.2
).)

Proof
This follows from Theorem
5.1
.
Since
\ast
is an isolated point, we only need to take the
\limsup
over
x\in T
.
That
\widetilde{L}
is jointly continuous on
S\times[0,\zeta)
, follows by
[
10
, Theorems 1.1 and 1.2]
.
The fact that the hypotheses on
u
and
f
imply the conditions in these theorems is well known; see e.g.,
[
9
, Chapter 6]
.

(Note that the terms that take the place of
\sigma^{2}(d+x,d)
in (
1.22
), (
1.34
), (
1.45
), (
1.51
) and (
1.55
) are asymptotically equivalent to
\sigma^{2}(d+x,d)
for the relevant potential densities
u(x,y)
. This is shown in the proofs of Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
.)

Remark 6.2

The measure
\mu
that determines the partially rebirthed Markov process
\widetilde{Z}
does not influence the denominator on left–hand side of (
6.11
). It is the same for all
\mu
and consequently for all potentials
f
in (
6.2
). The measure
\mu
, through
f
, influences the local times
\widetilde{L}_{t}^{y}
, as one can
see in (
6.9
).

There are six different functions
u(x,y)

in Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
and nine sets of conditions on
T
and
f
. (Take
g\equiv 1
.) To clarify Theorem
6.2
we give three specific examples.

Example 6.1

Consider the permanental processes in Theorem

, I. and the corresponding partially rebirthed process
\widetilde{Z}
in Theorem
6.1
for some finite positive measure

. The process

is a rebirthing of a symmetric Lévy process killed at the end of an exponential time with mean
1/\beta
as described prior the the statement of Theorem

. In this case

,

in (

) is given by

in (

) and
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)
is given by (
1.20
) and (
1.28
) for any
\beta>0
. The function
f
in
(
6.2
) is a
C^{1}(R^{1})
excessive function for
Y
in Theorem
1.2
.
Note that the requirement that
f\in C^{1}(R^{1})
imposes restrictions on the measures
\mu
that can be used for the rebirthing. Similar restrictions apply to all the applications of Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
and Theorem
6.1
in Theorem
6.2
.

The following corollary is an application of Theorem
6.2
. As in Theorem
6.2
the spaces
S
and
T
are as defined in Theorem
6.1
. In this case
T=R^{1}

Corollary 6.1

Let
\widetilde{L}=\{\widetilde{L}_{t}^{y},(y,t)\in S\times R_{+}^{1}\}
be the local times of
\widetilde{Z}
, normalized so that

(6.14)

almost surely, for all

.

Example 6.2

Corollary 6.2

Let
\widetilde{L}=\{\widetilde{L}_{t}^{y},(y,t)\in S\times R_{+}^{1}\}
be the local times of
\widetilde{Z}
, normalized so that

\widetilde{E}^{x}\left(\widetilde{L}^{y}_{\infty}\right)=\widetilde{u}(x,y).

(6.15)

Then the local times of
\widetilde{Z}
are jointly continuous on
S\times[0,\zeta)
.
When
f\in C^{1}(R^{1}-\{0\})
and
(\sigma^{0})^{2}
satisfies conditions
(i),(ii)
and
(iii)
in Theorem
1.4
,
then for any any
d\neq 0
,

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|\widetilde{L}_{t}^{d+x}-\widetilde{L}_{t}^{d}|}{(2(\sigma^{0})^{2}(x)\log\log 1/|x|)^{1/2}}=\left({2\widetilde{L}_{t}^{d}}\right)^{1/2}\qquad a.s.\mbox{ for a.e. }t<\zeta,

(6.16)

\widetilde{P}^{v}
almost surely, for all
v\in T
.
Furthermore,

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|\widetilde{L}_{\infty}^{d+x}-\widetilde{L}_{\infty}^{d}|}{(2(\sigma^{0})^{2}(x)\log\log 1/|x|)^{1/2}}=\left({2\widetilde{L}_{\infty}^{d}}\right)^{1/2}\qquad a.s.,

(6.17)

\widetilde{P}^{v}
almost surely, for all
v\in R^{1}-\{0\}
.

Proof
We only need to prove (
6.17
). This follows because in this case the Markov process is killed when it hits
0
. Since the paths are right continuous, for any
d\neq 0
, there is a random interval
I=[s,\zeta)
during which the path is not in some neighborhood, say B, of
d
. Consequently, for any
t\in I
and any
d+x\in B
, we have that
\widetilde{L}_{t}^{d+x}=\widetilde{L}_{\infty}^{d+x}
, so (
6.17
) follows from (
6.16
).

Remark 6.3

The LILs in (
1.66
) and (
1.67
) are given in (
6.22
) and (
6.23
) with, obviously,
d
replaced by
x_{0}
. We just have to check that the conditions on
f
are satisfied. We do this in Remark
9.1
.

There are five more sets
of conditions on
u(x,y)
,
T
and
f
in Theorems

–
1.5
for which specific realizations of (
