)
can be written out. We leave this to the interested reader.

7
Rebirthing transient Borel right processes

In this Section we apply Corollary
5.3
to recurrent Markov processes and the LILs for permanental processes in Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
and
1.5
to obtain LILs for the local times of certain fully rebirthed Borel right processes.

In Theorem
7.1
we obtain a general result about the potential densities of fully rebirthed Borel right processes:
Let
S
a be locally compact space with a countable base.
Let
{\cal Y}=(\Omega,{\cal F}_{t},{\cal Y}_{t},\theta_{t},P^{x})
be a transient Borel right process with state space
S
and continuous strictly positive
p-
potential densities

\overline{u}^{p}=\{\overline{u}^{p}(x,y),x,y\in S\},

(7.1)

with respect to some
\sigma
–finite positive measure
m
on
S
. Let
\zeta=\inf\{t\,|\,{\cal Y}_{t}=\Delta\}
, where
\Delta
is the cemetery state for
{\cal Y}
and assume that
\zeta<\infty
almost surely. We do not require that
{\cal Y}

is symmetric.

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
S
. We modify
{\cal Y}
so that instead of going to
\Delta
at the end of its lifetime it is immediately “reborn” with measure
\mu
. (I.e., the process goes to the set
B\subset S
with probability
\mu(B)
, after which it continues to evolve the way
{\cal Y}
did, being reborn with probability
\mu
each time it dies.) We denote this rebirthed process by
\widetilde{Z}\!=\!(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\cal F}_{t},\widetilde{Z}_{t},\widetilde{\theta}_{t},\widetilde{P}^{x})
. To emphasize the parameters that determine
\widetilde{Z}
we sometimes write
\widetilde{Z}=\widetilde{Z}(\overline{u}^{p},m,\mu)
.

Theorem 7.1

The process

\widetilde{Z}=\widetilde{Z}(\overline{u}^{p},m,\mu)
is a recurrent Borel right process with state space
S
and
p
–potential densities,

w^{p}(x,y)=\overline{u}^{p}(x,y)+\left(\frac{1}{p}-\int_{S}\overline{u}^{p}(x,y)\,dm(y)\right)\frac{f(y)}{\|f\|_{1}},

(7.2)

with respect to
m
, where

f(y)=\int_{S}\overline{u}^{p}(x,y)\,d\mu(x),

(7.3)

and the
L_{1}
norm is taken with respect to
m
.

Proof of Theorem
7.1
It is a classical result that
\widetilde{Z}
is a recurrent Borel right process. (See, e.g.,
[
17
, Theorem 1]
or
[
19
, (14.17)]
.) We continue to show that it has the potential density in (
7.2
).

We use the following notation. For measurable functions
h
on
S
we define the
p
–th potential operator,

\overline{U}^{p}h(x)=\int_{S}\overline{u}^{p}(x,y)h(y)\,dm(y).

(7.4)

As a probabilistic statement this is

\overline{U}^{p}h(x)=E^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-pt}h\left({\cal Y}_{t}\right)\,dt\right)=E^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\zeta}e^{-pt}h\left({\cal Y}_{t}\right)\,dt\right),

(7.5)

as in (
1.9
).
For positive measures
\mu
on
S
we define,

\mu h=\int_{S}h(x)\,d\mu(x).

(7.6)

Note that by (
7.4
),

\mu\overline{U}^{p}h=\int_{S}\int_{S}\overline{u}^{p}(x,y)h(y)\,dm(y)\,d\mu(x).

(7.7)

In particular, when
h\equiv 1
, (
7.4
) is

\overline{U}^{p}1(x)=\int_{S}\overline{u}^{p}(x,y)\,dm(y),

(7.8)

and (
7.7
) is

\mu\overline{U}^{p}1=\int_{S}\int_{S}\overline{u}^{p}(x,y)\,dm(y)\,d\mu(x).

(7.9)

We now take
h
be bounded and consider
W^{p}
, the
p
–th potential operator for
\widetilde{Z}
.
For any
x\in S
,

\displaystyle W^{p}h(x)=\widetilde{E}^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-pt}h\left(\widetilde{Z}_{t}\right)\,dt\right)

(7.10)

\displaystyle\quad=\widetilde{E}^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\zeta_{1}}e^{-pt}h\left(\widetilde{Z}_{t}\right)\,dt\right)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\widetilde{E}^{x}\left(\int_{\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}}^{\widetilde{\zeta}_{n+1}}e^{-pt}h\left(\widetilde{Z}_{t}\right)\,dt\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\widetilde{E}^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\zeta}e^{-pt}h\left(\widetilde{Z}_{t}\right)\,dt\right)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\widetilde{E}^{x}\left(e^{-p\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}}\int_{0}^{\zeta_{n+1}}e^{-pt}h\left(\widetilde{Z}_{t}\circ\widetilde{\theta}_{\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}}\right)\,dt\right).

where
\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\zeta_{j}
, and
\{\zeta_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
are the consecutive lifetimes of the rebirthed process, so that
\zeta_{n+1}=\zeta\circ\widetilde{\theta}_{\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}}
.

Using the fact that
Z
is a Markov process we have,

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}^{x}\left(e^{-p\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}}\int_{0}^{\zeta_{n+1}}e^{-pt}h\left(Z_{t}\circ\widetilde{\theta}_{\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}}\right)\,dt\right)

(7.11)

\displaystyle\qquad=\widetilde{E}^{x}\left(e^{-p\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}}\left(\int_{0}^{\zeta}e^{-pt}h\left({\cal Y}_{t}\right)\,dt\right)\circ\widetilde{\theta}_{\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\widetilde{E}^{x}\left(e^{-p\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}}\widetilde{E}^{\widetilde{Z}_{\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}}}\left(\int_{0}^{\zeta}e^{-pt}h\left({\cal Y}_{t}\right)\,dt\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\widetilde{E}^{x}\left(e^{-p\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}}\right)\widetilde{E}^{\mu}\left(\int_{0}^{\zeta}e^{-pt}h\left({\cal Y}_{t}\right)\,dt\right),

where we use the fact that
\widetilde{Z}_{\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}}
has probability distribution
\mu
and is independent of
\widetilde{\cal F}_{\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}}
. We also use the notation that for any random variable
\cal Z
on
\widetilde{\Omega}
,

\widetilde{E}^{\mu}\left(\cal Z\right)=\int\widetilde{E}^{y}\left(\cal Z\right)\,d\mu(y).

(7.12)

Note that,

\widetilde{E}^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\zeta}e^{-pt}h\left(\widetilde{Z}_{t}\right)\,dt\right)=E^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\zeta}e^{-pt}h\left({\cal Y}_{t}\right)\,dt\right),

(7.13)

and

\widetilde{E}^{\mu}\left(\int_{0}^{\zeta}e^{-pt}h\left(\widetilde{Z}_{t}\right)\,dt\right)=E^{\mu}\left(\int_{0}^{\zeta}e^{-pt}h\left({\cal Y}_{t}\right)\,dt\right).

(7.14)

Using the Markov property and induction, in a similar manner we see that for
n\geq 1
,

\widetilde{E}^{x}(e^{-p\widetilde{\zeta}_{n}})=E^{x}\left(e^{-p\zeta}\right)\widetilde{E}^{\mu}\left(e^{-p\widetilde{\zeta}_{n-1}}\right)=E^{x}\left(e^{-p\zeta}\right)\left(E^{\mu}\left(e^{-p\zeta}\right)\right)^{n-1}.

(7.15)

Note that by simple integration and (
7.5
),

E^{x}\left(e^{-p\zeta}\right)=1-pE^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\zeta}e^{-pt}\,dt\right)=1-p\overline{U}^{p}1(x),

(7.16)

and

E^{\mu}\left(e^{-p\zeta}\right)=1-p\mu\overline{U}^{p}1,

(7.17)

which implies, in particular, that
p\mu\overline{U}^{p}1<1
.
Using this (
7.11
), (
7.13
) and (
7.14
) in (
7.10
)
we have,

\displaystyle W^{p}h(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\zeta}e^{-pt}h\left({\cal Y}_{t}\right)\,dt\right)

(7.18)

\displaystyle\qquad+E^{x}\left(e^{-p\zeta}\right)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(E^{\mu}\left(e^{-p\zeta}\right)\right)^{n-1}E^{\mu}\left(\int_{0}^{\zeta}e^{-pt}h\left({\cal Y}_{t}\right)\,dt\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{U}^{p}h(x)+\frac{E^{x}\left(e^{-p\zeta}\right)}{1-E^{\mu}\left(e^{-p\zeta}\right)}\mu\overline{U}^{p}h,

where we use the notation in (
7.4
)–(
7.7
).
Consequently,

W^{p}h(x)=\overline{U}^{p}h(x)+\left(1-p\overline{U}^{p}1(x)\right)\frac{\mu\overline{U}^{p}h}{p\mu\overline{U}^{p}1}.

(7.19)

Since this is true for all
h
we see that (
7.2
) follows by using (
7.4
), (
7.8
) and (
7.9
).

Lemma 7.1

The function

g(x)=1-p\int_{S}\overline{u}^{p}(x,y)\,dm(y)

(7.20)

is an excessive function for the process
obtained by killing
{\cal Y}
at the end of an independent exponential time with mean
1/p
.

Proof
It follows from (
7.8
) and (
7.16
) that
g\geq 0
. To complete the proof we show that,

e^{-pt}E^{x}\left(g\left({\cal Y}_{t}\right)\right)\uparrow g(x),\qquad\mbox{as }t\downarrow 0.

(7.21)

It follows from (
7.8
) and (
7.16
) that to show this it suffices to show that
E^{x}\left(e^{-p\zeta}\right)
is a
p
–excessive function for
{\cal Y}
. I.e., that,

e^{-pt}E^{x}\left(E^{{\cal Y}_{t}}\left(e^{-p\zeta}\right)\right)\uparrow E^{x}\left(e^{-p\zeta}\right),\quad\mbox{as }t\downarrow 0.

(7.22)

For
t<\zeta
we have
\zeta\circ\theta_{t}=\zeta-t
; see
[
9
, (2.137)]
. Consequently,

\displaystyle E^{x}\left(e^{-p\zeta},t<\zeta\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-pt}E^{x}\left(e^{-p\zeta\circ\theta_{t}},t<\zeta\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-pt}E^{x}\left(E^{{\cal Y}_{t}}\left(e^{-p\zeta}\right)\right)

by the strong Markov property. This gives(
7.22
).

We now use Theorem
7.1
along with Theorem
5.3
and Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
and
1.5
to obtain LILs for the local times
\widetilde{L}=\{\widetilde{L}_{t}^{y},(y,t)\in S\times(0,\infty)\}
of several fully rebirthed Markov processes. In particular whenever
\{\overline{u}^{p}(x,y),x,y\in T\}
is any of the symmetric potentials in Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
or
1.5
and it and
f
and
g
satisfy the conditions in the relevant theorem we have,

\limsup_{x\to 0}\frac{|\widetilde{L}_{t}^{d+x}-\widetilde{L}_{t}^{d}|}{\left(2(\overline{\sigma}^{p})^{2}\left(d+x,d\right)\log\log 1/|x|\right)^{1/2}}=\left(\widetilde{L}_{t}^{d}\right)^{1/2},\qquad\text{ for a.e. t},

(7.23)

\widetilde{P}^{v}
almost surely, for all
d
for which the relevant result in Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
or
1.5
hold, where,

(\overline{\sigma}^{p})^{2}\left(d+x,d\right)=\overline{u}^{p}(d+x,d+x)+\overline{u}^{p}(d,d)-2\overline{u}^{p}(d+x,d).

(7.24)

We give two broad examples:

Example 7.1

Let
Y=\{Y_{t},t\in R_{+}^{1}\}
to be a strongly symmetric Borel right process with locally compact state space
S
and
\gamma
–potential densities
u^{\gamma}(x,y)
with respect to some
\sigma
–finite measure
m
on
S
. Assume that
Y
has a Markov transition semigroup
P_{t}
, so that in particular,
P_{t}(x,S)=1
for all
t
and all
x\in S
. Note that for all
\gamma>0
,

\int_{S}u^{\gamma}(x,y)\,dm(y)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\gamma t}P_{t}(x,S)\,dt=\frac{1}{\gamma}.

(7.25)

Let
\overline{Y}=\{\overline{Y}_{t};t\in R^{1}_{+}\}
be the Markov process
obtained by killing
Y
at the end of an independent exponential time with mean
1/\alpha
. (
\overline{Y}
is an example of
\cal Y
introduced in the beginning of this section.) Let
\overline{u}^{p}=\{\overline{u}^{p},x,y\in S\}
denote the
p
–th potential density of
\overline{Y}
, for
p>0
. Note that
\overline{u}^{p}
is also the potential density of
Y
killed at the end of time
\xi\wedge\rho
, where
\xi
and
\rho
are independent exponential random variables with means
1/\alpha
and
1/p
. It is well known that
\xi\wedge\rho
is an exponential random variable with mean
1/(\alpha+p)
; see e.g.,
[
9
, Remark 5.2.8]
. Consequently,

\overline{u}^{p}(x,y)=u^{\alpha+p}(x,y).

(7.26)

We have

\int_{S}\overline{u}^{p}(x,y)\,dm(y)=\int_{S}u^{\alpha+p}(x,y)\,dm(y)=\frac{1}{\alpha+p}

(7.27)

and since
\mu
is a probability measure

\|f\|_{1}=\int_{S}u^{\alpha+p}(x,y)\,dm(y)\,d\mu(x)=\frac{1}{\alpha+p}.

(7.28)

Using these we can write (
7.2
) as,

w^{p}(x,y)=u^{\alpha+p}(x,y)+\frac{\alpha}{p}f(y).

(7.29)

It follows from Lemma
6.1
that
\{w^{p}(x,y),x,y\in S\}
has the form of the kernels of the permanental processes considered in Theorems
1.2

and
1.5
a. Therefore, by Theorem
5.1
whenever these permanental processes satisfy an LIL as in (
1.22
) or (
1.51
),
(
5.3
) holds for the local times of the fully rebirthed Markov processes
\widetilde{Z}(u^{\alpha+p},m,\mu)
, where the local times
\widetilde{L}=\{\widetilde{L}_{t}^{y},(y,t)\in S\times(0,\infty)\}
are normalized so that

\widetilde{E}^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-ps}\,d_{s}\widetilde{L}^{y}_{s}\right)=w^{p}(x,y).

(7.30)

Theorems
1.2

and
1.5
a. give conditions for
when these permanental processes satisfy the LILs in (
1.22
) or (
1.51
). These conditions also imply that the 1/2 permanental processes with kernels
w^{p}(x,y)
are continuous almost surely and that
\{\widetilde{L}_{s}^{y},s,y\in R^{1}\times R_{+}^{1}\}
are jointly continuous almost surely.
These properties follow from
[
10
, Theorems 1.1 and 1.2]
. (The fact that the hypotheses on
u^{\alpha+p}
and
f
imply the conditions in these Theorems is well known; see e.g.,
[
9
, Chapter 6]
.) Consequently when Theorems
1.2

and
1.5
a. satisfy the LILs in (
1.22
) or (
1.51
) it follows from Corollary
5.3
.
that the local times of the fully rebirthed Markov processes
\widetilde{Z}(u^{\alpha+p},m,\mu)
, normalized so that (
7.30
) holds,
satisfy the LIL in (
5.3
) for almost every
t\in R_{+}
,
\widetilde{P}^{x}
almost surely, for all
x\in S
.

Example 7.2

Let
Y
and
\overline{Y}

be as in Example
7.1
so that, as in (
7.26
), the
p
–th potential density of
\overline{Y}
is

u^{\alpha+p}(x,y)
. However, in that example we take
Y
and
\overline{Y}
to have to state space
R^{1}
. Now, let
\overline{Y}^{\prime}=\{\overline{Y}^{\prime}_{t},t\in R^{1}-\{0\}\}
be a symmetric Borel right process with state space
S=R^{1}-\{0\}
that is obtained by killing
\overline{Y}
the first time it hits
0
. It follows from
[
9
, (4.165)]
that the
p
–th potential density of
\overline{Y}^{\prime}
is

\overline{v}^{p}(x,y)=v^{\alpha+p}(x,y)=u^{\alpha+p}(x,y)-\frac{u^{\alpha+p}(x,0)u^{\alpha+p}(0,y)}{u^{\alpha+p}(0,0)}.

(7.31)

We now use (
7.25
) and (
7.26
) to see that,

\int_{R^{1}}\overline{v}^{p}(x,y)\,dm(y)=\frac{1}{\alpha+p}\left(1-\frac{u^{\alpha+p}(x,0)}{u^{\alpha+p}(0,0)}\right).

(7.32)

Therefore, by (
7.2
), we have,

w^{p}(x,y)=v^{\alpha+p}(x,y)+\frac{1}{\alpha+p}\left(\frac{\alpha}{p}+\frac{u^{\alpha+p}(x,0)}{u^{\alpha+p}(0,0)}\right)\frac{f(y)}{\|f\|_{1}},

(7.33)

where

f(y)=\int_{S}v^{\alpha+p}(x,y)\,d\mu(x).

(7.34)

(It is interesting to note that,

\frac{1}{p(1+p/\alpha)}<\frac{1}{\alpha+p}\left(\frac{\alpha}{p}+\frac{u^{\alpha+p}(x,0)}{u^{\alpha+p}(0,0)}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\alpha+p}\left(\frac{\alpha}{p}+1\right)=\frac{1}{p}.

(7.35)

This, and the fact that
f/\|f\|_{1}
has
L^{1}
norm 1, shows that the excessive functions added to
v^{\alpha+p}
in (
7.33
) are not be too large.)

Let
\overline{Y}^{\prime\prime}=\{\overline{Y}^{\prime\prime}_{t},t\in R^{1}_{+}\}
be the process
obtained by killing
\overline{Y}^{\prime}
at the end of an independent exponential time with mean
1/p
. Consequently,
\overline{Y}^{\prime\prime}
has
0
–potential densities
v^{\alpha+p}(x,y)
. Since
\{v^{\alpha+p}(x,y),x,y\in S\}
is symmetric it follows from Lemma
6.1
that
f
is an excessive function for
\overline{Y}^{\prime\prime}
and from Lemma
7.1
that
g
is an excessive function for
\overline{Y}^{\prime\prime}
. Consequently,
\{\overline{w}^{p}(x,y),x,y\in S\}
has the form of the kernels of the permanental processes considered in Theorem
1.3
.
(
\overline{Y}^{\prime\prime}
is an example of
\cal Y
introduced in the beginning of this section.)

We are now at the same place as in the paragraph containing (
7.30
) but with respect to the kernels of the permanental processes in Theorem
1.3
.
Therefore, by Theorem
5.1
, whenever these permanental processes satisfy an LIL as in (
1.34
) or (
1.51
),
(
5.3
) holds for the local times
\widetilde{L}=\{\widetilde{L}_{t}^{y},(y,t)\in S\times(0,\infty)\}
of the fully rebirthed Markov processes
\widetilde{Z}(v^{\alpha+p},m,\mu)
, where the local times are normalized so that

\widetilde{E}^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-ps}\,d_{s}\widetilde{L}^{y}_{s}\right)=\overline{w}^{p}(x,y).

(7.36)

Now we repeat the paragraph following (
7.30
) with Theorems
1.2
replaced by Theorem
1.3
to see that when they satisfy the LILs in (
1.34
) or (
1.51
) the local times of the fully rebirthed Markov processes
\widetilde{Z}(v,m,\mu)
, normalized so that (
7.36
) holds,
satisfy the LIL in (
5.3
) for almost every
t\in R_{+}
,
\widetilde{P}^{x}
almost surely, for all
x\in S
.

8
Lévy processes that satisfy the hypotheses of Theorems
1.2
–
1.4

In Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
we require that

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}\left(x\right)=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1-\cos\lambda x}{\beta+\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda,\qquad\beta\geq 0,

(8.1)

satisfies various conditions. With some regularity conditions on
\psi
it does satisfy them.
We show in
[
9
, Lemma 7.3.1]

that when
\psi
is regularly varying at infinity with index
1<p\leq 2
,

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(h)\sim C_{p}\frac{1}{|h|\psi(1/h)},\qquad\text{as $h\to 0$ for all $\beta\geq 0$}.

(8.2)

where,

C_{p}=\frac{4}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}s/2}{s^{p}}\,ds.

(8.3)

In (
1.23
) we point out that

\psi(\lambda)=C\lambda^{2}+\psi_{1}(\lambda),\qquad\text{where $\psi_{1}(\lambda)=o(\lambda^{2})$ as $\lambda\to\infty$}.

(8.4)

Clearly, when
C>0
,
\psi(\lambda)
is regularly varying at infinity with index 2 and

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(h)\sim\frac{|h|}{C},\qquad\text{as $h\to 0$ for all $\beta\geq 0$},

(8.5)

as we point out in (
1.24
).

Suppose
C=0
.
We now show that we can obtain functions
\psi_{1}(\lambda)
with nice regularity conditions. We can represent
\psi_{1}(\lambda)
by the equation,

\psi_{1}(\lambda)=2\int_{0}^{\infty}(1-\cos\lambda x)\nu(dx),

(8.6)

where
\nu
is a Lévy measure on
(0,\infty)
, i.e.,

\int_{0}^{\infty}(1\wedge x^{2})\nu(dx)<\infty.

(8.7)

We consider Lévy measures
\nu
that can be represented by the equation,

\nu([u,\infty))=\int_{u}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau(x)}\,dx,

(8.8)

where
\tau
is a regularly varying function at 0 with index
2<p\leq 3
. Note that the integral in (
8.7
) is finite at 0 when
\tau
is a regularly varying function at 0 with index
2<p<3
and in certain cases when
p=3
.

We now write (
8.6
) as

\psi_{1}(\lambda)=4\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}\lambda u/2}{\tau(u)}\,du.

(8.9)

It follows from
[
9
, Lemma 7.4.10]

that when
\tau
is a regularly varying function at 0 with index
2<p<3
,

\psi_{1}(\lambda)\sim\frac{\pi C_{p}}{\lambda\tau(1/\lambda)},\qquad\text{as $\lambda\to\infty$},

(8.10)

where
C_{p}
is given in (
1.30
). This shows that
\psi
can be taken to be asymptotic to any regularly varying function at infinity with index
1<p<2
, and consequently, that for every regularly varying function at zero of index
0<q<1
, say
g_{q}(x)
it follows from (
8.2
) that there is a Lévy process for which the functions

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\sim g_{q}(x),\quad\text{as $x\to 0$ for all $\beta\geq 0$ }.

(8.11)

When
\tau
is a regularly varying function at 0 with index
3
,

\psi_{1}(\lambda)\sim 2\lambda^{2}\int_{0}^{1/\lambda}\frac{u^{2}}{\tau(u)}\,du,\qquad\text{as $\lambda\to\infty$}.

(8.12)

This shows that
\psi_{1}
can also be asymptotic to certain regularly varying functions at infinity with index 2. See Remark
8.3
.
(The integral in (
8.12
) is
[
9
, 7.284]
which contains the typo that the term
\lambda^{2}
is omitted.)

When the integral in (
8.12
) is a slowly varying function as
\lambda\to\infty

we can write,

\psi_{1}(1/h)\sim\frac{2}{h^{2}}\int_{0}^{h}\frac{u^{2}}{\tau(u)}\,du:=\frac{1}{h^{2}L(1/h)},\qquad\text{as $h\to 0$ for all $\beta\geq 0$}.

(8.13)

where

L(1/h)
is a slowly varying function at 0 such that
\lim_{h\to 0}L(1/h)=\infty
. (See e.g.
[
9
, Theorem 14.7.4]
.) Therefore, by (
8.2
),

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(h)\sim C_{2}{h}{L(1/h)},\quad\text{as $h\to 0$ for all $\beta\geq 0$}.

(8.14)

In particular if
\tau(u)=u^{3}(\log 1/u)^{q+1}
, for any
q>0
,

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(h)\sim C_{q}h(\log 1/h)^{q},\qquad\text{as $h\to 0$ for all $\beta\geq 0$},

(8.15)

for constants
C_{q}
depending on
q
.

We now consider specifically, the hypotheses on the functions
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}
in each of the Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
. We consider the conditions on the excessive functions in these theorems in Section
9
.

8.1
Theorem
1.2

The material above shows that there are many examples of the functions
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}
,
0<\beta\leq 1
that are regularly varying at zero and satisfy the hypotheses of this theorem. Furthermore, we see from (
8.11
) and (
8.15
) that (
1.21
) holds for all such functions with
0<\beta<1
and for all
p>1
when
\beta=1
. In addition (
8.15
)
provides examples in which Theorem
1.2
, II holds for functions
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}
,
\beta>0
, for Lévy processes without a Gaussian component, for which
(
1.21
) does not hold. (For example, as pointed out following the statement of Theorem
1.2
, part II applies to all Lévy processes with a Gaussian component. For such processes (
1.21
) does not hold.)

8.2
Theorem
1.3

In this theorem there is a condition that must be satisfied that is not required in Theorem
1.2
, which is that for all
\beta\geq 0
,

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}\in C^{1}([d,d+\delta])
for
d>0.
With an additional regularity condition on
\psi
we can show that this is the case.
We consider Lévy processes
Y
with Lévy exponents
\psi
such that,

\psi\left(\lambda\right)=\int_{1}^{2}\lambda^{s}\,d\mu(s),

(8.16)

where
\mu
is a finite positive measure on
(1,2]
. We refer to these Lévy processes as stable mixtures. (See Remark
8.2
.)

Stable mixtures are a good class of Lévy processes to work with because we can get good estimates for the derivatives of
\psi(\lambda)
.

The next two lemmas follow immediately from the representation of
\psi
in (
8.16
).

Lemma 8.1

Suppose that
\mu
is supported on
[\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}]
where
1<\gamma_{0}\leq\gamma_{1}\leq 2
and set
\mu([\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}])=|\mu|
. Then
\psi(\lambda)
in (
8.16
) satisfies,

\psi(\lambda)\leq\left\{\begin{matrix}|\mu||\lambda|^{\gamma_{0}},&\quad|\lambda|\leq 1\\
\\
|\mu||\lambda|^{\gamma_{1}},&\quad|\lambda|\geq 1\end{matrix}\right.

(8.17)

and,

\psi(\lambda)\geq\left\{\begin{matrix}\mu([\gamma_{0},\gamma_{0}+\epsilon])|\lambda|^{\gamma_{0}+\epsilon},&\quad|\lambda|\leq 1\\
\\
\mu([\gamma_{1}-\epsilon,\gamma_{1}])|\lambda|^{\gamma_{1}-\epsilon},&\quad|\lambda|\geq 1,\end{matrix}\right.

(8.18)

for all
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small. (Without loss of generality we can assume that

\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
are such that for all
\epsilon>0
both
\mu([\gamma_{0},\gamma_{0}+\epsilon])>0
and
\mu([\gamma_{1}-\epsilon,\gamma_{1}])>0
.)

Lemma 8.2

Suppose that
\mu
is supported on
[\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}]
where
1<\gamma_{0}\leq\gamma_{1}\leq 2
. Then
\psi
in (
8.16
) is in
C^{2}\left((0,\infty)\right)
and for all
\lambda>0
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}\,\psi(\lambda)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lambda\psi^{\prime}(\lambda)\leq\gamma_{1}\,\psi(\lambda)

(8.19)

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}(\gamma_{0}-1)\,\psi(\lambda)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}\psi^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)\leq\gamma_{1}(\gamma_{1}-1)\,\psi(\lambda).

Lemma 8.3

When
\psi
is the characteristic exponent of a Lévy process that is a stable mixture, for all
\beta\geq 0
,
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\in C^{2}\left(R^{1}-\{0\}\right)
and,

|(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)^{\prime}|\leq\frac{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)}{|x|},

(8.20)

where
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)^{\prime}:=\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)
.

Differentiating under the integral sign is critical in the proof of this lemma. Because we can not find a suitable reference we include the following lemma:

Lemma 8.4

Let
f(t,x)
be a function on
(0,\infty)\times(a,b)
such that for each
t\in(0,\infty)
,
f_{x}(t,x):={d}f(t,x))/{dx}

is continuous on
(a,b)
. Assume furthermore that
\sup_{x\in(a,b)}|f_{x}(\cdot,x)|\in L^{1}\left((0,\infty),\,d\mu\right)
for some measure
\mu
on
(0,\infty)
. Then

\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{x}(t,x)\,d\mu(t)\text{ is continuous on $(a,b)$, }

(8.21)

and for all
x\in(a,b)
,

\frac{d}{dx}\int_{0}^{\infty}f(t,x)\,d\mu(t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{x}(t,x)\,d\mu(t).

(8.22)

Proof
The statement in (
8.21
) follows from the Dominated Convergence Theorem and the assumption that
f_{x}(t,x)
is continuous for
x\in(a,b)
. For (
8.22
) we note that for any
a<u<v<b
and
t\in(0,\infty)
,

f(t,v)-f(t,u)=\int_{u}^{v}f_{x}(t,x)\,dx.

(8.23)

Since
\|f_{x}(\cdot,x)\|_{1}
is uniformly bounded on
(a,b)
it follows by
Fubini’s Theorem that,

\int_{0}^{\infty}f(t,v)\,d\mu(t)-\int_{0}^{\infty}f(t,u)\,d\mu(t)=\int_{u}^{v}\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{x}(t,x)\,d\mu(t)\,dx.

(8.24)

Therefore,

\frac{1}{\delta}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(f(t,x+\delta)-f(t,x)\right)\,d\mu(t)\right)=\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{x}^{x+\delta}\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{x}(t,x)\,d\mu(t)\,dx.

(8.25)

Taking the limit as
\delta\to 0
and using (
8.21
) gives (
8.22
).

Proof of Lemma
8.3

Since
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)
is symmetric we can restrict our attention to
x>0
. Write
1-\cos\lambda x=2\sin^{2}\lambda x/2
in (
8.1
). Then, by a change of variables, when
x\neq 0
, for all
\beta\geq 0
,

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)=\frac{4}{\pi x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}t/2}{\beta+\psi(t/x)}\,dt,

(8.26)

and,

\displaystyle(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)}{x}+\frac{4}{\pi x}\frac{d}{dx}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin^{2}t/2\frac{1}{\beta+\psi(t/x)}\,dt\right)

(8.27)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-\frac{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)}{x}+\nu_{\beta}(x).

Note that using (
8.19
) we have,

\frac{d}{dx}\frac{1}{\beta+\psi(t/x)}=\frac{1}{x}\frac{(t/x)\psi^{\prime}(t/x)}{(\beta+\psi(t/x))^{2}}\leq\frac{\gamma_{1}}{x(\beta+\psi(t/x))}.

(8.28)

It follows from (
8.28
) and (
8.18
) that for any
x_{0}>0
there exists an
\epsilon>0

such that,

\sup_{|x-x_{0}|\leq\epsilon}\bigg|\frac{d}{dx}\frac{1}{\beta+\psi(t/x)}\bigg|\in L^{1}((0,\infty),\sin^{2}t/2\,dt).

(8.29)

Let
f(t,x)=1/(\beta+\psi(t/x))

and note that
f_{x}(t,x)
is continuous on
(a,b)
for all
a>0
. Therefore it follows from (
8.29
)
and Lemma
8.4
that,

\nu_{\beta}(x)=\frac{4}{\pi x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin^{2}t/2\left(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{1}{\beta+\psi(t/x)}\right)\,dt

(8.30)

and is continuous on
x>0
. Writing
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)^{\prime}
as in (
8.27
) we see
it too is continuous. Therefore,
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}\in C^{1}\left(R^{1}-\{0\}\right)
.

Using (
8.19
) we see that (
8.28
) is positive.
Therefore,

\frac{4}{\pi x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin^{2}t/2\bigg|\frac{d}{dx}\frac{1}{\beta+\psi(t/x)}\bigg|\,dt\leq\frac{4\gamma_{1}}{\pi x^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}t/2}{\beta+\psi(t/x)}\,dt=\frac{\gamma_{1}(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)}{x}.

(8.31)

Together with (
8.27
) and (
8.30
) this gives (
8.20
).

We now show that for all
\beta\geq 0
,
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\in C^{2}\left(R^{1}-\{0\}\right)
.
Using (
8.27
) we see that,

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)^{\prime\prime}=-\frac{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)^{\prime}}{x}+\frac{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)}{x^{2}}+\frac{d}{dx}\frac{4}{\pi x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin^{2}t/2\left(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{1}{\beta+\psi(t/x)}\right)\,dt.

(8.32)

The last term is equal to

\displaystyle-\frac{4}{\pi x^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin^{2}t/2\left(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{1}{\beta+\psi(t/x)}\right)\,dt

(8.33)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{4}{\pi x}\frac{d}{dx}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin^{2}t/2\left(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{1}{\beta+\psi(t/x)}\right)\,dt

The material in the beginning of this proof on differentiating under the integral sign allows us to write the first term in (
8.33
) as,

\displaystyle-\frac{4}{\pi x^{2}}\frac{d}{dx}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin^{2}t/2\left(\frac{1}{\beta+\psi(t/x)}\right)\,dt=-\frac{1}{x^{2}}\frac{d}{dx}\left(x(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=-\frac{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)}{x^{2}}-\frac{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)^{\prime}}{x}

(8.34)

Consequently.

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)^{\prime\prime}=-\frac{2(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)^{\prime}}{x}+\frac{4}{\pi x}\frac{d}{dx}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin^{2}t/2\left(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{1}{\beta+\psi(t/x)}\right)\,dt.

(8.35)

Set

g(t,x)=\frac{d}{dx}\frac{1}{(\beta+\psi(t/x))}=\frac{(t/x^{2})\psi^{\prime}(t/x)}{(\beta+\psi(t/x))^{2}}.

Then

\displaystyle g_{x}(t,x):=\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}\frac{1}{(\beta+\psi(t/x))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2(t/x^{2})\psi^{\prime}(t/x)}{x(\beta+\psi(t/x))^{2}}-\frac{(t/x^{2})^{2}\psi^{\prime\prime}(t/x)}{(\beta+\psi(t/x))^{2}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{2\left((t/x^{2})\psi^{\prime}(t/x)\right)^{2}}{(\beta+\psi(t/x))^{3}}:=I+II+III,

and is continuous on
(a,b)
for all
a>0
.
Using (
8.19
) we see that,

\displaystyle|I|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\gamma_{1}}{x^{2}(\beta+\psi(t/x))}

(8.36)

\displaystyle|II|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{1}(\gamma_{1}-1)}{x^{2}(\beta+\psi(t/x))}

\displaystyle|III|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\gamma^{2}_{1}}{x^{2}(\beta+\psi(t/x))}.

It follows from (
8.18
)
that for any
x_{0}>0
there exists an
\epsilon>0

such that,

\sup_{|x-x_{0}|\leq\epsilon}\big|g_{x}(t,x)\big|\in L^{1}([0,\infty),\sin^{2}\left(t/2\right)\,dt).

(8.37)

It then follows from Lemma
8.4
that the last term in (
8.35
) is continuous in
x>0
. In addition, we show above that
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\in C^{1}\left(R^{1}-\{0\}\right)
. Therefore, by (
8.35
),
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\in C^{2}\left(R^{1}-\{0\}\right)
.

Remark 8.1

Lemmas
8.1
–
8.3
require that
\psi
is the characteristic exponent of a stable mixture. We want to know what functions
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}
we can have when
\psi
has this property. This information is given in
[
9
, Corollary 9.6.5]
which gives the following lemma:

Lemma 8.5

Let
h
be any function that is regularly varying at infinity with positive index or is slowly varying at infinity and increasing. Then, for any
1<\gamma<2
, there exists a Lévy process that is a stable mixture for which,

(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\sim|x|^{\gamma-1}h(\log 1/|x|),\qquad\text{as}\quad x\to 0,\quad\forall\,\beta\geq 0.

(8.38)

If, in addition,

\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{dx}{h(x)}<\infty,

(8.39)

the above statement is also true when
\gamma=2
.

(The statement in
[
9
, (9.175)]
is given only for
(\sigma^{0})^{2}
and includes the statement that it is concave. If we remove this, as we do in Lemma

8.5
, it is easy to see from the material in
[
9
, Section 9.6]
,
that what remains holds for
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}
for all
\beta\geq 0
. Note also that
there is a typo in the statement of
[
9
, Corollary 9.6.5]
. What we have in (
8.39
) is given as the integral from 0 to 1. In the proof of
[
9
, (9.175)]
we state that it follows from
[
9
, (9.178)]
) since
\hat{\rho}(x)\sim 1/h(x)
at infinity. Clearly this gives (
8.39
).)

Remark 8.2

In
[
9
, Lemma 9.6.1]
we define a stable mixture to be a Lévy process with a characteristic exponent
\psi\left(\lambda\right)
that can be written as in (
8.16
) where
\mu
is a finite positive measure on
(1,2]
such that,

\int_{1}^{2}\,\frac{d\mu(s)}{2-s}<\infty.

(8.40)

With this definition
\mu
can not have positive mass at
s=2
. Therefore a Lévy process with this characteristic exponent does not have a Gaussian component. We now refer to such processes as stable mixtures without a Gaussian component.

Remark 8.3

Consider (
8.12
) in which
\tau
is a regularly varying function at 0 with index
3
. Set

\tau(u)=u^{3}g(u),

(8.41)

so that
g(u)
is a slowly varying function at 0. The function
\psi_{1}(\lambda)

is asymptotic to a regularly varying functions at infinity with index 2 if and only if,

\int_{0}^{x}\frac{u^{2}}{\tau(u)}\,du=\int_{0}^{x}\frac{1}{ug(u)}\,du:=h(x),

(8.42)

is a slowly varying function at 0. Therefore, when

\frac{1}{xg(x)}=h^{\prime}(x)

(8.43)

we have,

\psi_{1}(\lambda)\sim 2\lambda^{2}h(1/\lambda),\qquad\text{as $\lambda\to\infty$}.

(8.44)

Since
g(x)
is a slowly varying function at 0, it follows from (
8.43
) that this occurs whenever
h^{\prime}
is a regularly varying function at zero with index
-1
.

Here is a simple criteria for determining when this is the case: Without loss of generality we can describe
h
near 0 by,

h(x)=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{x}\frac{\epsilon(u)}{u}\,du\right),

(8.45)

where
\lim_{x\to 0}\epsilon(x)=0
. Note that,

h^{\prime}(x)=\frac{\epsilon(x)h(x)}{x}.

(8.46)

Therefore when
h
is a slowly varying function at zero,
h^{\prime}
is a regularly varying function at zero with index
-1
if and only if
\epsilon(x)
is a slowly varying function at zero.

9
Excessive functions that satisfy the hypotheses of Theorems
1.2
–
1.5

The innovation in this paper is to consider permanental processes that have kernels that are not symmetric. We construct these kernels by adding excessive functions to symmetric potentials as in (
1.11
).
In Part I of Theorems
1.2
–
1.5

we want to show that for different numbers
d
there are excessive functions
f,g\in C^{1}(\Delta_{d}(\delta))
, for some
\delta>0
. We also want to show that
g(x)\neq Cf(x)
in
\Delta_{d}(\delta)
for any constant
C
. This implies that (
1.11
) is not symmetric on
\Delta_{d}(\delta)
.
In Part II of Theorems
1.2
–
1.5

we want to show that for different numbers
d
there are excessive functions
f,g\in C^{2}(\Delta_{d}(\delta))
for some
\delta>0
such that
f^{\prime}(d)=g^{\prime}(d)=0
. As above we also want to show that
g(x)\neq Cf(x)
in
\Delta_{d}(\delta)
for any constant
C
for any
\delta>0
.

In both of these cases we can simplify the condition that
g(x)\neq Cf(x)
in
\Delta_{d}(\delta)
for any constant
C
for any
\delta>0
to

g(x)\neq f(x)\text{ in $\Delta_{d}(\delta)$ for any $\delta>0$.}

(9.1)

To see this suppose that
f
and
g

are two excessive functions. Then,

\widetilde{f}(x)=f(x)+g(d),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widetilde{g}(x)=g(x)+f(d)

(9.2)

are also excessive functions. Since
\widetilde{f}(d)=\widetilde{g}(d)
the condition
\widetilde{g}(x)\neq C\widetilde{f}(x)
in
\Delta_{d}(\delta)
for any constant
C
follows from (
9.1
).

The many examples of excessive functions that satisfy the criteria of Part I of Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
make it obvious that we can satisfy (
9.1
). The situation is more difficult for Part II of Theorems
1.2
–
1.5
because of the added requirement that
f^{\prime}(d)=g^{\prime}(d)=0
. We concentrate on this case.

9.1
Theorem
1.2
, I

In Theorem
1.2
we consider potential densities of the form

u^{\beta}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\cos\lambda(x-y)}{\beta+\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda,\qquad x,y\in R^{1},\,\,\beta>0;

(9.3)

see (
1.16
). Throughout the paper we take
\psi
even.

Set
u^{\beta}(x,y)=u^{\beta}(x-y)
.
Then when
h\in L^{1}
,

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u^{\beta}(x-y)h(y)\,dy

(9.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{i\lambda x}\frac{\widehat{h}(\lambda)}{\beta+\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda,

where
\widehat{h}
is the Fourier transform of
h
. Clearly
f
is bounded but, more significantly, it follows from this that
f^{(k)}
the
k-
th derivative of
f
is the Fourier transform of

C_{k}\frac{\lambda^{k}\widehat{h}(\lambda)}{\beta+\psi(\lambda)},

(9.5)

where
C_{k}
is a constant depending on
k.
Since
\widehat{h}(0)=\|h\|_{1}
, and we can find examples of
\widehat{h}
with any rate of decay as
\lambda\to\infty
, we can find potentials
f
with any number of derivatives, including
f\in C^{\infty}
. This shows that the condition in Theorem
1.2
, I. that
g
and
f
are
C^{1}(R^{1})
excessive functions for
Y
can be easily satisfied for many functions
g
and
f
.

Example 9.1

Let
h_{a,b}(x)=I_{[a,b]}(x)
,
a<b
and,

f_{a,b}(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u^{\beta}(x-y)h_{a,b}(y)\,dy.

(9.6)

To show that
f_{a,b}\in C^{1}(R^{1})
it suffices to show
f_{-1,1}\in C^{1}(R_{1})
, since the other cases are just translations and rescaling of this one. In this case,

C_{1}\frac{\lambda\widehat{h}(\lambda)}{\beta+\psi(\lambda)}=C^{\prime}_{1}\frac{\sin\lambda}{\beta+\psi(\lambda)},

(9.7)

which is in
L^{1}
. Therefore
f_{-1,1}\in C^{1}(R_{1})
.

However it is easier to show this by a direct calculation, since

f_{a,b}(x)=\int_{a}^{b}u^{\beta}(x-y)\,dy=\int_{x-b}^{x-a}u^{\beta}(y)\,dy.

(9.8)

Therefore,

f^{\prime}_{a,b}(x)=u^{\beta}(x-a)-u^{\beta}(x-b),

(9.9)

which shows that
f_{a,b}\in C^{1}(R_{1})
.

9.2
Theorem
1.2
, II

It follows from (
9.4
) that when
(\lambda\widehat{h}(\lambda))/(\beta+\psi(\lambda))\in L^{1}
,

f^{\prime}(x)=\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{i\lambda x}\frac{\lambda\widehat{h}(\lambda)}{\beta+\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda.

(9.10)

We get the following simple lemma:

Lemma 9.1

When
(\lambda\widehat{h}(\lambda))/(\beta+\psi(\lambda))\in L^{1}
,

\lim_{x\to\infty}f^{\prime}(x)=0.

(9.11)

When
h
is symmetric around
d
, i.e., when
h_{d}(y)=h(d+y)
is an even function,

f^{\prime}(d)=0.

(9.12)

In particular, when
h
is an even function

f^{\prime}(0)=0
.

Proof
The limit at infinity follows because
f^{\prime}
is the Fourier transform of an
L^{1}
function.
Furthermore,

f^{\prime}(0)=\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda\widehat{h}(\lambda)}{\beta+\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda.

(9.13)

When
h(x)
is an even function,
\lambda\widehat{h}(\lambda)
is an odd function so that
f^{\prime}(0)=0
.
Now consider,

f(x+d)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u^{\beta}(x+d-y)h(y)\,dy=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u^{\beta}(x-y)h_{d}(y)\,dy:=f_{d}(x)

(9.14)

Since
h_{d}(y)
is an even function,
f^{\prime}_{d}(0)=0
.
Consequently,
f^{\prime}(d)=0
.

Using (
9.5
) we can find many examples of potential functions
f\in C^{2}(R^{1})
.

To simplify the notation in what follows we substitute
x_{0}
for
d
.

Lemma 9.2

Let
h_{a,b}(x)=I_{[a,b]}(x)
,
a<b
and,

f_{a,b}(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u^{\beta}(x-y)h_{a,b}(y)\,dy.

(9.15)

Then

f^{\prime}_{a,b}(x_{0})=0\,\text{ when }\,x_{0}=\frac{a+b}{2}.

(9.16)

Furthermore, when
u^{\beta}(x)
is the potential density of a Lévy process with a characteristic exponent that is a stable mixture,
f_{a,b}\in C^{2}(R^{1}-\{a,b\})
.

Proof
The statement in (
9.16
) follows from Lemma
9.1
since
h_{a,b}
is symmetric about
x_{0}
. Furthermore, it follows from (
9.9
) that

f_{a,b}^{\prime}(x)=\frac{(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-a)-(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x-b)}{2}.

(9.17)

Therefore the final sentence in the statement of this lemma follows from Lemma
8.3
.

Lemma 9.3

When
u^{\beta}(x,y)
in (
9.3
) is the potential density of a Lévy process with a characteristic exponent
\psi(\lambda)
that is a stable mixture, for any
x_{0}
there exist potential functions
f,g\in C^{2}(\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta))
for some
\delta>0
, such that
f^{\prime}(x_{0})=g^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
and
g(x)\neq Cf(x)
for any constant
C
for
x\in\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta)
, for any
\delta>0
.

Proof
Recall that
u^{\beta}(x,y)
is a function of
x-y
. We often write it as
u^{\beta}(x-y)
. Let
f(x)=f_{a,b}(x)
as given in (
9.15
) with
(a+b)/2=x_{0}
and define,

g(x)=\int u^{\beta}(x-y)h_{\overline{a},\overline{b}}(y)\,dy,

(9.18)

where
\overline{a}<a<b<\overline{b}
and
(\overline{a}+\overline{b})/2=x_{0}
. Using (
9.8
) and (
9.9
) we see that,

g^{\prime}(x)=u^{\beta}(x-\overline{a})-u^{\beta}(x-\overline{b}),

(9.19)

and
g^{\prime}(x_{0})=f^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
.

Let
\widetilde{C}
be the constant for which,

g(x_{0})=\widetilde{C}f(x_{0}).

(9.20)

To prove this lemma it suffices to show that
g(x)\neq\widetilde{C}f(x)
for
x\in\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta)
, for any
\delta>0
.

Consider

l(x)=g(x)-\widetilde{C}f(x).

(9.21)

Since
u^{\beta}
is an even function,

l^{\prime}(x)=\left(u^{\beta}(x-\overline{a})-u^{\beta}(\overline{b}-x)\right)-\widetilde{C}\left(u^{\beta}(x-a)-u^{\beta}(b-x)\right).

(9.22)

Set
c=(b-a)/2
and
a-\overline{a}=\overline{b}-b=q
. It follows that (
9.22
) is,

\displaystyle l^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{\beta}(c+q+x-x_{0})-u^{\beta}(c+q-(x-x_{0}))

(9.23)

\displaystyle\qquad-\widetilde{C}\left(u^{\beta}(c+x-x_{0})-u^{\beta}(c-(x-x_{0}))\right).

Obviously,
l^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
. Note also that,

l^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})=2\left((u^{\beta})^{\prime}(c+q)-\widetilde{C}(u^{\beta})^{\prime}(c)\right).

(9.24)

We now show that there exist numbers

c
and
q
such that
(u^{\beta})^{\prime}(c+q)\neq\widetilde{C}(u^{\beta})^{\prime}(c)
, so that for these
c
and
q
,
|l^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})|\neq 0
. To see that such
c
and
q
exist, note that
\lim_{x\to\infty}u^{\beta}(x)=0
as
x\to\infty
by (
9.11
), which is impossible if
(u^{\beta})^{\prime}(x)
is a constant not equal to 0. One can show that
u^{\beta}(x)=0
, for all
x
sufficiently large, is not possible. Since
|l^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})|\neq 0
we see that

f(x)\neq\widetilde{C}g(x)\text{ in $\Delta_{d}(\delta)$ for any $\delta>0$.}

(9.25)

We continue provide examples of excessive functions
f
and
g
which satisfy the conditions of Theorem
1.3
I and II, Theorem
1.4
I and II and Theorem
1.5
a, b and c. The proof for Theorem
1.3
uses some results from the proof for Theorem
1.4
so we consider Theorem
1.4
first.

9.3
Theorem
1.4
, I

Consider potentials for
u^{(0)}(x,y)
of the form,

f(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u^{(0)}(x,y)h(y)\,dy.

(9.26)

Using obvious trigonometric identities we see that

u^{(0)}(x,y)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{(1-\cos\lambda x)(1-\cos\lambda y)+\sin\lambda x\sin\lambda y}{\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda.

(9.27)

Replace
u^{(0)}(x,y)
in (
9.26
) by the integral in (
9.27
) and consider the resulting double integral.
Let
h_{e}(y)=(h(y)+h(-y))/2
and
h_{o}(y)=(h(y)-h(-y))/2
. When
(|\lambda|\wedge 1)/\psi(\lambda)\in L^{1}
we can interchange the order of integration and get

f(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{(1-\cos\lambda x)\left(\widehat{h}(0)-\widehat{h}_{e}(\lambda)\right)+\sin\lambda x\,\widehat{h}_{o}(\lambda)}{\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda,

(9.28)

since
\widehat{h}_{e}(0)=\widehat{h}(0)
. We write this as,

f(x)=\phi(x)\|h\|_{1}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{(1-\cos\lambda x)\,\widehat{h}_{e}(\lambda)-\sin\lambda x\,\widehat{h}_{o}(\lambda)}{\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda.

(9.29)

Lemma 9.4

Let
u^{(0)}(x,y)
be the potential density of a Lévy process with a characteristic exponent
\psi(\lambda)
that is a stable mixture killed the first time it hits 0. Then for all positive even functions
h\in L^{1}
, or for all positive functions
h\in L^{1}
satisfying

\frac{|\lambda\widehat{h}(\lambda)|}{\psi(\lambda)}\in L^{1}((0,\infty),d\lambda),

(9.30)

the corresponding potential
f\in C^{1}(R^{1}-\{0\})
.

Proof
By Lemma
8.3
, since
2\phi(x)=(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x)
,
\phi^{\prime}(x)
is continuous on
R^{1}-\{0\}
. Also, when
h
is even or when (
9.30
)
holds it follows from Lemma
8.4
that for all
x\in R^{1}-\{0\}
,

f^{\prime}(x)=\phi^{\prime}(x)\|h\|_{1}+\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda\sin\lambda x\,\widehat{h}_{e}(\lambda)+\lambda\cos\lambda x\,\widehat{h}_{o}(\lambda)}{\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda.

(9.31)

Since for all
0<a<b
,

\sup_{x\in(a,b)}\frac{|\lambda\sin\lambda x\,\widehat{h}_{e}(\lambda)|}{\psi(\lambda)}\in L^{1}((0,\infty),d\lambda),

(9.32)

and

\sup_{x\in(0,\infty)}\frac{|\lambda\sin\lambda x\,\widehat{h}_{e}(\lambda)+\lambda\cos\lambda x\,\widehat{h}_{o}(\lambda)|}{\psi(\lambda)}\leq\frac{2|\lambda\widehat{h}(\lambda)|}{\psi(\lambda)},

(9.33)

the integral in (
9.28
) is continuous in
x\in R^{1}-\{0\}
.

Example 9.2

Let
h(x)=h_{a,b}(x)=I_{[a,b]}(x)
,
a<b
in (
9.26
). Then,

\widehat{h}(x)=\frac{e^{i\lambda b}-e^{i\lambda a}}{\lambda}

(9.34)

and

\frac{\lambda|\widehat{h}(\lambda)|}{\psi(\lambda)}\leq\frac{2(\lambda|a-b|\wedge 1)}{\psi(\lambda)}.

(9.35)

Therefore when,

\int_{0}^{\delta}\frac{\lambda}{\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda<\infty,\qquad\text{for some $\delta>0$,}

(9.36)

the corresponding potential
f
in (
9.26
) is in
C^{1}(R^{1}-\{0\})
.

9.4
Theorem
1.4
, II

We begin with an extension of
[
9
, Lemma 9.6.3]
.

Lemma 9.5

When
\psi(\lambda)
is the characteristic exponent of a stable mixture and is not equal to
C\lambda^{2}
for some
C>0
,

(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x)=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1-\cos\lambda x}{\psi(\lambda)}\,d\lambda\in C^{2}\left(R^{1}-\{0\}\right)

(9.37)

and is strictly concave on
[0,\infty)
. In particular
((\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x))^{\prime\prime}<0
for all
x>0
.

Proof
The fact that
(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x)\in C^{2}\left(R^{1}-\{0\}\right)
is given in Lemma
8.3
.
For concavity, refer to the proof of
[
9
, Lemma 9.6.3]
. The proof was written under the assumption that
\psi(\lambda)=o(\lambda^{2})
, as
\lambda\to\infty
. It only needs a minor change to give this lemma. In
[
9
, Lemma 9.6.3]
we introduce the function
g(x)
and show that
g^{\prime\prime}(x)\leq 0
. To prove this lemma we must sharpen this to
g^{\prime\prime}(x)<0
. That is, we need strictly less than in
[
9
, (9.167)]
. We get this by showing that the Schwartz inequality applied in
[
9
, Lemma 9.6.3]
is strict. It follows from the second line of
[
9
, (9.169)]
that for the Schwartz inequality to be an equality we must have,

\frac{s-1}{s}=C^{\prime}(s-1)s,\qquad\forall\,s\in(1,2]

(9.38)

for some constant
C^{\prime}>0
, which is only possible if
\mu(s)
, in
[
9
, Lemma 9.6.3]
, is concentrated at single point. Other parts of the proof of
[
9
, Lemma 9.6.3]
show that this point has to be 2.

Lemma 9.6

Let
u^{(0)}(x,y)
be the potential density of a Lévy process with a characteristic exponent
\psi(\lambda)
that is a stable mixture and
\psi(\lambda)\neq C\lambda^{2}
for any
C>0
. Then for any
x_{0}
there exist excessive functions
\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g}
for
u^{(0)}(x,y)
such that
\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g}\in C^{2}(\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta))
for some
\delta>0
,
\widetilde{f}^{\prime}(x_{0})=\widetilde{g}^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
and
\widetilde{g}(x)\neq C\widetilde{f}(x)
for
x\in\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta)
, for any constant
C
.

Proof
Let
f_{a,b}
be as given in (
9.8
) where
h_{a,b}(x)=I_{[a,b]}(x)/(b-a)
,
0<a<b
. Then,

\displaystyle f_{a,b}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(x)+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\phi(y)}h_{a,b}(y)\,dy-\int_{a}^{b}{\phi(y-x)}h_{a,b}(y)\,dy

(9.39)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(x)+\int_{a}^{b}\frac{\phi(y)}{b-a}\,dy-\int_{a}^{b}\frac{\phi(y-x)}{b-a}\,dy.

Using (
9.9
) we see that,

f_{a,b}^{\prime}(x)=\phi^{\prime}(x)-\frac{\phi(x-a)-\phi(-b+x)}{b-a}:=\phi^{\prime}(x)-m_{-b+x,x-a}.

(9.40)

Note that
m_{-b+x,x-a}
is the slope of the line segment from
(-b+x,\phi(-b+x))
to
(x-a,\phi(x-a))
.

Since
\phi(x)
is an non–negative continuous even function with
\phi(0)=0
and
\phi(x)
increasing and strictly concave for
x>0
,
for any
x_{0}>0
, we can find
0<a<x_{0}<b
such that
the slope of the line segment from
(x_{0}-b,\phi(x_{0}-b))
to
(x_{0}-a,\phi(x_{0}-a))
is equal to
\phi^{\prime}(x_{0})
, i.e.,

\phi^{\prime}(x_{0})=\frac{\phi(x_{0}-a)-\phi(-b+x_{0})}{b-a}=m_{x_{0}-b,x_{0}-a}.

(9.41)

It follows from (
9.40
) that
f^{\prime}_{a,b}(x_{0})=0
.

For the following argument it is useful
to draw the graph of of the line segment between
(x_{0}-b,\phi(x_{0}-b))
to
(x_{0}-a,\phi(x_{0}-a))
. (It is important to note that
x_{0}-b<0,x_{0}-a>0
.)

It is easy to see that there exist
b^{\prime}<b
and
a^{\prime}>a
such that the line segment joining
\left(-b^{\prime}+x_{0},\phi(-b^{\prime}+x_{0})\right)
and
\left(x_{0}-a^{\prime},\phi(x_{0}-a^{\prime})\right)
have the same slope, i.e. that,

m_{x_{0}-b,x_{0}-a}=m_{x_{0}-b^{\prime},x_{0}-a^{\prime}}.

(9.42)

Define
f_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(x)
as in (
9.39
). Clearly

f^{\prime}_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(x_{0})=f^{\prime}_{a,b}(x_{0})

(9.43)

Consequently, as we point out in the paragraph containing (
9.1
), to complete this proof we need only show that
f_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(x)\neq f_{a,b}(x)
in
\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta)
, for all
\delta>0
. We do this by showing that
f^{\prime}_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\left(x\right)<f^{\prime}_{a,b}\left(x\right)
for
x\in\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta)
for some
\delta>0
.
By (
9.40
) this is equivalent to showing that for all
\delta>0
, sufficiently small,

\frac{\phi(x_{0}+\delta-a)-\phi(-b+x_{0}+\delta)}{b-a}<\frac{\phi(x_{0}+\delta-a^{\prime})-\phi(-b^{\prime}+x_{0}+\delta)}{b^{\prime}-a^{\prime}}.

(9.44)

This follows because

\phi^{\prime}(x_{0}-a^{\prime})>\phi^{\prime}(x_{0}-a)\qquad\text{and}\qquad-\phi^{\prime}(-b^{\prime}+x_{0})>-\phi^{\prime}(-b+x_{0}),

(9.45)

since
\phi^{\prime}(x)
is decreasing when
x>0
and increasing when
x<0
, i.e., takes on smaller negative values, and
b^{\prime}-a^{\prime}<b-a
.

It follows from Lemma
9.2
that
f_{a,b}\left(x\right)
and
f_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\left(x\right)
are in
C^{2}(\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta))
.

The case when
u^{(0)}(x,y)
is the potential density of a Lévy process with characteristic exponent
\psi(\lambda)=C\lambda^{2}
, in which case the process is a constant time Brownian motion, in covered in Example
9.4
.

9.5
Theorem
1.3
, I

In this theorem,

v^{\beta}(x,y)=u^{\beta}(x-y)-\frac{u^{\beta}(x)u^{\beta}(y)}{u^{\beta}(0)},

with potentials,

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}v^{\beta}(x,y)h(y)\,dy

(9.46)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u^{\beta}(x-y)h(y)\,dy-\frac{u^{\beta}(x)}{u^{\beta}(0)}f(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(x)-\frac{u^{\beta}(x)}{u^{\beta}(0)}f(0)

where
f
is the potential in (
9.4
).

As we pointed out in Section
9.1
we can find examples of
\widehat{h}
with any rate of decay as
\lambda\to\infty
. Consequently we can find functions
f
with any number of derivatives, including
f\in C^{\infty}
. Note that,

\widetilde{f}^{\prime}(x)=f^{\prime}(x)+\left((\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\right)^{\prime}\frac{f(0)}{2u^{\beta}(0)}.

(9.47)

By Lemma
8.3
,
(\sigma^{\beta})^{2}(x)\in C^{1}\left(R^{1}-\{0\}\right)
. Therefore, when
u^{\beta}(x)
is the potential of a Lévy process with a characteristic exponent that is a stable mixture
\widetilde{f}(x)\in C^{1}(R^{1}-\{0\})
.

9.6
Theorem
1.3
, II

Lemma 9.7

When
v^{\beta}(x,y)
is the potential density of a Lévy process with a characteristic exponent
\psi(\lambda)
that is a stable mixture and
\psi(\lambda)\neq C\lambda^{2}
for any
C>0
, then for any
x_{0}\neq 0
there exist excessive functions
\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g}
for
u^{(0)}(x,y)
such that
\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g}\in C^{2}(\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta))
for some
\delta>0
,
\widetilde{f}^{\prime}(x_{0})=\widetilde{g}^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
and
\widetilde{g}(x)\neq Cf(x)
for
x\in\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta)
, for any constant
C
.

Proof
Let
Z
be a symmetric Lévy process. It follows from
[
9
, Example 4.5.4]
that for any
\beta>0
,
the
\beta
–potential density
u^{(\beta)}(x,y)
of the process obtained by killing
Z
the first time it hits
0
is the same as
v^{\beta}(x,y)
, the
0
–potential density of the process obtained by first killing
Z
at the end of an independent exponential
time with mean
1/\beta
, and then killing it the first time it hits
0
. That is,

u^{(\beta)}(x,y)=v^{\beta}(x,y),\qquad\forall\beta>0.

(9.48)

Consider the resolvent equation for
\{u^{(\beta)}(x,y),\,\beta\geq 0\}
where we substitute
v^{\beta}(x,y)
for
u^{(\beta)}(x,y)
when
\beta>0
:

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u^{(0)}(x,y)h(y)\,dy-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}v^{\beta}(x,y)h(y)\,dy=\beta\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}v^{\beta}(x,y)U^{(0)}h(y)\,dy,

(9.49)

where

U^{(0)}h(z)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u^{(0)}(z,y)h(y)\,dy.

(9.50)

Combining these two equations we see that,

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u^{(0)}(x,y)h(y)\,dy=U^{(0)}h(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}v^{\beta}(x,y)\left(h+\beta U^{(0)}h\right)(y)\,dy.

(9.51)

Let
h_{a,b}(x)=I_{[a,b]}(x)/(b-a)
,
0<a<b
and
h_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(x)=I_{[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]}(x)/(b^{\prime}-a^{\prime})
,
0<a^{\prime}<b^{\prime}
, be the functions in (
9.39
) that determine
f_{a,b}(x)
and
f_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(x)
in Lemma
9.6
. I.e.,

U^{(0)}h_{a,b}(x)=f_{a,b}(x),\quad\mbox{ and }\quad U^{(0)}h_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(x)=f_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(x).

(9.52)

Consider the two potentials,

k_{a,b}(x)=\int v^{\beta}(x,y)\left(h_{a,b}+\beta f_{a,b}\right)(y)\,dy,

(9.53)

and

k_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(x)=\int v^{\beta}(x,y)\left(h_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}+\beta f_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\right)(y)\,dy,

(9.54)

for
v^{\beta}(x,y)
.
By (
9.51
)

k_{a,b}(x)=f_{a,b}(x)\hskip 14.45377pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 14.45377ptk_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(x)=f_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(x).

(9.55)

Following the proof of Lemma
9.6
we see that

k_{a,b}^{\prime}(x_{0})=k_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
and
k_{a,b}^{\prime}(x)-k_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}^{\prime}(x)>0
for
x\in\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta)
for some
\delta>0
.

The rest of the proof follows the end of the proof of Lemma
9.6
.

The case when
v^{\beta}(x,y)
is the
\beta
potential density of a Lévy process with characteristic exponent
\psi(\lambda)=C\lambda^{2}
, in which case the process is a diffusion, in covered in Example
9.3
.

9.7
Theorem
1.5

In Parts a. b. and c. of
Theorem
1.5
we require that the excessive functions have a derivative equal to 0 at
some point in their state space as in Part b. of Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
. This is because
\sigma^{2}(d+t,d)
in (
3.7
) is equal to a constant times
|x|
in each of a. b. and c. Therefore
(i)

of Theorem
3.1
is not satisfied.

Theorem
1.5
is for diffusions. Sophisticated tools exist for studying them. We describe their potentials in much greater generality than those in Part b. of Theorems
1.2
–
1.4
.
We treat each of the three parts of Theorem
1.5
separately.

In the discussion preceding Theorem
1.5
we define
\cal Z
to a diffusion in
R^{1}
which is regular and without traps. In the examples we give
\cal Z
is symmetric with respect to Lebesgue measure and has generator,

L=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dx}b(x)\frac{d}{dx}=\frac{1}{2}b(x)\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}b^{\prime}(x)\frac{d}{dx},

(9.56)

where
b\in C^{1}(R^{1})
and is strictly positive. (This is the most general form of a symmetric generator with continuous coefficients, of a diffusion in
R^{1}
.)

9.7.1
Theorem
1.5
,
a.

We define
Z
to be
\cal Z
killed at the end of an independent exponential time with mean
1/\beta
,
\beta>0
. The potential density for
Z
has the form,

\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}p(x)q(y),&\quad x\leq y\\
q(x)p(y),&\quad y\leq x\end{array}\right.,

(9.57)

where
p,q\in C^{2}(R^{1})
are positive strictly convex functions with the properties that
p
is increasing and

q
is decreasing.

Lemma 9.8

For any
x_{0}\in R^{1}
there exist potentials
\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g}
for
\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)
such that
\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g}\in C^{2}(\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta))
for some
\delta>0
,
\widetilde{f}^{\prime}(x_{0})=\widetilde{g}^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
and
\widetilde{g}(x)\neq Cf(x)
for
x\in\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta)
, for any constant
C
.

Proof
Let
h
be a continuous non–negative integrable function and set,

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)h(y)\,dy

(9.58)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q(x)\int^{x}_{-\infty}p(y)h(y)\,dy+p(x)\int^{\infty}_{x}q(y)h(y)\,dy.

Using the fact that
h
is continuous we have,

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}\left(q(x)\int^{x}_{-\infty}p(y)h(y)\,dy\right)+\frac{d}{dx}\left(p(x)\int^{\infty}_{x}q(y)h(y)\,dy\right)

(9.59)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\prime}(x)\int^{x}_{-\infty}p(y)h(y)\,dy+p^{\prime}(x)\int^{\infty}_{x}q(y)h(y)\,dy.

Consequently,

f^{\prime}(x_{0})=q^{\prime}(x_{0})\int^{x_{0}}_{-\infty}p(y)h(y)\,dy+p^{\prime}(x_{0})\int^{\infty}_{x_{0}}q(y)h(y)\,dy.

(9.60)

Let
h_{1}(y)=1_{\{(-\infty,x_{0}]\}}h(y)
and
h_{2}(y)=1_{\{[x_{0},\infty)\}}h(y)
. Clearly,
f^{\prime}(x_{0})=0

when

\int^{x_{0}}_{-\infty}p(y)h_{1}(y)\,dy=-\frac{p^{\prime}(x_{0})}{q^{\prime}(x_{0})}\int^{\infty}_{x_{0}}q(y)h_{2}(y)\,dy.

(9.61)

Note that
p,q
and
-{p^{\prime}(x_{0})}/{q^{\prime}(x_{0})}
are all strictly positive.
It is obvious that for any positive continuous function
h_{2}(y)
, for which the right–hand side of (
9.61
) is finite, there exist an infinite number of different positive continuous functions
h_{2}(y)
with
h_{1}(x_{0})=h_{2}(x_{0})
, for which (
9.61
) is satisfied.

Let
(h_{1}(y),h_{2}(y))
and
(\widetilde{h}_{1}(y),\widetilde{h}_{2}(y))
be two such pairs and consider,

\displaystyle h(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}_{1}(y)1_{\{(-\infty,x_{0}]\}}+\widetilde{h}_{2}(y)I_{\{(x_{0},\infty\}}\qquad\text{and,}

(9.62)

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}_{1}(y)1_{\{(-\infty,x_{0}]\}}+\widetilde{h}_{2}(y)I_{\{(x_{0},\infty\}}.

Considering the infinite number of way we can choose these functions it is clear that we can choose a
\widetilde{h}(y)
that is not equal to a constant multiple of
h(y)
. It now follows from
Lemma
9.9
below
that the corresponding potentials are not multiples of each other in any neighborhood of
x_{0}
.

Lemma 9.9

When

\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)h_{1}(y)\,dy=C\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)(x,y)h_{2}(y)\,dy,

(9.63)

for some
C
in some interval
I
,
h_{1}(y)=Ch_{2}(y)
on
I
.

This lemma is an immediate corollary of the following theorem:

Theorem 9.1

There is a constant
c_{p,q}>0
such that for any continuous non-negative integrable function
h

\left(L-\beta\right)\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)h(y)\,dy=-c_{p,q}h(x).

(9.64)

Proof of Lemma
9.1

Using (
9.59
) we see that,

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}\left(q^{\prime}(x)\int^{x}_{-\infty}p(y)h(y)\,dy\right)+\frac{d}{dx}\left(p^{\prime}(x)\int^{\infty}_{x}q(y)h(y)\,dy\right)

(9.65)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\prime\prime}(x)\int^{x}_{-\infty}p(y)h(y)\,dy+p^{\prime\prime}(x)\int^{\infty}_{x}q(y)h(y)\,dy

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(q^{\prime}(x)p(x)-q(x)p^{\prime}(x)\right)h(x).

We have,

\left(Lf\right)(x)=\frac{1}{2}b(x)f^{\prime\prime}(x)+\frac{1}{2}b^{\prime}(x)f^{\prime}(x)

(9.66)

and similarly for
\left(Lp\right)(x)
and
\left(Lq\right)(x)
.
Consequently, using (
9.59
) (
9.65
) and (
9.66
) we see that,

\displaystyle(Lf)(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(Lq\right)(x)\int^{x}_{-\infty}p(y)h(y)\,dy+\left(Lp\right)(x)\int^{\infty}_{x}q(y)h(y)\,dy

(9.67)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}b(x)\left(q^{\prime}(x)p(x)-q(x)p^{\prime}(x)\right)h(x).

Note that,

Lq=\beta q\qquad\text{and}\qquad Lp=\beta p.

(9.68)

See the end of Section 16.12 in
[
4
]
, which refers to
[
8
]
for proofs.

Using (
9.67
) and (
9.68
) we can finally write,

\left(L-\beta\right)\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)h(y)\,dy=\frac{1}{2}b(x)\left(q^{\prime}(x)p(x)-q(x)p^{\prime}(x)\right)h(x).

(9.69)

Clearly the Wronskian,

W(x)=({1}/{2})b(x)\left(q^{\prime}(x)p(x)-q(x)p^{\prime}(x)\right),
is negative. We now show that it is a constant. This follows because

\displaystyle W^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(b^{\prime}(x)\left(q^{\prime}(x)p(x)-q(x)p^{\prime}(x)\right)\right)

(9.70)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\left(b(x)\left(q^{\prime\prime}(x)p(x)-q(x)p^{\prime\prime}(x)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(Lq\right)(x)\,p(x)-\,\left(Lp\right)(x)q(x)=0,

by (
9.68
).
We label it
-c_{p,q}
. Using this in (
9.69
) gives (
9.64
).

9.7.2
Theorem
1.5
,
b.

We consider
Z^{\prime}
to be the process with state space
T=(0,\infty)
which is obtained by starting
Z
in
T
and then killing it the first time it hits 0. The potential of
Z^{\prime}
is

\displaystyle\widetilde{v}^{\beta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)-\frac{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,0)\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,y)}{\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(0,0)},\qquad x,y>0,

(9.71)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)-\frac{p(0)}{q(0)}q(x)q(y),\qquad x,y>0.

Lemma 9.10

For any
x_{0}\in R^{1}
there exist potentials
\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g}
for
\widetilde{v}^{\beta}(x,y)
such that
\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g}\in C^{2}(\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta))
for some
\delta>0
,
\widetilde{f}^{\prime}(x_{0})=\widetilde{g}^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
and
\widetilde{g}(x)\neq Cf(x)
for
x\in\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta)
, for any constant
C
.

Proof
The proof is a minor modification of the proof of Lemma
9.8
. We have,

\displaystyle\left(L-\beta\right)\int^{\infty}_{0}\widetilde{v}^{\beta}(x,y)h(y)\,dy

(9.72)

\displaystyle\qquad=\left(L-\beta\right)\int^{\infty}_{0}\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)h(y)\,dy-\frac{p(0)}{q(0)}\left(\left(L-\beta\right)q\right)(x)\int^{\infty}_{0}q(y)h(y)\,dy

\displaystyle\qquad=\left(L-\beta\right)\int^{\infty}_{0}\widetilde{u}^{\beta}(x,y)h(y)\,dy=-c_{p,q}h(x),

where we use (
9.68
) to see that
Lq=\beta q
followed by Lemma
9.1
. Therefore, as we point in Lemma
9.9
, if

\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\widetilde{v}^{\beta}(x,y)h_{1}(y)\,dy=C\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\widetilde{v}^{\beta}(x,y)(x,y)h_{2}(y)\,dy

(9.73)

for some
C
in some interval
I
, then
h_{1}(y)=Ch_{2}(y)
on
I
.

Fix
x_{0}>0
. We now show how to find potentials

f(x)=\int^{\infty}_{0}\widetilde{v}^{\beta}(x,y)h(y)\,dy

(9.74)

for which
f^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
. Using (
9.58
) we have,

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p^{\prime}(x)\int_{x}^{\infty}q(y)h(y)\,dy+q^{\prime}(x)\int_{0}^{x}p(y)h(y)\,dy

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{p(0)}{q(0)}q^{\prime}(x)\int_{0}^{\infty}q(y)h(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(p(x)-\frac{p(0)}{q(0)}q(x)\right)^{\prime}\int_{x}^{\infty}q(y)h(y)\,dy

\displaystyle\qquad+q^{\prime}(x)\int_{0}^{x}\left(p(y)-\frac{p(0)}{q(0)}q(y)\right)h(y)\,dy.

Set
\rho(x)=p(x)-\left({p(0)}/{q(0)}\right)q(x)
and write this as

f^{\prime}(x)=\rho^{\prime}(x)\int_{x}^{\infty}q(y)h(y)\,dy+q^{\prime}(x)\int_{0}^{x}\rho(y)h(y)\,dy.

(9.75)

Since
\rho(0)=0
and
\rho^{\prime}(x)>0
for
x>0
we see that
\rho(y)>0
for all
y>0
. In addition
q^{\prime}(x)<0
. Consequently
f^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
for all positive continuous functions
h
for which,

\int_{0}^{x_{0}}\rho(y)h(y)\,dy=\frac{-\rho^{\prime}(x_{0})}{q^{\prime}(x_{0})}\int_{x_{0}}^{\infty}q(y)h(y)\,dy.

(9.76)

This is similar to the situation in the proof of Lemma
9.8
. Using the argument given there we see that there exist an infinite number of functions satisfying the hypotheses of this lemma.

Example 9.3

In (
1.26
) we show that for
\beta>0
,

u^{\beta}_{C}(x-y):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\cos\lambda(x-y)}{\beta+C\lambda^{2}}\,d\lambda={e^{-({\beta C})^{1/2}\,|x-y|}\over 2(\beta C)^{1/2}},\quad x,y\in R^{1}.

(9.77)

We can write this as in (
9.57
) with,

p(x)={e^{({\beta/C})^{1/2}\,x}\over\sqrt{2}(C\beta)^{1/4}},\qquad\text{and}\qquad q(y)={e^{-({\beta/C})^{1/2}\,y}\over\sqrt{2}(C\beta)^{1/4}}.

(9.78)

We consider
v^{\beta}(x,y)
for these values of
p
and
q
and can use Lemma
9.10
to augment Lemma
9.7
when
\psi(\lambda)\neq C\lambda^{2}
.

Note that,

\tau(d)=\frac{1}{2(\beta C)^{1/2}}\frac{d}{dx}\log\left(e^{2(\beta/C)^{1/2}x}\right)\bigg|_{x=d}=\frac{1}{C}.

(9.79)

Using (
1.27
), or more generally Lemma
4.9
, we see that

(\sigma_{C}^{\beta})^{2}\left(x\right)\sim\tau(d)x,\qquad\text{as $x\downarrow 0$}.

(9.80)

This verifies that the denominators in (
1.22
) and (
1.51
) are the same.

9.7.3
Theorem
1.5
,
c.

In the discussion preceding Theorem
1.5
and in Section
9.7.1
we define the diffusion
\cal Z
in
R^{1}
which has the generator

L=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dx}b(x)\frac{d}{dx},

(9.81)

where
b\in C^{1}(R^{1})
and is strictly positive. Let
s(x)\in C^{2}([0,\infty))
be a positive strictly increasing function with
s(0)=0
and
\lim_{x\to\infty}s(x)=\infty
. Let
b(x)=1/s^{\prime}(x)
, for
x\in[0,\infty)
.

Theorem 9.2

Let
\overline{Z}
be the process with state space
(0,\infty)
that is obtained by starting
\cal Z
in
(0,\infty)
and then killing it the first time it hits
0
. Then
\overline{Z}
has potential density

u(x,y)=2\left(s(x)\wedge s(y)\right),\qquad x,y>0,

(9.82)

with respect to Lebesgue measure.

Proof
It follows from
[
18
, VII. Exercise 3.20]
that
s(x)
is the scale function for the diffusion in
R^{1}
with generator
L
defined in (
9.81
) with
b(x)=1/s^{\prime}(x)
. In addition the speed measure of this diffusion is two times Lebesgue measure. Taking the limit
a\to 0
and
b\to\infty
in
[
18
, VII. Corollary 3.8]
shows that the potential density of
\overline{Z}
, the diffusion in
(0,\infty)
with infinitesimal generator
L
killed the first time it hits
0
, is given by (
9.82
).

Note that for functions
f\in C_{b}^{2}(0,\infty)
,

Lf(s(x))=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dx}\frac{1}{s^{\prime}(x)}\frac{d}{dx}f(s(x))=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dx}f^{\prime}(s(x))=\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(s(x))s^{\prime}(x).

(9.83)

Note that when
s(x)=x
we have
Lf(x))=\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(x)
.

Theorem 9.3

A function

f_{s}\in C^{2}(0,\infty)
is excessive for
\overline{Z}
if and only if

f_{s}(x)=f(s(x))\text{ for some $0\leq f\in C^{2}(0,\infty)$ with $f^{\prime\prime}(x)\leq 0$.}

(9.84)

It is helpful to note that the conditions on
f
imply that it is an increasing positive concave function.

Proof
We first show that a positive function
h
is excessive for
\overline{Z}
if and only if
Lh(x)\leq 0
. Recall that a function
h\geq 0
is excessive for
\overline{Z}
if

\displaystyle E^{x}\left(h(\overline{Z}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle h(x),\forall x,t\in(0,\infty)\quad\text{and}

(9.85)

\displaystyle E^{x}\left(h(\overline{Z}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle h(x)\text{ as $t\downarrow 0$, $\forall$ $x\in(0,\infty)$.}

(9.86)

The condition in (
9.86
) holds for all
h\in C_{b}(0,\infty)
because the paths of
\overline{Z}
are continuous.

It follows from
[
18
, Chapter VII, Proposition 2.2]
that when
h\in C_{b}^{2}(0,\infty)
,

E^{x}\left(h(\overline{Z}_{t})\right)-h(x)=E^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{t}Lh(\overline{Z}_{s})\,ds\right).

(9.87)

Consequently, if
Lh(z)\leq 0
for all
z\in(0,\infty)
then
E^{x}\left(h(\overline{Z}_{t}\right)\leq h(x)
for all
x,t\in(0,\infty)
and, as we just pointed out, (
9.86
) holds.
Conversely, (
9.87
) implies that

\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{E^{x}\left(h(\overline{Z}_{t})\right)-h(x)}{t}=Lh(x),

(9.88)

so that if
h
is excessive for
\overline{Z}
, which implies that (
9.85
) holds, we must have
Lh(x)\leq 0
for all
x
.

Since
s^{\prime}(x)>0
it follows from (
9.83
) that
L\left(f(s(x))\right)\leq 0
, that is,
f(s(x))
is excessive for
\overline{Z}
, if and only if
f^{\prime\prime}\leq 0
. Finally, note that any function
f_{s}\in C_{b}^{2}(0,\infty)
can be written in the form
f_{s}(x)=f(s(x))
where
f_{s}(x)=f(s^{-1}(x))\in C_{b}^{2}(0,\infty)
. This shows that the criteria in this theorem describes all excessive functions of
\overline{Z}
.

In Theorem
1.5
,
c.
we require that
f_{s}^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
for some
x_{0}\in(0,\infty)
. Since
f_{s}^{\prime}(x)=f^{\prime}(s(x))s^{\prime}(x)
and
s^{\prime}(x)>0
, we see that when
f_{s}^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
for some
x_{0}\in(0,\infty)
we must have
f^{\prime}(s(x_{0}))=0
. Consequently, the condition that
f^{\prime\prime}(x)\leq 0
, implies that
f^{\prime}(x)=0
for all
x\geq s(x_{0})
. Let
\widehat{f}
denote the functions
f
with these additional properties. All such functions
\widehat{f}\in C^{2}(0,\infty)
can be written as,

\widehat{f}(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle C-h\left({s(x_{0})-y}\right)&\qquad y\in(0,s(x_{0})]\\
\\
\displaystyle C&y>s(x_{0})\end{array}\right.,

(9.89)

for any constant
C>0
and increasing convex function
h\in C^{2}(0,s(x_{0})]
with
h(0)=h^{\prime}(0)=h^{\prime\prime}(0)=0
, and
h(s({x_{0}}))\leq C
.

Here are specific examples,

\widehat{f}^{(p)}(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle 1-\left(\frac{s(x_{0})-y}{s(x_{0})}\right)^{p}&\qquad y\in(0,s(x_{0})]\\
\\
\displaystyle 1&y>s(x_{0})\end{array}\right.,

(9.90)

for any
p>2
.

Lemma 9.11

For any
x_{0}>0
there exist excessive functions
f_{s}
and
g_{s}
for
\overline{Z}
such that
f_{s}
and
g_{s}\in C^{2}(0,\infty)
,
f_{s}^{\prime}(x_{0})=g_{s}^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
and
g_{s}(x)\neq Cf_{s}(x)
for
x\in\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta)
, for any
\delta>0
and constant
C^{\prime}
.

Proof
By (
9.89
) we can take any two functions
h
, that satisfy the conditions in (
9.89
), that are not equal on
[s(x_{0})-\delta,s(x_{0})]
for any
\delta>0
. To be more specific let
f_{s}(x)=\widehat{f}^{(p)}(s(x))
and
g_{s}(x)=\widehat{f}^{(q)}(s(x))
with
p\neq q>2
. It is easy to see that
g_{s}(x)\neq C^{\prime}f_{s}(x)
for
x\in\Delta_{x_{0}}(\delta)
, for any
\delta>0
and constant
C^{\prime}
.

The next theorem gives a different complete description
of all the excessive functions considered Theorem
9.3
and shows that they are potentials.

Theorem 9.4

Let
0\leq f\in C^{2}(0,\infty)
with
f^{\prime\prime}(x)\leq 0
for all
x\in(0,\infty)
,
and
f^{\prime}(x)=0
for all
x\geq s(x_{0})
for some
x_{0}\in(0,\infty)
. Let
f_{s}(x)=f(s(x))
for all
x\in(0,\infty)
. Then

\displaystyle f_{s}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{s(x_{0})}_{0}2(s(x)\wedge y)\left(-\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(y)\right)\,dy

(9.91)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{x_{0}}_{0}2(s(x)\wedge s(y))\left(-Lf_{s}(y)\right)\,dy.

Proof

It follows from the Riesz decomposition theorem,
[
3
, VI, (2.11), (2.19)]
, that
f
has a unique representation of the form,

f(x)=C_{0}x+\int_{0}^{\infty}(x\wedge y)\,d\mu(y),

(9.92)

for some constant
C_{0}\geq 0
and finite positive measure
\mu
. Since for any finite measure
\nu
,

\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{1}{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}(x\wedge y)\,d\nu(y)=0,

(9.93)

(see, e.g.,
[
13
, (5.36)]
), and
f^{\prime}(x)=0
for all
x\geq s(x_{0})
,
C_{0}=0
.

Clearly,
-f^{\prime\prime}(y)\,dy
is a finite, positive measure with compact support. We now show that
\mu(y)=-f^{\prime\prime}(y)\,dy
.
To see this, note that

\displaystyle-\frac{d}{dx}\int^{\infty}_{0}(x\wedge y)\,\,f^{\prime\prime}(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{d}{dx}\int^{x}_{0}y\,f^{\prime\prime}(y)\,dy-\frac{d}{dx}\left(x\int^{\infty}_{x}\,f^{\prime\prime}(y)\,dy\right)

(9.94)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int^{\infty}_{x}\,f^{\prime\prime}(y)\,dy=f^{\prime}(x).

Consequently,

f(x)=C_{1}x+\int^{\infty}_{0}(x\wedge y)(-f^{\prime\prime}(y))\,dy,

(9.95)

for some constant
C_{1}
.
Using (
9.93
) again and the fact that
f^{\prime}(x)=0
for all
x\geq x_{0}
, we see that
C_{1}=0
. Therefore,

f(x)=\int^{s(x_{0})}_{0}(x\wedge y)\left(-f^{\prime\prime}(y)\right)\,dy.

(9.96)

It follows that

\displaystyle f_{s}(x)=f(s(x))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{s(x_{0})}_{0}(s(x)\wedge y)\left(-f^{\prime\prime}(y)\right)\,dy

(9.97)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{x_{0}}_{0}(s(x)\wedge s(z))\left(-f^{\prime\prime}(s(z))\right)s^{\prime}(z)\,dz.

The first integral is first integral in (
9.91
). The second integral and (
9.83
) gives the second integral in (
9.91
).

Remark 9.1

It follows from (
1.10
) that for any
h\geq 0
the potential function

G(x)=\int^{\infty}_{0}2(s(x)\wedge s(y))h(y)\,dy

(9.98)

is an excessive function for a diffusion with potential density given in (
9.82
). In Theorem
1.5
, c. we also require that
G\in C^{2}(0,\infty)
and
G^{\prime}(x_{0})=0.
We now show that this is the case when
h\in C(0,\infty)
and

h(y)=0
for
y\geq x_{0}
. In this case,

G(x)=\int^{x_{0}}_{0}(s(x)\wedge s(y))h(y)\,dy.

(9.99)

We have,

\displaystyle G^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}\int^{x_{0}}_{0}2(s(x)\wedge s(y))h(y)\,dy

(9.100)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}\int^{x\wedge x_{0}}_{0}2s(y)\,\,h(y)\,dy+\frac{d}{dx}\left(2s(x)\int^{x_{0}}_{x\wedge x_{0}}\,h(y)\,dy\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2s(x)\,\,h(x)1_{\{x\leq x_{0}\}}+2s^{\prime}(x)\int^{x_{0}}_{x\wedge x_{0}}\,h(y)\,dy-2s(x)\,\,h(x)1_{\{x\leq x_{0}\}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2s^{\prime}(x)\int^{x_{0}}_{x\wedge x_{0}}\,h(y)\,dy.

(9.101)

This shows that that
G^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
.
In addition,

\displaystyle G^{\prime\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}2s^{\prime}(x)\int^{x_{0}}_{x\wedge x_{0}}\,h(y)\,dy

(9.102)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2s^{\prime\prime}(x)\int^{x_{0}}_{x\wedge x_{0}}\,h(y)\,dy-2s^{\prime}(x)\,\,h(x)1_{\{x\leq x_{0}\}},

which shows that
G\in C^{2}(0,\infty)
.

Note that
G^{\prime}(x_{0})=G^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})=0
and

G(x_{0})=\int^{x_{0}}_{0}2(s(x_{0})\wedge s(y))h(y)\,dy=\int_{0}^{x_{0}}2s(y)h(y)\,dy.

(9.103)

Example 9.4

Using (
1.42
) we see that when
\psi(\lambda)=C\lambda^{2}
,

(\sigma^{(0)})^{2}(x)=2\phi(x)=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1-\cos\lambda x}{C\lambda^{2}}\,d\lambda=\frac{2}{C}|x|

(9.104)

Using (
1.42
) we see that for
x,y>0
,

u^{(0)}(x,y)=\frac{1}{C}(2x\wedge 2y).

(9.105)

It follows from Theorem
9.3
that the excessive functions for
u^{(0)}(x,y)
are all functions
0\leq f\in C^{2}(0,\infty)
with
f^{\prime\prime}(x)\leq 0
.
